Rabinowitz Fukaya categories and the categorical formal punctured neighborhood of infinity
Sheel Ganatra and Yuan Gao and Sara Venkatesh

Abstract
. This paper constructs and studies the Rabinowitz (wrapped) Fukaya category, a categorical invariant of exact cylindrical Lagrangians in a Liouville manifold whose cohomological morphisms, “Rabinowitz wrapped Floer homology groups” measure the failure of wrapped Floer cohomology to satisfy Poincare duality (and in particular vanish for any pair with at least one compact Lagrangian). Our main result, answering a conjecture of Abouzaid, relates the Rabinowitz and usual wrapped Fukaya category by way of a general construction introduced by Efimov, the categorical formal punctured neighborhood of infinity . As an application, we show how Rabinowitz Fukaya categories can be fit into - and in particular often computed in terms of - mirror symmetry.



1.
Introduction

This goal of this paper is to develop, and relate to existing structures in (wrapped)
Floer theory the
Rabinowitz (wrapped) Fukaya category
, a chain-level categorical
open-string (Lagrangian) analogue of the closed string
Rabinowitz Floer
homology
.

Rabinowitz Floer homology, introduced in
[
CF
]
,
is a Floer-theoretic invariant of a contact-type
H
hypersurface in a
Liouville manifold
X
, generated as a chain-complex by
all
(positive,
negative, constant) characteristics of
H
, and with differential counting
certain Floer-type cylinders in
X
. Our focus here will be on the case

H
is the boundary of a domain completing to
X
, in which case the result
only depends on
X
and will be simply denoted
RFH^{*}(X)
.
The group
RFH^{*}(X)
relates to the
symplectic cohomology

SH^{*}(X)
and its dual theory,
symplectic homology

SH_{*}(X)
by way of a long-exact sequence
[
CFO
]
. Although there are multiple direct definitions of
RFH^{*}(X)
(e.g., using the Morse homology of Rabinowitz’s action functional
[
CF
]
or “V-shaped Hamiltonians”
[
CFO
]
),
following
[
venkatesh
]
one can take
[
CFO
]
’s long-exact sequence as a defining property of

RFH^{*}(X)
: it is the homology group measuring the failure of Poincaré
duality to hold in symplectic cohomology (with respect to a canonical geometrically defined copairing that exists on the theory), i.e., as the homology of the cone of a (chain-level version) of a
canonical connecting map
c:SH_{*}(X)\to SH^{*}(X)
called the
continuation map
. In the hypothetical absence of closed characteristics, this reduces to the usual way in which
H^{*}(\partial_{\infty}X)
is determined by the canonical (chain-level) map from compactly supported cohomology
H^{*}_{c}(X)
to ordinary cohomology
H^{*}(X)
.
On the other hand, for a Liouville manifold
RFH^{*}(X)
vanishes if and only if
SH^{*}(X)
vanishes
[
ritterTQFT
]
, which can be viewed as a measure of the degenerate nature of the map
c
for Liouville manifolds (a fact which does not generalize to non-exact settings in which
SH^{*}(X)
and
RFH^{*}(X)
can be defined

[
ritternegative
,
alberskang
]
).

In the open string setting, one can analogously define the Rabinowitz wrapped Floer homology
RW^{*}(K,L)
of a pair of (exact, non-compact, cylindrical at infinity) Lagrangians
K,L
in a Liouville manifold as the theory measuring the failure of Poincaré duality in the usual wrapped Floer cohomology
HW^{*}(K,L)
, i.e., as the cohomology of the cone of the canonical chain-level map from wrapped Floer homology
HW_{*}(K,L)
to wrapped Floer cohomology
HW^{*}(K,L)
.
(a direct definition of
RW^{*}(K,L)
in the spirit of
[
CF
]
’s definition of
RFH^{*}(X)
was also given in
[
merry
]
; and the analogous long-exact sequence

[
bounya
,
dahinden
]
compares this definition to the way we define these groups).
This theory can be computed as a chain complex by all characteristics between the Legendrian boundaries
\partial_{\infty}K
,
\partial_{\infty}L
. Notably, it vanishes if either
K
or
L
are compact (in which case
HW^{*}(K,L)
coincides with the ordinary unwrapped Floer cohomology
HF^{*}(K,L)
, which is finite dimensional).

Importantly, the theory
RFH^{*}(X)
admits an algebra structure, and its Lagrangian analogue admits composition maps
RW^{*}(K,L)\otimes RW^{*}(L,N)\to RW^{*}(K,N)

[
CO
]
. From the perspective of viewing these “boundary homology” groups as the cohomology of mapping cones of (chain level maps) comparing homology and cohomology, this algebra and composition structure is not completely obvious, and has received a fair bit of recent attention
[
seidel6
,
COcone
,
CHOduality
]
.
As a rather direct analogy, for a manifold-with-boundary
M
one can correspondingly recover the cup product on
H^{*}(\partial M)
from the perspective of viewing
H^{*}(\partial M)=H^{*}(\mathrm{Cone}(C^{*}(M,\partial M)\to C^{*}(M)))
, from (chain-level versions of) the cup product on cohomology of the interior
M
, along with (chain-level versions of) the structure of
H^{*}(M,\partial M)
as a module over
H^{*}(M)
.

There is a natural chain-level
A_{\infty}
structure underlying the above composition maps, which assemble into what we call
the
Rabinowitz (wrapped) Fukaya category

\mathcal{RW}(X);

by construction it comes with a natural functor from the usual wrapped Fukaya category
\mathcal{W}(X)
. A version of this category built using the “popsicle” moduli spaces of
[
abouzaidseidel
]
is implicit in
[
venkatesh
,
seidel6
]
; we spell out a definition of this category in §
4
. An independent construction of a (presumably equivalent) version of
\mathcal{RW}(X)
, using symplectic field theory (SFT) techniques, also appears in
[
legout
]
.

Just as the cohomology of the boundary of a manifold-with-boundary is not completely determined by the cohomology of its bounding manifold, one does not in general expect
RFH^{*}(X)
to be determined by
SH^{*}(X)
: although the (symplectic or ordinary) homology of the interior is determined by (symplectic) cohomology, the connecting map between them is an additional — if somewhat degenerate — piece of information. Somewhat surprisingly then, our main result, answering a conjecture of Abouzaid, says in fact that the category
\mathcal{RW}(X)

is
in fact (in good cases) equivalent to a category determined by
\mathcal{W}(X)
by a canonical formula:

Theorem 1.1

(Theorems
5.4
and
5.6
below)
.

Let
(X,\lambda)
be any Liouville manifold.

(i)

(Theorem
5.4
) There is an
A_{\infty}
functor

(1.1)

\Phi:\mathcal{RW}(X)\to\widehat{\mathcal{W}(X)}_{\infty}

where for an
A_{\infty}
category
\mathcal{C}
,
\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}
denotes its (algebraizable) formal punctured neighborhood of infinity of
\mathcal{C}

[
efimov
]
described below.

(ii)

(Theorem
5.6
) If
X
is Weinstein (or more generally non-degenerate) then
\Phi
is a quasi-equivalence.

Recall that a Liouville manifold is
non-degenerate
in the sense of
[
ganatra
]
if it admits a collection of Lagrangians satisfying Abouzaid’s generation criterion
[
abouzaid1
]
. Every Weinstein manifold is known to be non-degenerate by
[
CDGG
,
GPSstructural
,
ganatra
,
gao
]
.
The
(algebraizable) formal punctured neighborhood of
\infty

\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}
of a (dg or
A_{\infty}
) category
\mathcal{C}
, introduced by Efimov in
[
efimov
]
and reviewed in §
2
, is a purely algebraic construction of a new category from
\mathcal{C}
which whose non-triviality measures in the failure of
\mathcal{C}
to be
proper
. Recall that a category
\mathcal{C}
over
{\mathbf{k}}
is proper if
\hom_{\mathcal{C}}(A,B)\in\mathrm{Perf}({\mathbf{k}})
for all objects
A
and
B
, where, denoting by
\mathrm{Mod}({\mathbf{k}})
the dg category of chain complexes of
{\mathbf{k}}
modules,
\mathrm{Perf}({\mathbf{k}})\subset\mathrm{Mod}({\mathbf{k}})
, the subcategory of perfect
{\mathbf{k}}
-linear chain complexes, consists (if
{\mathbf{k}}
is a field) of chain complexes which have finite total cohomology. The failure of
\hom_{\mathcal{C}}
to always land in
\mathrm{Perf}({\mathbf{k}})\subset\mathrm{Mod}({\mathbf{k}})
can be measured by the induced map to the (dg or
A_{\infty}
) quotient:

(1.2)

\overline{\hom}_{\mathcal{C}}:\mathcal{C}^{op}\times\mathcal{C}\longrightarrow\mathrm{Mod}({\mathbf{k}})\longrightarrow\frac{\mathrm{Mod}({\mathbf{k}})}{\mathrm{Perf}({\mathbf{k}})},

whose non-triviality in turn is equivalent to the non-triviality of the essential image of the associated Yoneda functor
\overline{Y}
:

(1.3)

\begin{split}\mathcal{C}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\overline{Y}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathrm{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{op},\frac{\mathrm{Mod}({\mathbf{k}})}{\mathrm{Perf}({\mathbf{k}})})\\
K&\mapsto\overline{\hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(-,K);\end{split}

where
\mathrm{Fun}(-,-)
denotes a suitable category of (
A_{\infty}
) functors. The category
\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}
is precisely defined to be this essential image
\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}:=im(\overline{Y})
; see §
2
for more details and properties. Note, as reviewed there, that there is a computable formulae for morphism spaces in
\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}
in in terms of certain Hochschild cochain complexes in
\mathcal{C}
.

A few words about the proof of Theorem
1.1
, which appears in §
5
, are in order.
The construction of the functor
\Phi
involves a number of new moduli spaces of discs analogous to the construction of
\mathcal{RW}(X)
, and makes use of the explicit Hochschild cochain models of morphisms in
\widehat{\mathcal{W}(X)}_{\infty}
alluded to above. To reconcile the fact that
\widehat{\mathcal{W}(X)}_{\infty}
only depends on
\mathcal{W}(X)
with the fact that
the continuation map
c:HW_{*}(K,L)\to HW^{*}(K,L)
(whose chain level cone determines
morphisms in
\mathcal{RW}(X)
) appears to be extra information beyond that in

\mathcal{W}(X)
, we observe that
c
can be recovered from the
weak smooth
Calabi-Yau structure
on
\mathcal{W}(X)
. Weak smooth Calabi-Yau structures are a type of duality structure which exists on
wrapped Fukaya categories of non-degenerate Liouville manifolds
[
ganatra
]
,
which also come from extra geometric information beyond
\mathcal{W}(X)
. (This is a way in which the relationship between
\mathcal{W}(X)
and
\mathcal{RW}(X)
is
not
analogous to the relationship between
C^{*}(M)
and
C^{*}(\partial M)
for a manifold-with-boundary
M
: even if
M
is orientable
C^{*}(M)
does not possess sufficient duality to recover
C^{*}(\partial M)
by itself).
These additional geometric operations, coming
from discs with two outputs, constitute an operation
\mathcal{CY}
(called in

loc. cit.
the “non-compact Calabi-Yau morphism”) between
\mathcal{W}(X)
and
its “inverse dualizing bimodule”, which
loc. cit.
showed induces a
Poincaré-duality-type isomorphism in the non-degenerate case. Via a series
of holomorphic curve degenerations, our main work relates the functor
\Phi
we construct
to
\mathcal{CY}
and therefore deduces
\Phi
is an isomorphism if
\mathcal{CY}
is. As a warm-up, we note that the homology level continuation map
c
, which can be thought of as an operation associated to holomorphic curves one input in homology and one output in cohomology, can be thought of dually as coming from holomorphic curves with two outputs in cohomology (via the rule typical in Floer theory that, at least cohomologically, inputs and outputs can be exchanged at the cost of replacing cohomology with homology), which can be recovered from a limiting instance of the operations constituting
\mathcal{CY}
.

In the next few subsections, we detail initial applications that follow
more or less immediately from our main theorem and the existing literature: to situating
Rabinowitz Fukaya categories within mirror symmetry (and hence deducing new
computations of these categories), to computing these categories as quotients
of wrapped and compact Fukaya categories in certain instances, and to the
open-closed string relationship for Rabinowitz Floer homology (which is about
the question of recovering the closed-string Rabinowitz Floer homology from
open-string Rabinowitz and/or wrapped Fukaya categories).

1.1.
Rabinowitz Fukaya categories and mirror symmetry

Homological mirror symmetry (HMS) is well known to extend to (and has often been verified in) the setting of mirror pairs
(X,Y)
where
X
is a Weinstein manifold and
Y
some scheme, in which case it predicts there should be an equivalence
\mathcal{W}(X)=\mathrm{Coh}(Y)
(here
\mathrm{Coh}(Y)
denotes the dg category of coherent complexes, and
=
means “Morita equivalence”).
In Corollary
1.2
below, we show that our Theorem
1.1
and work of Efimov, which gives in many instances a purely algbro-geometric interpretation of the
\widehat{\mathrm{Coh}(Y)}_{\infty}
, allows us to situate the Rabinowitz Fukaya categories
\mathcal{RW}(X)
into HMS in a variety of cases, implying new computations. As reviewed in more detail in §
6.1
, Efimov
[
efimov
]
proves:

(1)

for a non-compact smooth variety
Y
over
{\mathbb{C}}
,
\widehat{\mathrm{Coh}(Y)}_{\infty}
coincides with a certain so-called category of
(algebraizable) perfect complexes on the formal punctured neighborhood at
\infty

\mathrm{Perf}_{alg}(\hat{Y}_{\infty})
, which can be defined by smoothly compactifying
Y
to
\bar{Y}
, and quotienting the perfect complexes along the formal completion
\hat{\bar{Y}}_{D}
of
\bar{Y}
along the compactification divisor
D=\bar{Y}\backslash Y
, by the image of the canonical inclusion functor
\mathrm{Perf}(D)\to\mathrm{Perf}(\hat{\bar{Y}}_{D})
(categorically “puncturing” along
D
).

(2)

If in turn
Y
is separated and finite type, and proper (but not necessarily smooth) over any perfect field, Efimov shows
\widehat{\mathrm{Coh}(Y)}_{\infty}
coincides with (the opposite of) the category of singularities
\mathrm{Sing}(Y)^{op}
.

From this and Theorem
1.1
we deduce:

Corollary 1.2
.

Suppose there is an HMS equivalence
\mathcal{W}(X)\cong\mathrm{Coh}(Y)
for a non-degenerate manifold
X
.
Then, there is also an equivalence

(1.4)

\mathcal{RW}(X)\cong\widehat{\mathrm{Coh}(Y)}_{\infty},

If
Y
is a smooth variety over
{\mathbb{C}}
, then this latter category can be computed as

\mathrm{Perf}_{alg}(\hat{Y}_{\infty})
. If
Y
is proper (separated, finite type)
over a perfect field, then this latter category can be computed as

\mathrm{Sing}(Y)^{op}
.

Recently there have been many computations of wrapped Fukaya categories of Weinstein manifolds, in large part through exploiting recent functoriality and local-to-global properties established for these categories
[
GPSsectorsoc
,
GPSstructural
]
). These computations have in particular lead to verifications of HMS for (on the symplectic side) Weinstein manifolds in a number of instances, see e.g.,
[
gammage-shende
,
lekili-polishchuk-pants
,
hacking-keating
,
lekili-ueda
,
lee
,
aaeko
]
. Corollary
1.2
, along with the general computability of the algebro-geometric categories appearing in Efimov’s result, therefore gives a method of extracting from these instances of HMS a number of new computations of the Rabinowitz Fukaya category
\mathcal{RW}(X)
in instances when the mirror is smooth or proper. We remark that
\mathcal{RW}(X)
on its own is significantly less functorial than
\mathcal{W}(X)
, in parallel with the categorical fact that Efimov’s construction
\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}
is not completely functorial in
\mathcal{C}
, and with the algebro-geometric fact that only
proper maps
of varieties induce maps between their formal punctured neighborhoods of infinity. Hence we do not expect recent functoriality/localization methods for Fukaya categories to be directly applicable to computing
\mathcal{RW}(X)
without first passing through
\mathcal{W}(X)
as an intermediary using our Theorem
1.1
.

Seeing as the mirror of a Weinstein manifold
X
is sometimes a scheme
Y
which is neither smooth nor proper, and sometimes not even a scheme but instead a Landau-Ginzburg model
(Y,w)
(in which case to obtain an HMS equivalence one should replace
\mathrm{Coh}(Y)
by a suitable matrix-factorization category
MF(Y,w)
), we ask:

Question 1.3
.

What is the geometric interpretation of
\widehat{\mathrm{Coh}(Y)}_{\infty}
if
Y
is neither smooth nor proper? And what is the geometric interpretation of
\widehat{MF(Y,w)}_{\infty}
?

An answer to this question would provide homological mirror counterparts to
\mathcal{RW}(X)
in a variety of additional cases.

1.2.
Koszul duality and computations of the Rabinowitz Fukaya category

Generalizing the fact that
\widehat{\mathrm{Coh}(Y)}_{\infty}=\mathrm{Sing}(Y)^{op}=(\mathrm{Coh}(Y)/\mathrm{Perf}(Y))^{op}
for a
proper

Y
(a separated, finite type scheme over a perfect field), Efimov
[
efimov
]
articulated criteria for a general
\mathcal{C}
under which the categorical formal punctured neighborhood
\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}
can be computed as a quotient
\mathcal{C}/\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{prop}}
, of
\mathcal{C}
by its subcategory of
(right) proper
(or pseudoperfect) objects.
1
1

1

Our convention is that
\mathcal{C}
is implicitly pre-triangulated and split-closed, so
\mathcal{C}=\mathrm{Perf}(\mathcal{C})
where
\mathrm{Perf}(-)
denotes perfect modules. In this case, if
\mathcal{C}
is smooth, then
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{prop}}
coincides with the category of
proper modules over
\mathcal{C}
, those modules that are cohomologically finite rank.
For
\mathcal{C}=\mathrm{Coh}(Y)
when
Y
is proper (even if non-smooth), these criteria hold and result in the computed equality
\widehat{\mathrm{Coh}(Y)}_{\infty}=\mathrm{Sing}(Y)^{op}
.

Efimov’s criteria, which amount to a certain Koszul duality holding between
\mathcal{C}
and a subcategory
\mathcal{D}
of its (right) proper objects, is applicable in symplectic geometry, given that such duality phenomena have frequently been observed between the wrapped Fukaya category
\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{W}(X)
and the Fukaya category
\mathcal{D}=\mathcal{F}(X)
of compact exact Lagrangians (mirror to the
properness
of a given
Y
); see e.g.,
[
etgulekili
,
ekholmlekili
,
likoszul
]
. Combining this discussion with Theorem
1.1
we immediately deduce:

Corollary 1.4
.

Suppose Koszul duality holds between the wrapped Fukaya category
\mathcal{W}(X)
and the compact Fukaya category
\mathcal{F}(X)
. Then there is an equivalence
\mathcal{RW}(X)\cong\mathcal{W}(X)/\mathcal{F}(X)
.

We will review the relevant notions in §
6.3
and give some examples of how this can be used as a computational device.

1.3.
The open-closed string relationship

For completeness, we describe some aspects of the open-closed string relationship in the Rabinowitz context. Recall that the wrapped Fukaya category of a non-degenerate Liouville manifold and the usual symplectic cohomology
SH^{*}(X)
are related by the
open-closed isomorphism

[
ganatra
]

(1.5)

\mathcal{OC}:\mathrm{HH}_{*-n}(\mathcal{W}(X))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\to}}SH^{*}(X),

where
\mathrm{HH}_{*}(\mathcal{W})
denotes the
Hochschild homology
of the wrapped Fukaya category (there is also a
closed-open
isomorphism with the Hochschild cohomology which we will not discuss here for brevity; see
loc. cit.
).

One can ask the corresponding question for Rabinowitz theory: how can one recover the Rabinowitz Floer homology
RFH^{*}(X)
categorically?
Note that it does
not
seem to always be the case that there is an isomorphism between
\mathrm{HH}_{*-n}(\mathcal{RW}(X))
and
RFH^{*}(X)
even when (
1.5
) holds, see Remark
1.5
.

Remark 1.5
.

When
X=T^{*}S^{1}
with its usual
{\mathbb{Z}}
-grading,
\mathcal{W}(X)
is Morita equivalent to the ordinary algebra
HW^{0}(T_{q}^{*},T_{q}^{*})=k[t,t^{-1}]
and the open-closed isomorphism gives an equivalence between
SH^{*}(X)
and
\mathrm{HH}_{*-1}(k[t,t^{-1}])
.
Passing to Rabinowitz Floer homology, one can again compute (directly or using Theorem
1.1
) that
RW^{0}(T_{q}^{*},T_{q}^{*})=k((t))\oplus k((t^{-1}))
with nothing in other degrees. Let us argue that there cannot be an isomorphism between
RFH^{*}(T^{*}S^{1})
and
\mathrm{HH}_{*-1}(k((t))\oplus k((t^{-1})))
. By direct computation
RFH^{0}(X)=RW^{0}(T_{q}^{*},T_{q}^{*})=k((t))\oplus k((t^{-1}))
, which is free rank 1 over
RW^{0}(T_{q}^{*},T_{q}^{*})
. In contrast,
HH_{-1}(k((t)))
(the case of
k((t^{-1}))
is analogous) equals the module of Kähler differentials
\Omega^{1}_{k((t))/k}
(
[
loday
]
*Prop 1.1.10, noting that we use a cohomological grading convention), which is not finitely generated over
k((t))
in characteristic zero as the size of a differential basis concides with the transcendence degree of
k((t))
over
k

[
matsumura
]
*Thm. 6.5, which is infinite (see e.g.,
[
maclaneschilling
]
*Lemma 1, compare also
[
shaul
]
*Remark 5.2 again noting the grading convention difference). It follows that
HH_{-1}(RW^{0}(T_{q}^{*},T_{q}^{*}))
cannot be isomorphic to
RFH^{0}(X)
as
RFH^{0}(X)
-modules.

Despite the failure of the analogue of (
1.5
), work-in-preparation of Rezchikov
[
rezchikov
]
, combined with
[
ganatra
]
and an algebraic folk Lemma asserted in
[
efimov
]
implies that
RFH^{*}(X)
, like
\mathcal{RW}(X)
,
can
be directly algebraically recovered from
\mathcal{W}(X)
when (
1.5
) holds:

Theorem 1.6
.

When the open-closed isomorphism holds, there is an isomorphism
\mathrm{HH}_{*-n}(\mathcal{W}(X),\mathcal{RW}(X))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\to}}RFH^{*}(X)
, where for a functor
f:A\to B
,
\mathrm{HH}_{*}(A,B)
denotes the Hochschild homology of
A
with coefficients in
B
(thought of via
f
as a bimodule over
A
).

In principle, the above Theorem could be proved directly geometrically by trying to directly construct and analyze
open-closed maps compatible with the long-exact sequence between
SH_{*}(X)
,

SH^{*}(X)
and
RFH^{*}(X)
. Instead, we appeal to Theorem
1.1
to deduce

\mathrm{HH}_{*-n}(\mathcal{W}(X),\mathcal{RW}(X))\cong\mathrm{HH}_{*-n}(\mathcal{W}(X),\widehat{\mathcal{W}(X)}_{\infty})
, which can be algebraically computed as a cone of a map from
\mathrm{HH}_{*}(\mathcal{W}(X))^{\vee}
to
\mathrm{HH}_{*}(\mathcal{W}(X))
, putting us in a position to appeal to the open-closed isomorphism
[
ganatra
]
and its compatibility with this connecting map (recently verified in
[
rezchikov
]
).

By general properties of Hochschild homology, if
\mathcal{D}
is a quotient of
\mathcal{C}
then
\mathrm{HH}_{*}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})=\mathrm{HH}_{*}(\mathcal{D})
. Hence we conclude that in some instances, the Hochschild homology of the Rabinowitz category computes Rabinowitz Floer homology.

Corollary 1.7
.

When Koszul duality holds as in Corollary
1.4
, then there is an isomorphism
\mathrm{HH}_{*}(\mathcal{RW}(X))\cong RFH^{*}(X)
.

As noted at the start of the introduction, a natural and interesting question
would be to understand the compatibility of Theorem (
1.6
) with
algebraic structures, in a manner often done for (
1.5
) (both in the
Floer-theoretic
[
abouzaid1
,
ganatra
,
rittersmith
,
sheridanfano
,
fooo
,
afooo
,
BEEproduct
]
and formal topological field theoretic
[
costello
,
kontsevichsoibelman
]
contexts). For
instance, we ask

Question 1.8
.

Is there a product on
\mathrm{HH}_{*-n}(\mathcal{W}(X),\mathcal{RW}(X))
(or the
equivalent
\mathrm{HH}_{*}(\mathcal{W}(X),\widehat{\mathcal{W}(X)}_{\infty})
), using the data of a
Calabi-Yau structure on
\mathcal{W}(X)
, which recovers the product on
RFH^{*}(X)
under
the open-closed isomorphism?

Overview of paper

In §
2
, we recall Efimov’s definition of the categorical formal punctured neighborhood of
\infty
and some basic algebraic results about these categories. In §
3
we review geometric preliminaries, and collate all moduli spaces of domains and maps used throughout the paper, and study their degenerations. Using these various moduli spaces, in §
4
we define the category
\mathcal{RW}(X)
and in §
5
we prove Theorem
1.1
(statement (i) appears as Theorem
5.4
and (ii) appears as Theorem
5.6
). We recommend the reader read these latter two sections first, referring back to §
3
as necessary for various moduli spaces. (We have written things in this fashion in order to streamline the discussions in §
4
-
5
).
Finally, in §
6
we establish the applications described above, as well as some examples. To give some geometric intuition for the category
\mathcal{RW}(X)
, we also sketch a direct computation of
\mathcal{RW}(X)
for
X=T^{*}S^{1}
, giving an example of Theorem
1.1
in practice.

2.
Homological algebra of categorical formal punctured neighborhoods of infinity

The goal of this section is to recall Efimov’s definition of the algebraizable categorical formal punctured neighborhood
\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}
of an
A_{\infty}
category
\mathcal{C}
, give computable formula for its chain-level morphism complexes and compositions in terms of certain Hochschild cochain complexes in
\mathcal{C}
, and describe some initial properties of
\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}
. We essentially follow the original reference for this material
[
efimov
]
, with the small caveat that (
loc. cit.
assumes
\mathcal{C}
is a dg category whereas we allow
\mathcal{C}
to be more generally
A_{\infty}
; the constructions generalize straightforwardly with the same definition.

2.1.
Categorical preliminaries

First, we review some notation and definitions concerning chain complexes,

A_{\infty}
categories and their functor, module, and bimodule categories, all of
which are relatively standard (compare
[
seidel_book
,
seidel_ainf
,
ganatra
,
tradler
,
sheridanformulae
]
).
We start
by fixing some base field
{\mathbf{k}}
(
{\mathbf{k}}
can also be a ring such as the integers
with some additional requirements which we indicate in parantheticals) and an abelian group
G
with a non-zero map from
{\mathbb{Z}}
(for our purposes one can take
G
to be
{\mathbb{Z}}
or
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
), over which all the chain complexes we study are implicitly graded.
We will denote by

(2.1)

\mathrm{Ch}_{\mathbf{k}}=\mathrm{Mod}({\mathbf{k}})

the associated category of (cohomologically
G
-graded) dg
{\mathbf{k}}
-modules, i.e.,
cochain complexes of
{\mathbf{k}}
modules (which we require to be cofibrant if
{\mathbf{k}}
is
a ring). Given an element
x\in M\in\mathrm{Ch}_{\mathbf{k}}
, we denote the degree of
x
by

|x|=\deg(x)
. We denote morphism spaces in this category by either

\mathrm{Ch}_{\mathbf{k}}(M,N)
or
\hom_{{\mathbf{k}}}(M,N)
. Inside this category one has the subcategory
of
perfect
chain complexes

(2.2)

\mathrm{Perf}({\mathbf{k}})\subset\mathrm{Mod}({\mathbf{k}}),

which can equivalently be defined as the compact objects in (
2.1
) or
as those objects of (
2.1
) which are (quasi-)isomorphic to a retract of a finite
complex of free finitely generated
{\mathbf{k}}
-modules (i.e., if
{\mathbf{k}}
is a field, those chain complexes
with total finite-dimensional cohomology).

Let
\mathcal{C}
be an
A_{\infty}
category over
{\mathbf{k}}
, which concretely consists of a collection of objects (denoted
\mathrm{ob\ }\mathcal{C}
or simply

\mathcal{C}
), a morphism space in
\mathrm{Mod}({\mathbf{k}})
denoted
\hom_{\mathcal{C}}(X,Y)
or
\mathcal{C}(X,Y)
for every
X,Y\in\mathcal{C}
, and operations

\mu^{d}:\mathcal{C}(X_{d-1},X_{d})\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}\cdots\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}\mathcal{C}(X_{0},X_{1})\to\mathcal{C}(X_{0},X_{d})

of degree
2-d
for every
d+1
tuple
X_{0},\ldots,X_{d}\in\mathcal{C}
and every
d\geq 1
(where
\mu^{1}:\mathcal{C}(X_{0},X_{1})\to\mathcal{C}(X_{0},X_{1})
is the differential on
\mathcal{C}(X,Y)\in\mathrm{Mod}({\mathbf{k}})
) satisfying the
A_{\infty}
equations (c.f.,
[
seidel_book
]
*(1.2)).
For a tuple of objects
(X_{0},\ldots,X_{d})
we use the
shorthand
\mathcal{C}(X_{0},\ldots,X_{d}):=\mathcal{C}(X_{d-1},X_{d})\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}\cdots\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}\mathcal{C}(X_{0},X_{1})
, so
\mu^{d}:\mathcal{C}(X_{0},\ldots,X_{d})\to\mathcal{C}(X_{0},X_{d})[2-d]
.
The opposite category
\mathcal{C}^{op}
has the same collection of objects, while the morphism spaces are

\mathcal{C}^{op}(X_{0},X_{1})=\mathcal{C}(X_{1},X_{0})
, and the
A_{\infty}
-structure maps are

\mu^{d}_{\mathcal{C}^{op}}(x_{d},\cdots,x_{1})=(-1)^{*_{d}}\mu^{d}_{\mathcal{C}}(x_{1},\cdots,x_{d}),

where

(2.3)

*_{d}=|x_{1}|+\cdots+|x_{d}|-d.

For a pair of
A_{\infty}
categories
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{D}
there is an associated
A_{\infty}

category of
A_{\infty}
functors

\mathrm{Fun}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D}),

(called
nu-fun(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})
in
[
seidel_book
]
).
Objects are
A_{\infty}
functors
F:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
, which consist of

•

a map on objects
F:\mathrm{ob\ }\mathcal{C}\to\mathrm{ob\ }\mathcal{D}
and

•

for every
tuple
(X_{0},\ldots,X_{d})\in\mathrm{ob\ }\mathcal{C}^{d+1}
, a map on morphism complexes

F^{d}:\mathcal{C}(X_{0},\ldots,X_{d})\to\mathcal{D}(FX_{0},FX_{d}).

satisfying the
A_{\infty}
functor equations

(2.4)

\begin{split}&\sum_{s\geq 1}\sum_{i_{1}+\cdots+i_{s}=d}\mu_{\mathcal{D}}^{s}(F^{i_{s}}(x_{d},\cdots,x_{i_{1}+\cdots+i_{s-1}+1}),\cdots,F^{i_{1}}(x_{i_{1}},\cdots,x_{1}),)\\
=&\sum_{i,j}(-1)^{*_{i}}F^{d-j+1}(x_{d},\cdots,x_{i+j+1},\mu_{\mathcal{C}}^{j}(x_{i+j},\cdots,x_{i+1}),x_{i},\cdots,x_{1}).\end{split}

The morphism space in
\mathrm{Fun}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})
between two functors
F
and
G
, the cochain complex of
A_{\infty}
natural transformations, is

\mathrm{Fun}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})^{g}(F,G):=\prod_{X_{0},\ldots,X_{d}\in\mathcal{C}}\hom_{{\mathbf{k}}}(\mathcal{C}(X_{0},\ldots,X_{d}),\mathcal{D}(FX_{0},GX_{d})[g-d]),

with differential given by, for
T\in\mathrm{Fun}^{g}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})(F,G)
a morphism of degree
g=|T|
:

(2.5)

\begin{split}\mu^{1}(T)^{k}(x_{k},\ldots,x_{1}):=&\sum_{r,i}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}s_{1},\ldots,s_{r}\geq 0\\
s_{1}+\cdots+s_{r}=k\end{subarray}}(-1)^{\dagger}\mu_{\mathcal{D}}^{r}(G^{s_{r}}(x_{k},\ldots,x_{s_{1}+\cdots+s_{r-1}+1}),\ldots,\\
&G^{s_{i+1}}(x_{s_{1}+\cdots+s_{i+1}},\ldots,x_{s_{1}+\cdots+s_{i}+1}),T^{s_{i}}(x_{s_{1}+\cdots+s_{i}},\ldots,x_{s_{1}+\cdots+s_{i-1}+1}),\\
&F^{s_{i-1}}(x_{s_{1}+\cdots+s_{i-1}},\ldots,x_{s_{1}+\cdots+s_{i-2}+1}),\ldots,F^{s_{1}}(x_{s_{1}},\ldots,x_{1}))\\
&-\sum_{m,n}(-1)^{*_{n}+|T|-1}T^{k-m+1}(x_{k},\ldots,x_{n+m+1},\mu_{\mathcal{C}}^{m}(x_{n+m},\ldots,x_{n+1})x_{n},\ldots,x_{1}),\end{split}

where the signs are

\dagger=(|T|-1)(|x_{1}|+\cdots+|x_{s_{1}+\cdots+s_{i-1}}|-s_{1}-\cdots-s_{i-1})

and
*_{n}
is defined as in (
2.3
).

The higher compositions

\mu^{d}:\mathrm{Fun}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})(F_{0},\ldots,F_{d})\to\mathrm{Fun}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})(F_{0},F_{d})

are given by

(2.6)

\begin{split}\mu^{d}(T_{d},\ldots,T_{1})^{k}(x_{k},\ldots,x_{1})=&\sum_{r,i_{1},\ldots,i_{d}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}s_{1},\ldots,s_{r}\geq 0\\
s_{1}+\cdots+s_{r}=k\end{subarray}}(-1)^{\circ}\mu^{r}_{\mathcal{D}}(F_{d}^{s_{r}}(x_{k},\ldots,x_{k-s_{r}+1}),\ldots,F_{d}^{s_{i_{d}+1}}(\ldots),\\
&T_{d}^{s_{i_{d}}}(\ldots),\ldots,T_{2}^{s_{i_{2}}}(\ldots),F_{1}^{s_{i_{2}-1}}(\ldots),\ldots,F_{1}^{s_{i_{1}+1}}(\ldots),\\
&T_{1}^{s_{i_{1}}}(x_{s_{1}+\cdots+s_{i_{1}}},\ldots,x_{s_{1}+\cdots+s_{i_{1}-1}+1}),F_{0}^{s_{i_{1}-1}}(\ldots),\ldots,F_{0}^{s_{1}}(x_{s_{1}},\ldots,x_{1}))\end{split}

with

(2.7)

\circ=\sum_{p=1}^{d}(|T_{p}|-1)(\sum_{j=1}^{s_{1}+\cdots+s_{i_{p}-1}}(|x_{j}|-1))

and
1\leq i_{1}\leq i_{2}\leq\cdots\leq i_{d}\leq r
.
In the case that
F=G=\mathrm{id}
, the composition
\mu^{2}
defined above recovers the usual formulaic definition of the Yoneda product of Hochschild cohomology cochains.

In the special case that
\mathcal{D}
is a dg category (in the sense that
\mu^{\geq 3}_{\mathcal{D}}=0
), then one can directly see that
\mathrm{Fun}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})
is a dg category and the above formulae
greatly simplify to:

(2.8)

\displaystyle\mu^{1}(T)^{k}(x_{k},\ldots,x_{1})

\displaystyle=\mu^{1}_{\mathcal{D}}(T^{k}(x_{k},\ldots,x_{1}))-\sum_{m,n}(-1)^{*_{n}+|T|-1}T^{k-m+1}(x_{k},\ldots,\mu_{\mathcal{C}}^{m}(x_{n+m},\ldots,x_{n+1}),\ldots,x_{1})

(2.9)

\displaystyle\mu^{2}(S,T)^{k}(x_{k},\ldots,x_{1})

\displaystyle=\sum_{1<\ell<k}\mu^{2}_{\mathcal{D}}(S^{k-\ell}(x_{k},\ldots,x_{\ell+1}),T^{\ell}(x_{\ell},\ldots,x_{1})).

The category of left, resp. right modules is by definition the category of
functors
\mathrm{Fun}(\mathcal{C},\mathrm{Ch}_{\mathbf{k}})
, resp.
\mathrm{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{op},\mathrm{Ch}_{\mathbf{k}})
,
and a
\mathcal{C}-\mathcal{D}
bimodule is an
A_{\infty}
-bilinear functor
\mathcal{C}\times\mathcal{D}^{op}\to\mathrm{Ch}_{{\mathbf{k}}}
(in the sense of
[
lyubashenkomulti
]
).
The first examples of left/right modules are the left/right Yoneda modules, sometimes
simply called the representable left/right modules

\begin{split}Y^{l}_{K}&:=\mathcal{C}(K,-)\\
Y^{r}_{K}&:=\mathcal{C}(-,K)\end{split}

Morphism spaces between and tensor products of Yoneda modules provide some interesting and important examples of bimodules,
for which we would like to provide formulae below.

One starts by recalling that the diagonal bimodule
\mathcal{C}_{\Delta}
is the bimodule with underlying cochain spaces

\mathcal{C}_{\Delta}(K,L)=\mathcal{C}(L,K),

such that the bimodule structure maps
\mu_{\mathcal{C}_{\Delta}}^{k,l}
are given by

(2.10)

\mu_{\mathcal{C}_{\Delta}}^{k,l}(x_{k},\ldots,x_{1},c,x^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,x^{\prime}_{l})=(-1)^{\sum_{j=1}^{l}|x^{\prime}_{j}|-l-1}\mu^{k+1+l}_{\mathcal{C}}(x_{k},\ldots,x_{1},c,x^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,x^{\prime}_{l}).

Definition 2.1
.

Define the linear dual diagonal bimodule

(2.11)

(\mathcal{C})^{\vee}

to be the
A_{\infty}
-bimodule over
\mathcal{C}
such that

(i)

the underlying cochain complex is

(2.12)

(\mathcal{C})^{\vee}(K,L)=\hom_{{\mathbf{k}}}(\mathcal{C}^{-*}(K,L),{\mathbf{k}}),

with the opposite grading.

(ii)

The bimodule structure maps

(2.13)

\begin{split}\mu^{k,l}_{(\mathcal{C})^{\vee}}:&\mathcal{C}(K_{k-1},K_{k})\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathcal{C}(K_{0},K_{1})\otimes(\mathcal{C})^{\vee}(K_{0},L_{0})\\
&\otimes\mathcal{C}(L_{1},L_{0})\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathcal{C}(L_{l},L_{l-1})\to(\mathcal{C})^{\vee}(K_{k},L_{l})\end{split}

are induced from
A_{\infty}
-structure maps of the
A_{\infty}
-category
\mathcal{C}

(2.14)

\begin{split}&\mu^{k,l}_{(\mathcal{C})^{\vee}}(x_{k},\ldots,x_{1},f,x^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,x^{\prime}_{l})(z)\\
=&(-1)^{\sum_{j=1}^{l}\deg(x^{\prime}_{j})-l}f(\mu^{k+l+1}_{\mathcal{C}}(x_{k},\ldots,x_{1},z,x^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,x^{\prime}_{l})).\end{split}

Definition 2.2
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be an
A_{\infty}
-category, and
(K,L)\in\mathrm{ob\ }\mathcal{C}\times\mathrm{ob\ }\mathcal{C}
a pair of objects.
Define an
A_{\infty}

\mathcal{C}^{op}-\mathcal{C}^{op}
-bimodule, or simply a bimodule over
\mathcal{C}^{op}
,

(2.15)

\hom_{{\mathbf{k}}}(Y_{K}^{r},Y_{L}^{r})

named as the space of
{\mathbf{k}}
-linear homomorphisms from the right Yoneda module of
K
over
\mathcal{C}
to that of
L
as follows.

•

For each pair of objects
(K^{\prime},L^{\prime})\in\mathrm{ob\ }\mathcal{C}^{op}\times\mathrm{ob\ }\mathcal{C}^{op}=\mathrm{ob\ }\mathcal{C}\times\mathrm{ob\ }\mathcal{C}
,

(2.16)

\hom_{{\mathbf{k}}}(Y_{K}^{r},Y_{L}^{r})=\hom_{{\mathbf{k}}}(Y_{K}^{r}(K^{\prime}),Y_{L}^{r}(L^{\prime}))=\hom_{{\mathbf{k}}}(\mathcal{C}(K^{\prime},K),\mathcal{C}(L^{\prime},L)).

•

The bimodule structure maps,

(2.17)

\begin{split}\mu^{k,l}_{\hom_{{\mathbf{k}}}(Y_{K}^{r},Y_{L}^{r})}:&\mathcal{C}^{op}(K^{\prime}_{0},\ldots,K^{\prime}_{k})\otimes\hom_{{\mathbf{k}}}(\mathcal{C}(K^{\prime}_{0},K),\mathcal{C}(L^{\prime}_{0},L))\\
&\otimes\mathcal{C}(L^{\prime}_{0},\ldots,L^{\prime}_{l})\to\hom_{{\mathbf{k}}}(\mathcal{C}(K^{\prime}_{k},K),\mathcal{C}(L^{\prime}_{l},L))\end{split}

expressed in
\hom_{\mathcal{C}}
in view that
\hom_{\mathcal{C}^{op}}(K,L)=\hom_{\mathcal{C}}(L,K)
, are defined as

(2.18)

\begin{split}\mu^{k,l}_{\hom_{{\mathbf{k}}}(Y_{K}^{r},Y_{L}^{r})}:&\mathcal{C}(K^{\prime}_{1},K^{\prime}_{0})\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathcal{C}(K^{\prime}_{k},K^{\prime}_{k-1})\otimes\hom_{{\mathbf{k}}}(\mathcal{C}(K^{\prime}_{0},K),\mathcal{C}(L^{\prime}_{0},L)))\\
&\otimes\mathcal{C}(L^{\prime}_{l-1},L^{\prime}_{l})\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathcal{C}(L^{\prime}_{0},L^{\prime}_{1})\to\hom_{{\mathbf{k}}}(\mathcal{C}(K^{\prime}_{k},K),\mathcal{C}(L^{\prime}_{l},L))\end{split}

are determined by
A_{\infty}
-module maps for
Y_{K}^{r}
and
Y_{L}^{r}
(the right Yoneda modules over
\mathcal{C}
are regarded as left
A_{\infty}
-modules over
\mathcal{C}^{op}
),

(2.19)

\begin{split}&\mu^{k,l}_{\hom_{{\mathbf{k}}}(Y_{K}^{r},Y_{L}^{r})}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{k},\phi,y_{l},\ldots,y_{1})(z)\\
=&\begin{cases}0,&k>0,l>0\\
(-1)^{|\phi|+|y_{1}|+\cdots+|y_{l}|+|z|-l-1}\phi(\mu_{Y_{K}^{r}}^{l}(z,y_{l},\ldots,y_{1}))&k=0,l>0\\
(-1)^{|x_{1}|+\cdots+|x_{k}|+|z|-k-1}\mu_{Y_{L}^{r}}^{k}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{k},\phi(z))&k>0,l=0\\
(-1)^{|\phi|+|z|-1}\mu^{0}_{Y_{L}^{r}}(\phi(z))+\phi(\mu^{0}_{Y_{K}^{r}}(z)),&k=l=0\end{cases}\end{split}

for elements
x_{i}\in\mathcal{C}^{op}(K^{\prime}_{i-1},K^{\prime}_{i})=\mathcal{C}(K^{\prime}_{i},K^{\prime}_{i-1})
,

y_{j}\in\mathcal{C}^{op}(L^{\prime}_{j},L^{\prime}_{j-1})=\mathcal{C}(L^{\prime}_{j-1},L^{\prime}_{j})
,

\phi\in\hom_{{\mathbf{k}}}(\mathcal{C}(K^{\prime}_{0},K),\mathcal{C}(L^{\prime}_{0},L)
and
z\in\mathcal{C}(K^{\prime}_{k},K)

Definition 2.3
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be an
A_{\infty}
-category, and
(K,L)\in\mathrm{ob\ }\mathcal{C}\times\mathrm{ob\ }\mathcal{C}
a pair of objects.
Define another bimodule over
\mathcal{C}^{op}

(2.20)

(Y_{K}^{r})^{\vee}\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}Y_{L}^{r}

named as the space of finite-rank
{\mathbf{k}}
-linear homomorphisms from the right Yoneda modules of
K
to that of
L
as follows.

•

For each pair of objects
(K^{\prime},L^{\prime})\in\mathrm{ob\ }\mathcal{C}^{op}\times\mathrm{ob\ }\mathcal{C}^{op}=\mathrm{ob\ }\mathcal{C}\times\mathrm{ob\ }\mathcal{C}
,

(2.21)

((Y_{K}^{r})^{\vee}\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}Y_{L}^{r})(K^{\prime},L^{\prime})=(Y_{K}^{r})^{\vee}(K^{\prime})\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}Y_{L}^{r}(L^{\prime})=(\mathcal{C}(K^{\prime},K))^{\vee}\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}\mathcal{C}(L^{\prime},L).

•

The bimodule structure maps

(2.22)

\begin{split}\mu^{k,l}_{(Y_{K}^{r})^{\vee}\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}Y_{L}^{r}}:\mu^{k,l}_{(Y_{K}^{r})^{\vee}\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}Y_{L}^{r}}:&\mathcal{C}^{op}(K^{\prime}_{0},\ldots,K^{\prime}_{k})\otimes(\mathcal{C}(K^{\prime}_{0},K))^{\vee}\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}\mathcal{C}(L^{\prime}_{0},L)\\
&\otimes\mathcal{C}(L^{\prime}_{0},\ldots,L^{\prime}_{l})\to(\mathcal{C}(K^{\prime}_{k},K))^{\vee}\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}\mathcal{C}(L^{\prime}_{l},L),\end{split}

are defined by

(2.23)

\begin{split}&\mu^{k,l}_{(Y_{K}^{r})^{\vee}\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}Y_{L}^{r}}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{k},f\otimes w,y_{l},\ldots,y_{1})(z)\\
=&\begin{cases}0,&k>0,l>0\\
(-1)^{|f|+|y_{1}|+\cdots+|y_{l}|+|z|-l-1}f(\mu_{Y_{K}^{r}}^{l}(z,y_{l},\ldots,y_{1}))\otimes w&k=0,l>0\\
(-1)^{|x_{1}|+\cdots+|x_{k}|+|z|-k-1}\mu_{Y_{L}^{r}}^{k}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{k},f(z)w)&k>0,l=0\\
(-1)^{|f|+|z|-1}\mu^{0}_{Y_{L}^{r}}(f(z))+\phi(\mu^{0}_{Y_{K}^{r}(z)}),&k=l=0.\end{cases}\end{split}

More generally, for any two right modules
\mathcal{M},\mathcal{N}
,
we can define in a similar way to Definition
2.2
a bimodule

(2.24)

\hom_{{\mathbf{k}}}(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{N}).

While a general version of Definition
2.3
is the tensor product (over
{\mathbf{k}}
) of a left module with a right module,
which is more standard.

Remark 2.4
.

A
\mathcal{C}^{op}-\mathcal{C}^{op}
-bimodule naturally gives rise to a
\mathcal{C}-\mathcal{C}
-bimodule,
except that covariance and contravariance are interchanged.
More generally, a
\mathcal{C}-\mathcal{D}
bimodule
\mathcal{B}
is also a
\mathcal{D}^{op}-\mathcal{C}^{op}
bimodule with the module structure maps reversed:

\mu^{k,l}_{\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}-\mathrm{mod\!-\!}\mathcal{D}}(x_{1},\cdots,x_{k},b,y_{l},\cdots,y_{1})=(-1)^{*}\mu^{l,k}_{\mathcal{B},\mathcal{D}^{op}-\mathrm{mod\!-\!}\mathcal{C}^{op}}(y_{1},\cdots,y_{l},b,x_{k},\cdots,x_{1})

with a sign twist.

Remark 2.5
.

One can define an
A_{\infty}
-quadmodule
\hom_{{\mathbf{k}}}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C})
, as a special case of
A_{\infty}
-
n
-modules, following
[
mau
]
.
For us, the most important structure is that the
A_{\infty}
-quadmodule
\hom_{{\mathbf{k}}}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C})
can be thought of as a ’bimodule-valued bimodule’:

\hom_{{\mathbf{k}}}(\mathcal{C}^{op}_{\Delta},\mathcal{C}^{op}_{\Delta})(K,-,L,-)=\hom_{{\mathbf{k}}}(Y_{K}^{r},Y_{L}^{r}),

similarly for
(\mathcal{C}^{op})^{\vee}\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}\mathcal{C}^{op}_{\Delta}
.
The Hoschschild cohomology cochain spaces

\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{C}^{op},\hom_{{\mathbf{k}}}(\mathcal{C}^{op}_{\Delta},\mathcal{C}^{op}_{\Delta}))

and

\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{C}^{op},(\mathcal{C}^{op})^{\vee}\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}\mathcal{C}^{op}_{\Delta})

carry natural structures of
A_{\infty}
-bimodules over
\mathcal{C}^{op}
, induced from the
A_{\infty}
-quadmodule structures of the above-mentioned quadmodules.

Given a
\mathcal{C}-\mathcal{C}
bimodule
\mathcal{P}
(or simply called a bimodule over
\mathcal{C}
), the
Hochschild cohomology cochain complex
is defined to be

(2.25)

\mathrm{CC}^{k}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{P})=\prod_{X_{0},\ldots,X_{d}\in\mathcal{C}}\hom_{{\mathbf{k}}}(\mathcal{C}(X_{0},\ldots,X_{d}),\mathcal{P}(X_{0},X_{d})[k-d]),

with the differential

(2.26)

\begin{split}\delta(F)^{k}(x_{k},\ldots,x_{1})&=\sum(-1)^{\dagger}\mu^{r,s}_{\mathcal{P}}(x_{k},\ldots,F^{k-r-s}(\ldots),x_{s},\ldots,x_{1})\\
&-\sum(-1)^{*_{i}}F^{k-j+1}(x_{k},\ldots,\mu^{j}_{\mathcal{C}}(\ldots),x_{i},\ldots,x_{1}).\end{split}

where

(2.27)

\dagger=|F|\cdot*_{s}

Observe that the morphism spaces of the functor category
\mathrm{Fun}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})
coincide with the (bar model of the) Hochschild cohomology co-chain complex of
\mathcal{C}
with values in the pulled-back bimodule
\mathcal{D}(F(-),G(-)):=(F,G)^{*}(\mathcal{D}_{\Delta})
,

(2.28)

\mathrm{Fun}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})(F,G)=\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D}(F(-),G(-))).

See for example
[
seidel_book
]
*§(1f) for a discussion.

A module
\mathcal{M}
or bimodule
\mathcal{B}
is
proper
if, when
thought of as a functor (or bilinear functor) to chain complexes, it lands in

perfect chain complexes
; i.e., if
\mathcal{M}(X)
(resp.

\mathcal{B}(X,Y)
) is a perfect chain complex for all
X
(resp.
X,Y
)
A module is
perfect
if it is isomorphic to a summand of a finite complex
of representable modules. A category
\mathcal{C}
is
smooth
if its diagonal
bimodule is a perfect object in the category of bimodules, i.e., if it is a
retract of a finite complex of Yoneda bimodules. A category is
proper
if
its diagonal bimodule is proper or equivalently if
\mathcal{C}(K,L)\in\mathrm{Perf}({\mathbf{k}})
for
all
K,L
.

Lastly, let us also mention a useful result regarding tensor product of bimodules.
Recall that the tensor product of a
(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})
-bimodule
\mathcal{P}
with a
(\mathcal{D},\mathcal{E})
-bimodule
\mathcal{Q}
over
\mathcal{D}
is defined to be the
(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{E})
-bimodule
\mathcal{P}\otimes_{\mathcal{D}}\mathcal{Q}
with underlying cochain space

(2.29)

\mathcal{P}\otimes_{\mathcal{D}}\mathcal{Q}(X,Y)=\bigoplus_{Z_{0},\ldots,Z_{s}}\mathcal{P}(X,Z_{0})\otimes\mathcal{D}(Z_{0},\ldots,Z_{s})\otimes\mathcal{Q}(Z_{s},Y),

the differential
\mu^{0,0}_{\mathcal{P}\otimes_{\mathcal{D}}\mathcal{Q}}
given by the sum of operations
\mu^{0,l}_{\mathcal{P}}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{D}}^{\otimes s-l}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{Q}},\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{P}}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{D}}^{\otimes s-k}\otimes\mu^{k,0}_{\mathcal{Q}}
and
\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{P}}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{D}}^{\otimes s-i-j}\otimes\mu_{\mathcal{D}}^{j}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{D}}^{\otimes i}
,
and the higher structure maps
\mu^{r,s}_{\mathcal{P}\otimes_{\mathcal{D}}\mathcal{Q}}
being, up to signs

\mu^{r,0}_{\mathcal{P}\otimes_{\mathcal{D}}\mathcal{Q}}=\sum_{l}\mu^{r,l}_{\mathcal{P}}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{D}}^{\otimes s-l}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{Q}},

\mu^{0,s}_{\mathcal{P}\otimes_{\mathcal{D}}\mathcal{Q}}=\sum_{k}\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{P}}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{D}}^{\otimes s-k}\otimes\mu^{k,s}_{\mathcal{Q}},

and
\mu^{r,s}_{\mathcal{P}\otimes_{\mathcal{D}}\mathcal{Q}}=0
if both
r,s>0
.
(see for example
[
ganatra
]
for more details about the definitions of tensor products with signs).
Taking the tensor product over
\mathcal{C}
with
\mathcal{C}_{\Delta}
either from the left or from the right induces a quasi-isomorphism of modules and bimodules,
where for bimodules the statement is the following:

Lemma 2.6

(Proposition 2.2,
[
ganatra
]
)
.

For any bimodule
\mathcal{B}
over
\mathcal{C}
,
the collapse map

(2.30)

\mu_{\Delta}:\mathcal{C}_{\Delta}\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}\mathcal{B}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\mathcal{B},

given by the following data:

(2.31)

\mathcal{C}(X_{0},\ldots,X_{k})\otimes\mathcal{C}_{\Delta}(X_{0},Z_{0})\otimes\mathcal{C}(Z_{0},\ldots,Z_{s})\otimes\mathcal{B}(Z_{s},Y_{0})\otimes\mathcal{C}^{op}(Y_{0},\ldots,Y_{l})\to\mathcal{B}(X_{k},Y_{l})

(2.32)

\mu_{\Delta}^{s;k,l}(x_{k},\ldots,x_{1},c,z_{1},\ldots,z_{s},b,y_{1},\ldots,y_{l})=(-1)^{\circ^{s}_{-l}}\mu_{\mathcal{B}}^{k+s+1,l}(x_{k},\ldots,x_{1},c,z_{1},\ldots,z_{s},b,y_{1},\ldots,y_{l})

is a quasi-isomorphism.
Here the sign is

(2.33)

\circ^{s}_{-l}=\sum_{i=1}^{s}|z_{i}|-s+|b|-1+\sum_{j=1}^{l}|y_{j}|-l.

2.2.
Calkin complexes

The dg category of
Calkin complexes
over
{\mathbf{k}}
is by definition the dg
quotient of (co)chain complexes by perfect complexes:

(2.34)

\mathrm{Calk}_{\mathbf{k}}:=\mathrm{Mod}({\mathbf{k}})/\mathrm{Perf}({\mathbf{k}})\cong\mathrm{Mod}({\mathbf{k}})/{\mathbf{k}}.

(note that the quotient by any subcategory — the one object category consisting of
{\mathbf{k}}
in this instance — agrees with the quotient by its split-closed pre-triangulated closure —
\mathrm{Perf}({\mathbf{k}})
in this instance).

By definition of the dg quotient
[
drinfeld
]
, morphism spaces in (
2.34
) can be calculated by:

(2.35)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Calk}_{\mathbf{k}}(M,N)

\displaystyle:=\mathrm{Cone}(\hom_{{\mathbf{k}}}(M,{\mathbf{k}})\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}^{\mathbb{L}}\hom_{{\mathbf{k}}}({\mathbf{k}},N)\to\hom_{{\mathbf{k}}}(M,N))

(2.36)

\displaystyle\cong\mathrm{Cone}(M^{*}\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}N\stackrel{{\scriptstyle ev}}{{\to}}\hom_{{\mathbf{k}}}(M,N))

\displaystyle:=(M^{*}\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}N)[1]\oplus\hom_{{\mathbf{k}}}(M,N),d=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}d&ev\\
0&d\end{array}\right)

The second equality can be realized by either observing that the derived
tensor product is — in this case — the ordinary tensor product. Or more
formulaically, if one uses the reduced bar resolution for the derived
tensor product over
{\mathbf{k}}
, the higher length terms are just empty (as the kernel
of the augmentation
{\mathbf{k}}\to{\mathbf{k}}
is zero).

With respect to the direct sum decomposition (
2.2
), we refer to
the left factor of the direct sum as the “
-
” factor and the right factor as
the “
+
” factor. Note that the
-
factor can be identified with the
sub-dg
{\mathbf{k}}
-module of
\hom_{{\mathbf{k}}}(M,N)
consisting of finite-rank homomorphisms
from
M
to
N
. The
composition of elements in
\mathrm{Calk}_{k}
is given by, for
(\phi\otimes n,\psi)\in\mathrm{Calk}_{k}(M,N)
and
(\phi^{\prime}\otimes p,\psi^{\prime})\in\mathrm{Calk}_{k}(N,P)
by

(2.39)

(\phi\otimes\psi^{\prime}(n)+\psi^{*}(\phi^{\prime})\otimes p,\psi^{\prime}\circ\psi).

In terms of the decomposition (
2.2
), there are three interesting
components of composition: The
++\to+
component is simply the
composition of homomorphisms, and the
-+\to-
and
+-\to-
components are
the (right resp. left) module action of homomorphisms over finitely supported
homomorphisms. The remaining components are zero.

One way to deduce the above
formula for composition is to note that, while in the usual bar complex
formula for localization (c.f.,
[
lyubashenkoquotient
]
) there would be a
\mu^{2}
of length two tensors,
and hence a
--\to-
component of the product, this
\mu^{2}
produces a length 3 tensor, and we are in a quotient complex where the length 3
tensors are zero.

There is a natural quotient functor
j:\mathrm{Mod}({\mathbf{k}})\to\mathrm{Calk}_{\mathbf{k}}
. Essentially by definition, a chain
complex
C^{*}
is perfect if and only if
j(C^{*})=0
,
where
=0
means isomorphic to zero in the cohomology category in this subsection and next.

2.3.
The formal punctured neighborhood of infinity

Following Efimov
[
efimov
]
, we shall define the categorical formal punctured neighborhood at infinity of an arbitrary small

A_{\infty}
category
\mathcal{C}
. The (algebraizable) formal punctured neighborhood of infinity
of an
A_{\infty}
category
\mathcal{C}
can be thought of as the obstruction to
\mathcal{C}
being
proper.
Recall that any
A_{\infty}
category
\mathcal{C}
has a tautological bilinear functor given by the hom pairing

\hom:\mathcal{C}^{op}\times\mathcal{C}\to\mathrm{Mod}({\mathbf{k}}),

and the non-triviality of the composition of this functor with the quotient map
\mathrm{Mod}({\mathbf{k}})\to\mathrm{Calk}_{\mathbf{k}}
,

\overline{\hom}:\mathcal{C}^{op}\times\mathcal{C}\to\mathrm{Mod}({\mathbf{k}})\to\mathrm{Calk}_{{\mathbf{k}}}

measures the failure of
\hom
to take values in perfect complexes. In turn,
the non-triviality of the latter functor can be detected by looking at the
essential image of the induced partial adjoint map

(2.40)

\begin{split}y:\mathcal{C}&\to\mathrm{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{op},\mathrm{Calk}_{{\mathbf{k}}})\\
K&\mapsto\overline{\hom}(-,K)\end{split}

which is the composition of the standard yoneda functor
y:\mathcal{C}\to\mathrm{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{op},\mathrm{Ch}_{{\mathbf{k}}})
with the natural projection
\mathrm{Ch}_{{\mathbf{k}}}\to\mathrm{Calk}_{{\mathbf{k}}}
,
also denoted by
y
by abuse of notation.

Definition 2.7

(
[
efimov
]
)
.

The category of algebrizeable perfect complexes on the formal punctured neighborhood at
\infty
of
\mathcal{C}
, denoted

\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}
is by definition the (Karoubi completed
pre-triangulated closure of the) essential image of
y
in (
2.40
) above.

There is, by construction a functor
y:\mathcal{C}\to\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}
. Immediately by definition, we see that:

Lemma 2.8
.

If
K\in\mathcal{C}
is any right proper object, meaning
\hom(-,K)\in\mathrm{Perf}({\mathbf{k}})
for all
-
, then
y(K)=0
. ∎

Lemma 2.9
.

\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}=0
if and only if
\mathcal{C}
is proper.

Proof.

If
\mathcal{C}
is proper, then every object is right proper so Lemma
2.8
implies
y(\mathcal{C})=0
.
Conversely, if
\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}=0
, then every Yoneda functor
\overline{\hom}(-,X):\mathcal{C}\to\mathrm{Mod}({\mathbf{k}})\to\mathrm{Calk}_{{\mathbf{k}}}
is the zero functor, which means that
\overline{\hom}(X,Y)=j(\hom(X,Y))=0\in\mathrm{Calk}_{{\mathbf{k}}}
for all
Y
, i.e.,
\hom(X,Y)\in\mathrm{Perf}({\mathbf{k}})
for all
X
and
Y
.
∎

The general fact that morphism spaces in the functor category are Hochschild cohomology cochain complexes (
2.28
) suggests that morphism spaces in
\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}
are also Hochschild cohomology cochain complexes of some sort.
It turns out that the coefficients of these Hochschild cohomology cochain complexes have appeared in Definitions
2.2
and
2.3
.

For any
A_{\infty}
category
\mathcal{C}
, the category
\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}
is a dg category, i.e. an
A_{\infty}
category with
\mu^{\geq 3}_{\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}}=0
.
The objects are the same as those of
\mathcal{C}
. Let us compute the morphism spaces explicitly.
For
K,L\in\mathrm{ob\ }\mathcal{C}
and their images in
\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}
as
\bar{y}(K),\bar{y}(L)
, we have

(2.41)

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}(y(K),y(L)):=\mathrm{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{op},\mathrm{Calk}_{{\mathbf{k}}})(Y^{r}_{K},Y^{r}_{L})

(2.42)

\displaystyle=\prod_{X_{0},\ldots,X_{k}\in\mathcal{C}}\hom_{{\mathbf{k}}}(\mathcal{C}^{op}(X_{0},\ldots,X_{k}),\mathrm{Calk}_{{\mathbf{k}}}(\mathcal{C}(X_{0},K),\mathcal{C}(X_{k},L)))

(2.43)

\displaystyle=\hom_{{\mathbf{k}}}(\prod_{X_{0},\ldots,X_{k}\in\mathrm{ob\ }\mathcal{C}^{op}}\mathcal{C}^{op}(X_{0},\ldots,X_{k}),\mathrm{Cone}(\mathcal{C}(X_{0},K)^{*}\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}\mathcal{C}(X_{k},L)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle ev}}{{\to}}\hom_{{\mathbf{k}}}(\mathcal{C}(X_{0},K),\mathcal{C}(X_{k},L)))

(2.44)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Cone}(\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{C}^{op},(Y^{r}_{K})^{*}\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}Y^{r}_{L})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle ev\circ}}{{\to}}\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{C}^{op},\hom_{{\mathbf{k}}}(Y^{r}_{K},Y^{r}_{L})))

\displaystyle=\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{C}^{op},(Y^{r}_{K})^{*}\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}Y^{r}_{L})[1]\oplus\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{C}^{op},\hom_{{\mathbf{k}}}(Y^{r}_{K},Y^{r}_{L})),d=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\delta_{-}&ev\circ\\
\ 0&\delta_{+}\end{array}\right))

where
\delta_{-}
and
\delta_{+}
are the usual Hochschild cohomology differentials.

Now let us compute the differential and the composition.
Following (
2.8
), the differential acts on an element
\phi\in\eqref{cinfmorphism}
, with respect to the decomposition
\phi=\phi_{-}\oplus\phi_{+}
of (
2.44
), by
\delta_{-}(\phi_{-})\oplus(\mathrm{ev}\circ\phi_{-}+\delta_{+}(\phi_{+}))
.
Let us write down the differential and product concretely.
Let
\mu_{\hom_{{\mathbf{k}}}(Y^{r}_{K},Y^{r}_{L})}^{k,l}
denote the
A_{\infty}

\mathcal{W}^{op}
-bimodule structure maps on
\hom_{{\mathbf{k}}}(Y^{r}_{K},Y^{r}_{L})
,
If
\phi_{+}=\{\phi_{+}^{d}\}_{d=0}^{\infty}
, and
\phi_{-}=\{\phi_{-}^{d}\}_{d=0}^{\infty}
, then

(2.47)

\begin{split}&(\delta_{+}(\phi_{+}))^{d}(x_{1},\cdots,x_{d})\\
=&\sum(-1)^{|\phi_{+}|+|x_{1}|+\cdots+|x_{i}|-i}\phi_{+}^{d-j+1}(x_{1},\ldots,\mu_{\mathcal{C}}^{j}(x_{i+1},\ldots,x_{i+j}),\ldots,x_{d})\\
&+\sum(-1)^{(|\phi_{+}|-1)(|x_{1}|+\cdots+|x_{i}|-i)}\mu_{\hom_{{\mathbf{k}}}(Y^{r}_{K},Y^{r}_{L})}^{k-i-j,i}(x_{1},\ldots,\phi_{+}^{j}(x_{i+1},\ldots,x_{i+j}),\ldots,x_{d})\\
=&\sum(-1)^{|\phi_{+}|+|x_{1}|+\cdots+|x_{i}|-i}\phi_{+}^{d-j+1}(x_{1},\ldots,\mu_{\mathcal{C}}^{j}(x_{i+1},\ldots,x_{i+j}),\ldots,x_{d})\\
&+\sum\mu_{\hom_{{\mathbf{k}}}(Y^{r}_{K},Y^{r}_{L})}^{d-j,0}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{d-j},\phi_{+}^{j}(x_{d-j+1},\ldots,x_{d}))\\
&+\sum\mu_{\hom_{{\mathbf{k}}}(Y^{r}_{K},Y^{r}_{L})}^{0,d-j}(\phi_{+}^{j}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{j}),x_{j+1},\ldots,x_{d})\end{split}

and

(2.48)

\begin{split}&(\delta_{-}(\phi_{-}))^{d}(x_{1},\cdots,x_{d})\\
=&\sum(-1)^{|\phi_{-}|-1+|x_{1}|+\cdots+|x_{i}|-i}\phi_{-}^{d-j+1}(x_{1},\ldots,\mu_{\mathcal{C}}^{j}(x_{i+1},\ldots,x_{i+j}),\ldots,x_{d})\\
&+\sum(-1)^{(|\phi_{-}|)(|x_{1}|+\cdots+|x_{i}|-i)}\mu_{(Y^{r}_{K})^{*}\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}Y^{r}_{L}}^{k-i-j,i}(x_{1},\ldots,\phi_{-}^{j}(x_{i+1},\ldots,x_{i+j}),\ldots,x_{d})\\
=&\sum(-1)^{|\phi_{-}|-1+|x_{1}|+\cdots+|x_{i}|-i}\phi_{-}^{d-j+1}(x_{1},\ldots,\mu_{\mathcal{C}}^{j}(x_{i+1},\ldots,x_{i+j}),\ldots,x_{d})\\
&+\sum\mu_{(Y^{r}_{K})^{*}\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}Y^{r}_{L}}^{d-j,0}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{d-j},\phi_{+}^{j}(x_{d-j+1},\ldots,x_{d}))\\
&+\sum\mu_{(Y^{r}_{K})^{*}\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}Y^{r}_{L}}^{0,d-j}(\phi_{+}^{j}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{j}),x_{j+1},\ldots,x_{d})\end{split}

We can also write out the
\mu^{2}
-product explicitly as the composition

(2.49)

\mu^{2}_{\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}}(\phi_{2},\phi_{1})^{d}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{d}):=\sum_{\ell}(-1)^{|\phi_{1}|}\phi_{2}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{\ell})\circ\phi_{1}(x_{\ell+1},\ldots,x_{d})

where composition is in the category
\mathrm{Calk}_{{\mathbf{k}}}
, i.e., is given by
(
2.39
) with respect to the decomposition
(
2.44
).

2.4.
Smoothness and Koszul duality

In case of a smooth
A_{\infty}
-category
\mathcal{C}
, there are some useful results and formulas which will be convenient for computational purposes.
In this subsection, we include three such: the first one is a formula for taking tensor products, the second one is a formula for the categorical formal punctured neighborhood of infinity as a bimodule over the original category, and the third one has to do with understanding the categorical formal punctured neighborhood of infinity in the setting of
Koszul duality
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be an
A_{\infty}
-category,
\mathcal{B}
a bimodule over
\mathcal{C}
, and
\mathcal{N}
a right
\mathcal{C}
-module, or a bimodule over
\mathcal{C}
.
The
{\mathbf{k}}
-linear tensor product

(2.50)

\mathcal{B}\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}\mathcal{N}

is a quadmodule (if
\mathcal{N}
is a bimodule), or a trimodule (if
\mathcal{N}
is a right module).
Consider the following Hochschild cohomology cochain complex

(2.51)

\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{B}\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}\mathcal{N}),

which is either a bimodule or a right module.
Let
\mathcal{P}
be another bimodule over
\mathcal{C}
, or a left module.
An interesting question is: when taking tensor product of the above Hochschild cohomology cochain complex over
\mathcal{C}
with
\mathcal{P}
(with respect to the left action on
\mathcal{P}
if
\mathcal{P}
is bimodule),

\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{B}\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}\mathcal{N})\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}\mathcal{P},

can we bring in
\mathcal{P}
into the coefficients of the Hochschild cohomology cochain complex?

First let us consider the case where
\mathcal{N}
is a bimodule.
Note that the bimodule structure on
\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{M}\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}\mathcal{N})
comes from the quadmodule structure on
\mathcal{B}\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}\mathcal{N}
,
such that the right action on
\mathcal{B}
and the left action on
\mathcal{N}
survive after taking Hochschild cochains.
Therefore, taking the tensor product with
\mathcal{P}
over
\mathcal{C}
induces a canonical map

(2.52)

\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{B}\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}\mathcal{N})\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}\mathcal{P}\to\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{C},(\mathcal{B}\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}\mathcal{P})\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}\mathcal{N}).

Now suppose
\mathcal{N}
is a right module.
Then the Hochschild complex (
2.51
) is a right module with the right action induced by that on
\mathcal{B}
.
For the same reasoning, we again have the map (
2.52
) and a variant of the arguments in
[
ganatra
]
*Lemma 2.1, Proposition 2.16 shows:

Proposition 2.10
.

Suppose
\mathcal{C}
is smooth. Then the map (
2.52
) is a quasi-isomorphism, in all circumstances listed above.∎

For the second result, let us recall that for a bimodule
\mathcal{P}
over
\mathcal{C}
, its
bimodule dual
is defined to be

(2.53)

\mathcal{P}^{!}:=\hom_{\mathcal{C}-\mathcal{C}}(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{C}_{\Delta}\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}\mathcal{C}_{\Delta}).

In particular, for the diagonal bimodule, its bimodule dual is called the
inverse dualizing bimodule
of
\mathcal{C}
,

(2.54)

\mathcal{C}^{!}:=\hom_{\mathcal{C}-\mathcal{C}}(\mathcal{C}_{\Delta},\mathcal{C}_{\Delta}\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}\mathcal{C}_{\Delta}).

Using a canonical quasi-equivalence from the ordinary Hochschild cohomology cochain complex to the two-pointed Hochschild cohomology cochain complex as in
[
ganatra
]
, we obtain a bimodule quasi-isomorphism

(2.55)

\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}_{\Delta}\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}\mathcal{C}_{\Delta})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\hom_{\mathcal{C}-\mathcal{C}}(\mathcal{C}_{\Delta},\mathcal{C}_{\Delta}\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}\mathcal{C}_{\Delta})=\mathcal{C}^{!}.

By definition, for a smooth
A_{\infty}
-category
\mathcal{C}
, there is a canonical bimodule map given as the following composition

(2.56)

\mathcal{C}^{!}\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}\mathcal{C}^{\vee}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C}^{\vee}\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}\mathcal{C}_{\Delta})\to\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{C},\hom_{{\mathbf{k}}}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C})).

By choosing an inverse of the canonical Yoneda quasi-isomorphism

\mathcal{C}_{\Delta}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{C},\hom_{{\mathbf{k}}}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C})),

we get the following bimodule map

(2.57)

\mathcal{C}^{!}\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}\mathcal{C}^{\vee}\to\mathcal{C}_{\Delta}

The following proposition relates this map with the formal punctured neighborhood of infinity,
\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}
.

Proposition 2.11
.

Suppose
\mathcal{C}
is smooth. Then, we have a natural quasi-isomorphism of
\mathcal{C}^{op}
-bimodules:

(2.58)

\mathrm{Cone}(\mathcal{C}_{op}^{!}\otimes_{\mathcal{C}^{op}}(\mathcal{C}^{op})^{\vee}\to\mathcal{C}_{\Delta}^{op})\to\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}.

Proof.

The formula (
2.44
) for morphisms in
\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}
implies that
\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}
as a bimodule over
\mathcal{C}^{op}
can be written as

(2.59)

\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}=\mathrm{Cone}(\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{C}^{op},(\mathcal{C}^{op})^{\vee}\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}\mathcal{C}^{op}_{\Delta})\to\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{C}^{op},\hom_{{\mathbf{k}}}(\mathcal{C}^{op}_{\Delta},\mathcal{C}^{op}_{\Delta}))).

By Yoneda lemma, we have a natural quasi-isomorphism of bimodules

\mathcal{C}^{op}_{\Delta}\to\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{C}^{op},\hom_{{\mathbf{k}}}(\mathcal{C}^{op}_{\Delta},\mathcal{C}^{op}_{\Delta})).

By (
2.55
), we have a quasi-isomorphism

\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{C}^{op},\mathcal{C}^{op}_{\Delta}\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}\mathcal{C}^{op}_{\Delta})\otimes_{\mathcal{C}^{op}}(\mathcal{C}^{op})^{\vee}\to\mathcal{C}_{op}^{!}\otimes_{\mathcal{C}^{op}}(\mathcal{C}^{op})^{\vee}.

By Proposition
2.10
, smoothness of
\mathcal{C}
implies that there is also a quasi-isomorphism

\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{C}^{op},\mathcal{C}^{op}_{\Delta}\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}\mathcal{C}^{op}_{\Delta})\otimes_{\mathcal{C}^{op}}(\mathcal{C}^{op})^{\vee}\to\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{C}^{op},(\mathcal{C}^{op}_{\Delta}\otimes_{\mathcal{C}^{op}}(\mathcal{C}^{op})^{\vee})\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}\mathcal{C}^{op}_{\Delta})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{C}^{op},(\mathcal{C}^{op})^{\vee}\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}\mathcal{C}^{op}_{\Delta}).

The proposition follows by inverting the quasi-isomorphism.
∎

The third result about smoothness is related to Koszul duality.
To state it, we need to recall a few more definitions.
An object
K
in
\mathcal{C}
is
(right, resp. left) proper
if its (right, resp. left) Yoneda module is a proper module,
meaning that hom into (respectively from)
K
is a perfect complex for any other object.
Denote by
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{prop}}
the subcategory of
\mathcal{C}
consisting of its right proper objects.
Another notation,
\mathrm{Prop}\mathcal{C}
, will be reserved for the subcategory of
\mathrm{mod\!-\!}\mathcal{C}
consisting of proper modules.
Observe that the functor (
2.40
) always sends right proper objects to zero,
and hence factors through the quotient of
\mathcal{C}
by its right proper objects, inducing a functor

(2.60)

\bar{y}:\mathcal{C}/\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{prop}}\to\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}.

The natural question is when this functor is an equivalence.
To recall (and adapt to the
A_{\infty}
setting) a sufficient criterion for
equivalence established by Efimov, we introduce the following notion of Koszul
duality.

Definition 2.12
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be an
A_{\infty}
-category over
{\mathbf{k}}
.
A full subcategory
j:\mathcal{A}\xhookrightarrow{}\mathcal{C}
is called a Koszul dualizing subcategory,
if the following conditions are satisfied:

(i)

\mathcal{A}
is a subcategory of
\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{prop}}
,

(ii)

For any two proper right modules
M,N
over
\mathcal{A}^{op}
, or equivalently proper left modules over
\mathcal{A}
,
the natural morphism

M^{*}\otimes_{\mathcal{A}}N=N\otimes_{\mathcal{A}^{op}}M^{*}\to\hom_{\mathrm{mod\!-\!}\mathcal{A}^{op}}(N,M)^{*}

is a quasi-isomorphism,
where the linear dual
M^{*}
carries the induced left module structure over
\mathcal{A}^{op}
, or the right module structure over
\mathcal{A}
.

(iii)

The natural functor
\mathrm{Perf}\mathcal{C}^{op}\to\mathrm{Prop}\mathcal{A}^{op}
on right modules over
\mathcal{C}^{op}
and
\mathcal{A}^{op}

induced by pullback and computing
\hom_{\mathrm{mod\!-\!}\mathcal{A}^{op}}
is cohomologically fully faithful.

If
\mathcal{C}
is a smooth
A_{\infty}
-category, there is an embedding

\mathrm{Fun}(\mathcal{C}^{op},\mathrm{Calk}_{{\mathbf{k}}})\to\mathrm{Perf}(\mathcal{C}\otimes\mathrm{Calk}_{{\mathbf{k}}}),

by noticing that functors embed in the ’right perfect’ bimodules.
Thus, the kernel of
\bar{y}
is the subcategory of
\mathcal{C}/\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{prop}}
which represent zero modules over
\mathcal{C}\otimes\mathrm{Calk}_{{\mathbf{k}}}
.
In fact, with the extra conditions in Definition
2.12
,
one can show moreover that the functor
\bar{y}
is indeed cohomologically fully faithful.
The following Proposition
2.13
is essentially in
[
efimov
]
, in the case of dg categories.
For the convenience of the reader, we include an analogous proof in the case of
A_{\infty}
-categories.
For the proof of Proposition
2.13
,
we will need a certain
A_{\infty}
-module of the form

(2.61)

\hom_{\mathrm{mod\!-\!}\mathcal{C}}(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{N}),

defined by taking the right
\mathcal{C}
-module
\hom
from the right module structure on a given bimodule
\mathcal{P}
over
\mathcal{C}
to a right
\mathcal{C}
-module.
The underlying cochain space, after specializing a testing object
Y
for the right module
\hom_{\mathrm{mod\!-\!}\mathcal{C}}(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{N})
, is

(2.62)

\hom_{\mathrm{mod\!-\!}\mathcal{C}}(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{N})(Y)=\hom_{\mathrm{mod\!-\!}\mathcal{C}}(\mathcal{P}(Y,\cdot),\mathcal{N}).

The differential
\mu^{0}_{\hom_{\mathrm{mod\!-\!}\mathcal{C}}(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{N})}
is given by the differential in the dg category
\mathrm{mod\!-\!}\mathcal{C}
taking all the right-sided bimodule structure maps
\bigoplus_{l}\mu^{0,l}_{\mathcal{P}}
of
\mathcal{P}
.
The higher right module structure maps

(2.63)

\mu^{k}:\hom_{\mathrm{mod\!-\!}\mathcal{C}}(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{N})(Y_{0})\otimes\mathcal{C}(Y_{1},Y_{0})\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathcal{C}(Y_{k},Y_{k-1})\to\hom_{\mathrm{mod\!-\!}\mathcal{C}}(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{N})(Y_{k})

are defined by

(2.64)

\mu^{k}(f,c_{1},\ldots,c_{k})=f_{c_{1},\ldots,c_{k}}\in\hom_{\mathrm{mod\!-\!}\mathcal{C}}(\mathcal{P}(Y_{k},\cdot),\mathcal{N})

where
f_{c_{1},\ldots,c_{k}}
is the module morphism consisting of the following sequence of maps

(2.65)

f_{c_{1},\ldots,c_{k}}^{l}(p,a_{1},\ldots,a_{l})=\sum_{i}(-1)^{*}f^{l-i}(\mu^{k,i}_{\mathcal{P}}(c_{1},\ldots,c_{k},p,a_{1},\ldots,a_{i}),a_{i+1},\ldots,a_{l}),

with sign

(2.66)

*=|f|(|a_{i+1}|+\cdots+|a_{l}|-l+j-1).

Proposition 2.13

(
[
efimov
]
Proposition 9.3)
.

If
\mathcal{C}
is smooth and has a Koszul dualizing subcategory
\mathcal{A}
, then the functor
\bar{y}
(
2.60
) is a quasi-equivalence.

Proof.

It suffices to prove that the natural functor
\mathcal{C}/\mathcal{A}\to\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}
is a quasi-equivalence.
By the construction of
\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}
, this functor is essentially surjective on objects.
It remains to show that it is cohomologically fully faithful.

Let
K,L\in\mathrm{ob\ }\mathcal{C}
,
thought of as objects of
\mathcal{C}/\mathcal{A}
, and of
\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}
via the functor
y
.
We compute their
\hom
in
\mathcal{C}/\mathcal{A}
to be

(2.67)

(\mathcal{C}/\mathcal{A})(K,L)\cong\mathrm{Cone}(j^{*}Y^{r}_{L}\otimes_{\mathcal{A}}j^{*}Y^{l}_{K}\to\mathcal{C}(K,L)).

We also compute their
\hom
in
\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}
to be

(2.68)

\begin{split}\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty}(y(K),y(L))\cong&\mathrm{Cone}(\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{C}^{op},(Y^{r}_{K})^{\vee}\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}Y^{r}_{L})\to\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{C}^{op},\hom_{{\mathbf{k}}}(Y^{r}_{K},Y^{r}_{L})))\\
\cong&\mathrm{Cone}(\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{C}^{op},(Y^{r}_{K})^{\vee}\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}Y^{r}_{L})\to\mathcal{C}(K,L))\end{split}

where the second quasi-isomorphism is the statement of Yoneda lemma.
Let
\mathcal{C}_{\Delta}
denote the diagonal bimodule over
\mathcal{C}
,
whose underlying complex is

\mathcal{C}_{\Delta}(K,L)=\mathcal{C}(L,K).

Thus, it suffices to find a quasi-isomorphism

(2.69)

j^{*}Y^{r}_{L}\otimes_{\mathcal{A}}j^{*}Y^{l}_{K}\to\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{C}^{op},(Y^{r}_{K})^{\vee}\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}Y^{r}_{L}),

that is compatible with maps to
\mathcal{C}(K,L)
.

Consider the following composition of quasi-isomorphisms:

(2.70)

j^{*}Y^{r}_{L}\otimes_{\mathcal{A}}j^{*}Y^{l}_{K}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}j^{*}\hom_{\mathrm{mod\!-\!}\mathcal{C}}(\mathcal{C}_{\Delta},Y^{r}_{L})\otimes_{\mathcal{A}}j^{*}Y^{l}_{K}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{C}^{op},((\mathrm{id}\times j)^{*}\mathcal{C}_{\Delta})^{\vee}\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}Y^{r}_{L})\otimes_{\mathcal{A}}j^{*}Y^{l}_{K}.

Here
j^{*}\hom_{\mathrm{mod\!-\!}\mathcal{C}}(\mathcal{C}_{\Delta},Y^{r}_{L})
is the right module over
\mathcal{A}
defined as

(2.71)

j^{*}\hom_{\mathrm{mod\!-\!}\mathcal{C}}(\mathcal{C}_{\Delta},Y^{r}_{L})(-)=\hom_{\mathrm{mod\!-\!}\mathcal{C}}(\mathcal{C}_{\Delta}(j(-),\cdot),Y^{r}_{L}).

The second quasi-isomorphism in (
2.70
) follows from the assumption that the diagonal bimodule
\mathcal{C}_{\Delta}
is perfect,
as well as that
\mathcal{A}
is a proper subcategory.

Again because
\mathcal{C}
is smooth, we can ‘bring in the tensor factor’ by Proposition
2.10
, and obtain a quasi-isomoprhism

(2.72)

\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{C}^{op},((\mathrm{id}\times j)^{*}\mathcal{C}_{\Delta})^{\vee}\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}Y^{r}_{L})\otimes_{\mathcal{A}}j^{*}Y^{l}_{K}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{C}^{op},((\mathrm{id}\times j)^{*}\mathcal{C}_{\Delta})^{\vee}\otimes_{\mathcal{A}}j^{*}Y^{l}_{K}\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}Y^{r}_{L}).

Then, by the condition (ii) of Definition
2.12
,
we have a quasi-isomoprhism

(2.73)

\begin{split}&\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{C}^{op},((\mathrm{id}\times j)^{*}\mathcal{C}_{\Delta})^{\vee}\otimes_{\mathcal{A}}j^{*}Y^{l}_{K}\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}Y^{r}_{L})\\
=&\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{C}^{op},j^{*}Y^{l}_{K}\otimes_{\mathcal{A}^{op}}((\mathrm{id}\times j)^{*}\mathcal{C}_{\Delta})^{\vee}\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}Y^{r}_{L})\\
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}&\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{C}^{op},\hom_{\mathrm{mod\!-\!}\mathcal{A}^{op}}(j^{*}Y^{l}_{K},(\mathrm{id}\times j)^{*}\mathcal{C}_{\Delta})^{\vee}\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}Y^{r}_{L}).\end{split}

Finally, by the condition (iii) of Definition
2.12
,
we obtain a quasi-isomorphism

(2.74)

\begin{split}&\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{C}^{op},\hom_{\mathrm{mod\!-\!}\mathcal{A}^{op}}(j^{*}Y^{l}_{K},(\mathrm{id}\times j)^{*}\mathcal{C}_{\Delta})^{\vee}\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}Y^{r}_{L})\\
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}&\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{C}^{op},\hom_{\mathrm{mod\!-\!}\mathcal{C}^{op}}(Y^{l}_{K},\mathcal{C}_{\Delta})^{\vee}\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}Y^{r}_{L})\\
=&\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{C}^{op},\hom_{\mathrm{mod\!-\!}\mathcal{C}}(\mathcal{C}_{\Delta},Y^{r}_{K})^{\vee}\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}Y^{r}_{L})\\
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}&\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{C}^{op},(Y^{r}_{K})^{\vee}\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}Y^{r}_{L}).\end{split}

Combining (
2.70
), (
2.72
), (
2.73
), (
2.74
), we get the desired quasi-isomorphism (
2.69
),
which completes the proof of cohomological fully faithfulness.
∎

3.
Moduli spaces of domains and maps

In this section, we collect the various moduli spaces of disks and maps (and their compactifications and boundary strata) that are required for the main constructions of our paper. Our construction begins by fusing together the disks and Floer equations of
[
abouzaid1
]
with the “popsicle framework” introduced in
[
abouzaidseidel
]
, which we use in a manner somewhat different from
loc. cit.
, as a convenient mechanism for tracking “higher homotopies” (as also used in e.g.,
[
seidel6
]
); see Remark
3.3
for more elaboration on the technical differences.
In particular, unlike the construction in
[
abouzaidseidel
]
or
[
seidel6
]
, it will turn out (as in
[
abouzaid1
]
) that the associated
definition of wrapped Floer cohomology/wrapped Fukaya categories given in §
4.1
using such moduli spaces will not require any non-trivial “popsicles”; the variants of popsicles described here will be mostly used in our framework of Rabinowitz Fukaya categories (discussed in Sections
4.2
and
4.3
) as well as in the proof of Theorem
1.1
.
The other main difference is that the inputs and outputs will carry an addition decoration of being “positive” or “negative”,
which means that when it comes to defining maps from popsicles, we reverse negative inputs into positive outputs and take the dual.

3.1.
Popsicle domains

Popsicles are introduced in
[
abouzaidseidel
]
to define the wrapped Fukaya category using linear Hamiltonians.
To keep the review short, we closely follow the exposition sketched in
[
rittersmith
]
.
For an integer
d\geq 1
, let
F
be a finite set, and
\mathbf{p}:F\to\{1,\ldots d\}
a map that defines labels, where we indicate
p_{f}:=\mathbf{p}(f)
for
f\in F
. (In general there is no need for the
p_{f}
to be distinct; however when we define the
A_{\infty}
-structure maps for the wrapped Fukaya category, we will restrict to the case where
F\subseteq\{1,\ldots,d\}
and
\mathbf{p}
is the inclusion.)

A
popsicle
of flavor
\mathbf{p}
consists of a pair
(S,\sigma)
, where

(i)

S
is a Riemann surface isomorphic to a disk with
d+1
boundary punctures

S:=D^{2}\setminus\{z_{0},\dots,z_{d}\},

where
z_{0},...,z_{d}
are points of
\partial D^{2}
, ordered cyclically counterclockwise along the boundary of
D^{2}
.

(ii)

\sigma
is a collection of holomorphisms

\left\{\sigma_{f}:S\rightarrow D^{2}\right\}_{f\in F}

that each extend to a biholomorphism

\overline{\sigma}_{f}:S\cup\{z_{1},\dots,\hat{z}_{p_{f}},\dots,z_{d}\}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}\times[0,1]

taking the puncture
z_{0}
to
-\infty
and the puncture
z_{p_{f}}
to
+\infty
.
Equivalently, we can think of
\sigma_{f}
as a choice of a preferred point on the unique hyperbolic geodesic
C_{p_{f}}
connecting the points at infinity
z_{0}
and
z_{p_{f}}
. This point is
\sigma_{f}(0,\frac{1}{2})
,
which we interchangeably denote by the same symbol
\sigma_{f}
as well.
Call the geodesics
C_{k}

popsicle sticks
and the points
\sigma_{f}\in C_{p_{f}}

sprinkles
. Observe that the size
|\mathbf{p}^{-1}(i)|
is the number of sprinkles on the
i
th geodesic
C_{i}
.

A popsicle
(S,\sigma)
of flavor
\mathbf{p}
is stable if
d+|F|\geq 2
. The stable popsicles form a moduli space
\mathcal{R}^{d+1,\mathbf{p}}
, which is a smooth manifold of dimension
d+|F|-2
, diffeomorphic to
\mathbb{R}^{d+|F|-2}
.
This moduli space can be compactified by adding broken popsicles.
Broken popsicles are modelled over a ribbon tree
T
with
d
leaves and one root, just as in the case of disks.
Suppose for each vertex
v
, the valency
|v|\geq 2
.
Let
\mathbf{F}=\{F_{v}\}_{v\in T}
be a decomposition of
F
into subsets,
and let
j_{v}:F_{v}\to F
be the inclusion map.
Let
\mathbf{p}_{v}:F_{v}\to\{1,\cdots,|v|-1\}
be the map induced by
\mathbf{p}
.
The decomposition and the induced maps should satisfy the following condition: for each
f\in F_{v}
, the
\mathbf{p}_{v}(f)
-th edge adjacent to
v
either is the
p_{f}
-th leaf of the tree, or lies on the path from
v
to that leaf.

A broken popsicle of type
T
and flavors
\vec{\mathbf{p}}=\{\mathbf{p}_{v}\}_{v\in T}
is a collection of popsicles
\{(S_{v},\sigma_{v})\}_{v\in T}
where
(S_{v},\sigma_{v})
is a popsicle of flavor
\mathbf{p}_{v}
.
It is stable if for every vertex
v
,
|v|+|F_{v}|\geq 3
.
These form a moduli space, the relevant product of moduli spaces of stable popsicles:

(3.1)

\mathcal{R}^{T,\vec{\mathbf{p}}}\cong\prod_{v\in T}\mathcal{R}^{|v|,\mathbf{p}_{v}}.

The stable map compactification of the moduli space
\mathcal{R}^{d+1,\mathbf{p}}
is the disjoint of all these moduli spaces of popsicles (over all such ribbon trees
T
with
d
leaves and one root)

(3.2)

\bar{\mathcal{R}}^{d+1,\mathbf{p}}=\coprod_{T,\mathbf{F}}\mathcal{R}^{T,\vec{\mathbf{p}}},

which is a compact manifold with corners.

There is a group
Aut(\mathbf{p})
acting on the moduli space
\mathcal{R}^{d+1,\mathbf{p}}
, the group of automorphisms of the index set
F
preserving
\mathbf{p}
, i.e
g\in Aut(\mathbf{p})\subset Sym(F)
if
p_{f}=p_{g(f)}
.
Note that if
\mathbf{p}
is injective, then
Aut(\mathbf{p})
is trivial.

3.2.
Auxiliary data

Fix a
\mathbf{p}
as above.
Let
\mathbf{w}=(w_{0},\ldots,w_{d})
be a collection of non-positive integers,
w_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\leq 0}
, called weights,
satisfying the following conditions

(3.3)

w_{0}=w_{1}+\cdots+w_{d}+|F|,

(3.4)

|\mathbf{p}^{-1}(i)|\leq-w_{i}\text{ for all }i=1,\ldots,d.

We think of each weight
w_{i}
as being assigned to the
i
th puncture
z_{i}
of a popsicle of flavor
\mathbf{p}
(e.g.,
w_{0}
is assigned to the outgoing puncture
z_{0}
).
The moduli space
\mathcal{R}^{d+1,\mathbf{p}}
can be in particular be decorated with associated weights
\mathbf{w}
, with result denoted
\mathcal{R}^{d+1,\mathbf{p},\mathbf{w}}
, though there is nothing changed in the space itself.

A collection of weights for a popsicle uniquely determines collections of weights
(w_{v,0},\ldots,w_{v,|v|-1})
for each component of a broken popsicle in the following way.
On each component
(S_{v},\sigma_{v})
, if the
ith
incoming puncture
z_{v,i}
is one of the original points
z_{j}\in\{z_{0},\cdots,z_{d}\}
, then we associate weight
w_{v,i}
equal to the original weight
w_{j}
assigned to that point.
In the other cases, whenever two components are connected by an edge, the weights on these two ends must agree.
In addition, the weights for each component satisfy the condition

(3.5)

w_{v,0}=w_{v,1}+\cdots+w_{v,|v|-1}+|F_{v}|.

(which determines the outgoing weight in terms of the incoming weights and
F_{v}
).
Also, the analogue of (
3.4
) carries over to each component.

We are mainly interested in the case

(3.6)

w_{0},\ldots,w_{d}\in\{-1,0\}.

We say the point
z_{i}
is a
positive marked point
if
w_{i}=0
and a
negative marked point
if
w_{i}=-1
.
If
w_{j}=0
for some
j>0
, then
\mathbf{p}^{-1}(j)=\varnothing
, which means that there is no sprinkle on the geodesic
C_{j}
.
If
w_{j}=-1
, then
|\mathbf{p}^{-1}(j)|\leq 1
.
Note that (
3.6
) might not be satisfied by broken popsicles which appear in the boundary strata of the moduli space of popsicles.
However, we have the following result:

Lemma 3.1

(
[
seidel6
]
, Lemma 4.2)
.

Let
\mathbf{w}
be a collection of weights in
\{-1,0\}
.
If in a boundary stratum of
\bar{\mathcal{R}}^{d+1,\mathbf{p},\mathbf{w}}
of codimension
1
of type
T=\{v_{2}\to v_{1}\}
the induced weights for the components of broken popsicles do not lie in
\{-1,0\}
, i.e. for some
i
,

(3.7)

w_{v_{1},i}=w_{v_{2},0}<-1,

then either of the following is true:

(i)

|\mathbf{p}_{v_{1}}^{-1}(i)|\geq 2
, or

(ii)

w_{v_{1},0}=w_{0}=-1,w_{v_{1},i}=w_{v_{2},0}=-2
, and
\mathbf{p}_{v_{1}}^{-1}(i)
consists of one element
f
.
In this case, there are exactly two possible values for
k=\mathbf{p}(j_{v_{1}}(f))

(i.e., for indices
k
of geodesics
C_{k}
where the sprinkle
\sigma_{j_{v_{1}}(f)}
can lie before breaking),
satisfying
w_{k}=w_{v_{2},k-i+1}=-1
and
\mathbf{p}_{v_{2}}^{-1}(k-i+1)=\varnothing
.

An example of a popsicle in
\mathcal{R}^{5,\mathbf{p},\mathbf{w}}
is sketched in Figure
1
,
where all the weights need to be either
0
or
-1
.
One can figure out the weights by the following rule:
the dotted geodesics are only drawn from inputs
z_{j}
with
w_{j}=-1
;
the weight
w_{0}
associated to the output
z_{0}
is the sum of the input weights
w_{j}
’s minus the number of sprinkle points.

Return to a popsicle with a smooth domain
S
, carrying weights
w_{j}\leq 0
.
Equip
S
with
strip-like ends

\{\epsilon_{j}\}_{j\in\{0,\dots,d\}}
in the following way.
Let
Z^{\pm}=\{s:\pm s\geq 0\}\times[0,1]
denote the positive/negative half-infinite strips,
with standard coordinates
(s,t)
.
Define symbols

(3.8)

\delta_{j}=\begin{cases}0,&\text{ if }j=0,w_{j}<0\text{ or }j>0,w_{j}=0,\\
-1,&\text{ if }j=0,w_{j}=0\text{ or }j>0,w_{j}>0.\end{cases}

Further, introduce a notation for signs:

(3.9)

s_{j}=\begin{cases}+,&\text{ if }j=0,w_{j}<0\text{ or }j>0,w_{j}=0,\\
-,&\text{ if }j=0,w_{j}=0\text{ or }j>0,w_{j}>0.\end{cases}

The
j
-th strip-like end near
z_{j}
is a holomorphic embedding

(3.10)

\epsilon_{j}:Z^{s_{j}}\to S,

such that
\epsilon_{j}^{-1}(\partial S)=\{t=0,1\}

and
\lim\limits_{s\to s_{j}\infty}\epsilon_{j}(s,t)=z_{j}
.
We require that the images of these
\epsilon_{j}
’s be disjoint. Note that for positive punctures
z_{i}
