, the sign of the strip-like end is positive if
z_{i}
is an input (
i>0
) and negative if
z_{i}
is an output (
i<0
), and that for negative punctures
z_{i}
the situation is reversed.

Figure 1.
A popsicle with two sprinkles

Notably, there is a sub-collection of popsicles satisfying

(3.11)

w_{0}=w_{1}=\cdots=w_{d}=0.

This condition is automatically inherited by components of a broken popsicle.
This sub-collection is precisely the usual space of disks with boundary punctures, i.e. popsicles without sprinkles,
whose moduli space is denoted

\mathcal{R}^{d+1}=\mathcal{R}^{d+1,\varnothing,\mathbf{0}}.

3.3.
Geometric preliminaries

Having described the moduli spaces of domains in the previous two subsections,
we now describe the target space geometry.
We are mainly interested in a class of open symplectic manifolds, called
Liouville manifolds
.
Recall that a Liouville manifold is a symplectic manifold
(X,\omega)
with an exact symplectic form
\omega=d\lambda
,
where
\lambda
is called the
Liouville form
, satisfying a convexity condition “near infinity” expressed in terms of the

Liouville vector field

Z
,
defined to be the symplectic dual of
\lambda
:

(3.12)

i_{Z}\omega=\lambda.

Our convention for
Z
is such that the time-
\log(\rho)
-flow of
Z
,
\psi^{\rho}
, conformally expands the symplectic form:

(3.13)

(\psi^{\rho})^{*}\omega=\rho\omega.

The convexity condition then requires that outside of a compact set in
X
, the vector field
Z
can be modeled by the expanding vector field
r\partial_{r}
on the positive symplectization
([1,\infty)_{r}\times\Sigma,r\alpha)
of a contact manifold
(\Sigma,\alpha)
.
Abusing notation, we identify
X
outside of this compact set with
[1,\infty)\times\Sigma
, thus identifying
\lambda
with
r\alpha
.

Let
L
be a Lagrangian submanifold of
X
. We say that
L
is
admissible
if

(i)

L
is
exact
, that is, there exists a function
f_{L}:L\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
such that
\theta\big|_{L}=df_{L}
,

(ii)

L
is
cylindrical at infinity
, that is, there exists a Legendrian submanifold
\Lambda
of
\Sigma
such that
L
takes the form
[1,\infty)\times\Lambda
on the infinite end
[1,\infty)\times\Sigma
, and

(iii)

the relative first Chern class
2c_{1}^{X,L}\in H^{2}(X,L)
and second Stiefel-Whitney class
w_{2}(L)\in H^{2}(L;{\mathbb{Z}}_{2})
vanish.

Condition (iii) implies that
L
can be equipped with a
Spin structure
and a
grading
.
Abusing notation, we implicitly assume that an admissible Lagrangian also comes with the data of a choice of a Spin structure (and therefore orientation) and grading.

We say a Hamiltonian
H:X\to{\mathbb{R}}
is
admissible
,
if away from compact set and in the specified cylindrical coordinates,

(3.14)

H(r,y)=r^{2}.

The set of all admissible Hamiltonians (which implicitly depends on a choice of cylindrical coordinate) is denoted by

(3.15)

\mathcal{H}(X).

Let
X_{H}
be the Hamiltonian vector field associated to
H
, uniquely defined by

dH(-)=\omega(-,X_{H}).

Denote the flow of
X_{H}
by
\phi_{H}^{t}
.

Let
c>0
be a positive real number.
An
\omega
-compatible almost-complex structure
J
is said to be
c
-rescaled contact type
on the cylindrical end,
if it satisfies

(3.16)

\frac{c}{r}\lambda\circ J=dr

on
[1,\infty)\times\Sigma
.
The set of all
c
-rescaled contact type almost complex structures is denoted by

(3.17)

\mathcal{J}_{c}(X).

We assume that the Liouville form
\lambda
is chosen generically such that

(3.18)

\begin{split}&\text{ all Reeb orbits of }\alpha\text{ are non-degenerate, and }\\
&\text{all Reeb chords between Legendrian boundaries of admissible Lagrangians are non-degenerate}.\end{split}

3.4.
Inhomogeneous pseudoholomorphic maps

Now let us consider maps from popsicles to our target Liouville manifold
X
.
Consider a popsicle
(S,\sigma)
, equipped with weights
w_{j}\leq 0
.
To write down the appropriate inhomogeneous Cauchy-Riemann equation on
S
, we need to choose Floer data.
Let us further equip each strip-like end
\epsilon_{j}
with a choice of positive real number
\nu_{j}\geq 1
,
which we will call a
rescaling factor
for the strip-like end.
These rescaling factors are called and in fact identified with the popsicle weights in
[
abouzaidseidel
]
,
but they are different in our setup, and are not to be confused with the weights
w_{j}
chosen for the popsicle.
The rescaling factors
\nu_{j}
for strip-like ends should satisfy the following inequality:

(3.19)

\sum_{j=0}^{d}(-1)^{\delta_{j}}\nu_{j}\leq 0,

where
\delta_{j}
are as in (
3.8
).
In addition, fix a time-dependent almost complex structure
J_{t}:[0,1]\to\mathcal{J}_{1}(X)
.

Definition 3.2
.

A Floer datum
\mathbf{D}_{S,\sigma,\mathbf{w}}
on a popsicle
(S,\sigma)
with weights
\mathbf{w}
in
\mathbb{Z}_{\leq 0}
consists of the following choices:

(i)

A collection of strip-like ends
\epsilon_{j},j=0,\ldots,d
;

(ii)

Rescaling factors
\nu_{j}\geq 1
satisfying (
3.19
);

(iii)

A sub-closed one-form
\alpha_{S}\in\Omega^{1}(S)
such that
\alpha_{S}|_{\partial S}=0
,
d\alpha_{S}\leq 0
everywhere, and

\epsilon_{j}^{*}\alpha_{S}=\nu_{j}dt.

(iv)

A rescaling function
\rho_{S}:S\to[1,+\infty)
such that
\rho_{S}
is constant and equal to
\nu_{j}
over the
j
-th strip-like end
\epsilon_{j}
.

(v)

A domain-dependent family of Hamiltonians
H_{S}:S\to\mathcal{H}(X)
which is compatible with weighted strip-like ends in the following sense:

(3.20)

\epsilon_{j}^{*}H_{S}=\frac{H\circ\psi^{\nu_{j}}}{\nu_{j}^{2}},j=0,\ldots,d.

(vi)

A domain-dependent family of almost complex structures
J_{S}
such that
J_{z}\in\mathcal{J}_{\rho_{S}(z)}(X)
for every
z\in S
,
and

(3.21)

\epsilon_{j}^{*}J_{S}=(\psi^{\nu_{j}})^{*}J_{t},j=0,\ldots,d.

Liouville flow induces an
{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}
action on the space of Floer data for any
(S,\sigma)
, preserving the collection of strip-like ends and sending, for
\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}
,

(3.22)

(\{\nu_{j}\},\alpha_{S},\rho_{S},H_{S},J_{S})\mapsto(\{\lambda\nu_{j}\},\lambda\alpha_{S},\lambda\rho_{S},\frac{H_{S}\circ\psi^{\lambda}}{\lambda^{2}},(\psi^{\lambda})^{*}J_{S}).

There is a further
{\mathbb{R}}
-action on the space of Floer data by adding a constant to the Hamiltonian function

(3.23)

H\mapsto H+C.

We also extend the definition to the case of an unstable domain
Z
,
where the popsicle structure is trivial.
In that case, a Floer datum is greatly simplified:
the rescaling factors are both
1
,
the one-form is
dt
,
the rescaling function is the constant function
1
,
the Hamiltonian is domain-independent
H
,
and the almost complex structure is
J_{t}
.

Remark 3.3
.

The integer weights
w_{j}
in the definition of our popsicles have no effects on the sub-closed one-forms as part of Floer data chosen on the domain, but only the rescaling factors
\nu_{j}
do,
whereas in
[
abouzaidseidel
]
,
[
seidel6
]
these two collections of numbers coincide, and together with the popsicle structures, they determine the asymptotics of the sub-closed one-form.

A
Lagrangian label
is a collection of admissible Lagrangians
L_{0},\ldots L_{d}
, each of which is assigned to a boundary component of
S
.
We denote the component between
z_{j}
and
z_{j+1}
by
\partial_{j}S
for
0\leq j<d
,
and the component between
z_{0}
and
z_{d}
by
\partial_{d}S
.
Then
L_{j}
is assigned to the boundary component
\partial_{j}S
.

Having chosen a Floer datum and a Lagrangian label for the
\mathbf{w}
-weighted popsicle
(S,\sigma)
,
we can write down the
inhomogeneous Cauchy-Riemann equation
for a map
u:S\to X

(3.24)

(du-X_{H_{S}}\otimes\alpha_{S})^{0,1}=\frac{1}{2}[(du-X_{H_{S}}\otimes\alpha_{S})+J_{S}\circ(du-X_{H}\otimes\alpha_{S})\circ j]=0,

such that the following boundary and asymptotic conditions are satisfied:

(3.25)

\begin{cases}u(z)\in(\psi^{\rho_{S}(z)})^{*}L_{j}\text{ for }z\in\partial_{j}S\\
\lim\limits_{s\to s_{j}\infty}u\circ\epsilon_{j}(s,\cdot)=(\psi^{\nu_{j}})^{*}x_{j},\end{cases}

where

•

For a Lagrangian
L
,
(\psi^{\rho})^{*}(L):=(\psi^{\rho})^{-1}(L)
;

•

for
j=1,\ldots,d
,
x_{j}
is a time-one chord for
H
,
which runs from
L_{j-1}
to
L_{j}
if
w_{j}=0
,
or from
L_{j}
to
L_{j-1}
if
w_{j}=-1
; and

•

x_{0}
is a time-one chord for
H

which runs from
L_{0}
to
L_{d}
if
w_{0}=0

or from
L_{d}
to
L_{0}
if
w_{0}=-1
.

In the translation-invariant case where
S=Z=\mathbb{R}\times[0,1]
is a strip and the weights are
w_{0}=w_{1}=0
,
choose the one-form to be translation-invariant on the whole strip,
\alpha=dt
,
and the rescaling function
\rho_{Z}
to be the constant function
1
.
Then (
3.24
) becomes the familiar Floer’s equation for a map
u:Z\to X
:

(3.26)

\frac{\partial u}{\partial s}+J_{t}\left(\frac{\partial u}{\partial t}-X_{H}\right)=0

and (
3.25
) becomes

(3.27)

\begin{cases}u(s,0)\in L_{0},u(s,1)\in L_{1}\\
\lim\limits_{s\to-\infty}u(s,\cdot)=x_{0},\lim\limits_{s\to+\infty}u(s,\cdot)=x_{1}.\end{cases}

In the other case where
w_{0}=w_{1}=-1
,
the equation is the same as (
3.26
),
but we interchange the roles of the input and the output:

(3.28)

\lim\limits_{s\to+\infty}u(s,\cdot)=x_{0},\lim\limits_{s\to-\infty}u(s,\cdot)=x_{1},

where
x_{0}
and
x_{1}
are chords from from
L_{1}
to
L_{0}
instead (i.e.,
u(s,0)\in L_{1}
and
u(s,1)\in L_{0}
)

3.5.
Moduli space of popsicle maps

The moduli space of popsicle maps is the space of equivalence classes of triple
(S,\sigma,u)
, with
(S,\sigma)
an arbitrary popsicle, such that
u
satisfies (
3.24
) and (
3.25
).
In order for such moduli spaces to have nice compactifications, we need to make a
universal and conformally consistent
choice of Floer data in the sense of
[
abouzaid1
]
. This is by definition a smoothly varying choice of Floer data for every
(S,\sigma)
in
\bar{\mathcal{R}}^{d+1,\mathbf{p},\mathbf{w}}
for each
d,\mathbf{p},\mathbf{w}
which agrees with previously made choices along boundary strata up to the
{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}\times{\mathbb{R}}
(Liouville rescaling and constant addition) actions described in (
3.22
)-(
3.23
).
In addition, the space of Floer data for popsicles of type
(d+1,\mathbf{p},\mathbf{w})
fibers over
\mathcal{R}^{d+1,\mathbf{p},\mathbf{w}}
;
and we assume that the action of
Aut(\mathbf{p})
lifts, meaning that we always choose Floer data to be invariant under
Aut(\mathbf{p})
-action.
This is possible because

•

the average of one-forms or rescaling functions still satisfies the conditions of a Floer datum;

•

we can take
Aut(\mathbf{p})
-invariant sections of the pullback bundle
\pi^{*}End(TX)\to\mathcal{S}^{d+1,\mathbf{p},\mathbf{w}}\times X
and exponentiate them to perturb a fixed almost complex structure.

All the choices involved in the definition of a Floer datum form a contractible space,
so that a universal and conformally consistent choice of Floer data exists by a standard induction argument.
we make such a choice as well as a choice of consistent Lagrangian labels.
Given a collection of Hamiltonian chords
\mathbf{x}=(x_{0},\ldots,x_{d})
, define

(3.29)

\mathcal{R}^{d+1,\mathbf{p},\mathbf{w}}(\mathbf{x})

to be the moduli space of triples
(S,\sigma,u)
,
such that
u
satisfies (
3.24
) and (
3.25
).
This is called the moduli space of popsicle maps of type
(d+1,\mathbf{p},\mathbf{w})
.
In the case where
d=1
and the domain
F
of
\mathbf{p}
is empty,
we use the same notation for the moduli space of non-constant solutions modulo translation action by
\mathbb{R}
,

\mathcal{R}^{2}(x_{0},x_{1})=\mathcal{R}^{2,\varnothing,\mathbf{0}}(x_{0},x_{1}).

More generally, when
d
is arbitrary and the domain of
F
of
\mathbf{p}
is empty, (
3.29
) specializes to the usual moduli spaces of maps

(3.30)

\mathcal{R}^{d+1}(\mathbf{x}):=\mathcal{R}^{d+1,\varnothing,\mathbf{0}}(\mathbf{x})

appearing in the construction of wrapped Fukaya categories as in
[
abouzaid1
]
.
By Sard-Smale and Baire category theorem, the set of Floer data that make all moduli spaces
\mathcal{R}^{d+1,\mathbf{p},\mathbf{w}}(\mathbf{x})
smooth is non-empty, and is in fact dense in a Banach manifold.
Such Floer data are called
regular
, and from now on we shall always assume we choose regular Floer data.

Remark 3.4
.

Our moduli space of popsicle maps is different from the ones considered in
[
abouzaidseidel
]
or in
[
seidel6
]
,
as the Floer data are chosen differently.
Here we turn every input with a negative weight
w<0
into an output, and vice versa.
And we have chosen additional rescaling factors
\nu_{j}
to define the one-form which have no relation to the popsicle weights
w_{j}
,
except that they must satisfy (
3.19
).

Lemma 3.5
.

The moduli space
\mathcal{R}^{d+1,\mathbf{p},\mathbf{w}}(\mathbf{x})
is a smooth manifold of dimension

(3.31)

\dim\mathcal{R}^{d+1,{\bf p},{\bf w}}({\bf x})=d-2+|F|+n(1+\sum_{j=0}^{d}\delta_{j})-\sum_{j=0}^{d}(-1)^{\delta_{j}}\deg(x_{j}),

where
\delta_{j}
are as in (
3.8
).

Proof.

The dimension follows from an index computation for the associated linearized Fredholm operator, following Proposition 11.13 of
[
seidel_book
]
.
∎

Although the way we choose Floer data is different from the usual way for popsicles used in the linear Hamiltonian framework,
it turns out that the moduli space (
3.29
) has a natural Gromov compactification

(3.32)

\bar{\mathcal{R}}^{d+1,\mathbf{p},\mathbf{w}}(\mathbf{x}),

which behaves exactly the same as one would expect for moduli spaces of broken popsicles.
The boundary strata are obtained by adding broken popsicles with non-positive weights,
plus additionally inhomogeneous pseudoholomorphic strips (
3.26
).
These can be described as follows.
Take
(T,\vec{\mathbf{p}})
an underlying model for a broken popsicle, but without imposing the stability condition
|v|+|F_{v}|\geq 3
.
For each vertex
v
, we have a popsicle map

(3.33)

(S_{v},\sigma_{v},u_{v})\in\mathcal{R}^{|v|,\mathbf{p}_{v},\mathbf{w}_{v}}(\mathbf{x}_{v}).

The only unstable case is where
|v|=2
and
F_{v}=\varnothing
.
In that case we have a popsicle map in
\mathcal{R}^{2,(w_{v,0},w_{v,1})}(x_{v,0},x_{v,1})
with
w_{v,0}=w_{v,1}
, which is a translation-invariant inhomogeneous pseudoholomorphic strip.
The asymptotic chords
\mathbf{x}_{v}=(x_{v,0},\ldots,x_{v,|v|-1})
should satisfy the following condition:

•

If the
k
-th edge of
v
corresponds to the
j
-th leaf, then
x_{v,k}=x_{j}
is one of the given chords for a smooth popsicle
u
;

•

Otherwise, if
v_{-}
and
v_{+}
are adjacent vertices connected by a finite edge which is the
0
-th edge for
v_{-}
and
k
-th edge for
v_{+}
, we require that
x_{v_{-},0}=x_{v_{+},k}
.

Under the assumption that our choices of Floer data are regular, we have

Proposition 3.6
.

When the virtual dimension (
3.31
) is zero,
then
\mathcal{R}^{d+1,\mathbf{p},\mathbf{w}}(\mathbf{x})
is a compact smooth manifold of dimension zero.
When the virtual dimension (
3.31
) is one,
the compactified moduli space
\bar{\mathcal{R}}^{d+1,\mathbf{p},\mathbf{w}}(\mathbf{x})
is a compact smooth manifold-with-boundary,
with boundary strata given by moduli spaces of stable maps from broken popsicles that are modeled on trees
T
with two vertices,
such that each component of a broken popsicle map belongs to a zero-dimensional moduli space of popsicle maps with smooth domains.

Proof.

Standard Gromov compactness and gluing arguments apply once it is shown, via some version of a maximum principle, that image of elements of the moduli spaces
\mathcal{R}^{d+1,\mathbf{p},\mathbf{w}}(\mathbf{x})
lie in a fixed compact subset (possibly depending on
\mathbf{x}
and the system of Floer data chosen) of
X
. For this needed fact see e.g.,
[
abouzaidseidel
]
*Lemma 7.2 or
[
abouzaid1
]
*§B.
∎

3.6.
Disks with no output

The purpose of this subsection is to introduce certain moduli spaces of disks that are needed for establishing a version of bimodule Poincaré duality for wrapped Floer theory, whose algebraic formulation will be spelt out in §
5.6
.
From the algebraic perspective of Poincaré duality, these disks should be thought of as disks with two main inputs, arbitrarily many auxiliary inputs and no output,
where the two main inputs are equipped with weights
-1
and
0
as in the framework of weighted popsicles.
However, analytically it will be more convenient to reverse the negatively weighted input to a positively weighted output,
and thus to consider disks with one main input and one main output.

Let
S_{p}
be a Riemann surface isomorphic to a disk with two boundary punctures
z_{-},z_{+}
,
with one additional interior marked point
\zeta
such that the domain is stable.
The moduli space of such disks is a one-dimensional smooth connected manifold, and one explicit diffeomorphism with
\mathbb{R}
can be seen as follows.
Each such disk admits a unique biholomorphism to the unit disk in the complex plane mapping
one of the boundary punctures to
-1
and the interior marked point
\zeta
to
0
.
The other boundary marked point is then mapped to some point
z\in\partial D^{2}\setminus\{-1\}
, and the space of possible such positions is diffeomorphic to
\mathbb{R}
.

We could think of this as a variant of a popsicle with two input punctures, with first input
z_{-}
negative and second input
z_{+}
positive i.e., with associated weights
w_{-}=-1
,
w_{+}=0
in the sense of §
3.2
, but we will not emphasize that perspective.
Choose strip-like ends

(3.34)

\epsilon_{-}:Z^{-}\to S_{p}

around
z_{-}
, and

(3.35)

\epsilon_{+}:Z^{+}\to S_{p}

around
z_{+}

If we forget the interior marked point
\zeta
,

S_{p}
is just a copy of a strip
Z
.
However, with the extra interior marked point
\zeta
, there is no translation symmetry on
S_{p}
.

Choose a Lagrangian label
K,L
for the two boundary components divided by the two punctures
z_{-},z_{+}
.
A Floer datum for
S_{p}
can be defined in a similar way to the one for a strip
Z
,
except that we do not impose on translation invariance on the family of almost complex structures.

Consider smooth maps

u:S_{p}\to X

which satisfy the inhomogeneous Cauchy-Riemann equation with respect to the chosen Floer datum,
satisfy the boundary conditions given by the Lagrangian label,
and asymptotic conditions given by some Hamiltonian chords
x_{-},x_{+}\in\mathcal{X}(L,K;H)
.
The moduli space of pairs
(S_{p},u)
where
S_{p}
is an arbitrary domain as above, is denoted by

(3.36)

\tilde{\mathcal{R}}_{p}(x_{-},x_{+}).

A standard index computation similar to Lemma
3.5
, together with the extra one dimension contributed from the moduli space of domains, implies

Lemma 3.7
.

This moduli space has virtual dimension

(3.37)

\dim\tilde{\mathcal{R}}_{p}(x_{-},x_{+})=\deg(x_{-})-\deg(x_{+})+1.

∎

Inside (
3.36
), there is a codimension-one subspace,
obtained by cutting down the moduli space of domains by the constraint that
\zeta
should only vary along the geodesic between
z_{-}
and
z_{+}
; this fixes a unique
S_{p}
.
The resulting subspace of maps from this
S_{p}
is denoted by

(3.38)

\mathcal{R}_{p}(x_{-},x_{+}),

which has virtual dimension

(3.39)

\dim\mathcal{R}_{p}(x_{-},x_{+})=\deg(x_{-})-\deg(x_{+}).

There is a natural Gromov compactification

\bar{\mathcal{R}}_{p}(x_{+,1},x_{+,2})

by adding broken inhomogeneous pseudoholomorphic strips with boundary conditions
(L,K)
over both ends.
The boundary strata of one-dimensional moduli spaces are covered by the following product moduli spaces of dimension zero:

(3.40)

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{p}(y_{-},x_{+})\times\mathcal{R}^{2}(x_{-},y_{-})

(3.41)

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{p}(x_{-},y_{+})\times\mathcal{R}^{2}(y_{+},x_{+}).

Lemma 3.8
.

The moduli spaces
\mathcal{R}_{p}(x_{-},x_{+})
with
\deg(x_{-})=\deg(x_{+})
are all empty unless
x_{-}=x_{+}
,
in which case the only element in the moduli space is the constant map.

Proof.

Choose Floer data to be translation-invariant.
If
\deg(x_{-})=\deg(x_{+})
but
x_{-}\neq x_{+}
, and
\mathcal{R}_{p}(x_{-},x_{+})\neq\varnothing
,
then there must be a non-constant solution
u:S_{p}\to X
with asymptotics
x_{-},x_{+}
.
Then, for any
s\in{\mathbb{R}}
,
u_{s}=u(\cdot+s,\cdot)
is another solution,
which implies that
\dim\mathcal{R}_{p}(x_{-},x_{+})\geq 1
, contradicting the degree condition.
∎

Now we add more boundary punctures.
For
k,l\geq 0
, consider a Riemann surface
S^{k,l}_{p}
a disk with
k+l+2
boundary punctures,

z_{-},z^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,z^{\prime}_{l},z_{+},z_{k},\ldots,z_{1},

ordered cyclically counterclockwise, and an additional interior marked point
\zeta
.
Such a Riemann surface is obtained from
S_{p}
by adding
k
punctures on one boundary component between
z_{-}
and
z_{+}
,
and another
l
punctures on the other boundary component.
Denote by

(3.42)

\mathcal{R}_{p}^{k,l}

the moduli space of such disks. See Figure
2
for a picture of such a disk.
The compactification
\bar{\mathcal{R}}_{p}^{k,l}
is obtained by adding broken disks,
such that the interior marked point belongs to one disk component of each broken disk,
and the other components belong to
\mathcal{R}^{|v|}
,
provided that the broken disk is modeled by a ribbon tree
T
with vertices
v
.

Figure 2.
A disk with two main inputs,
k,l
auxiliary inputs and one interior marked point

Choose the strip-like ends near
z_{-},z_{+}
as before,
and positive strip-like ends near the other punctures

\epsilon_{i}:Z^{+}\to S_{p}^{k,l},i=1,\ldots,k,

and

\epsilon^{\prime}_{j}:Z^{+}\to S_{p}^{k,l},j=1,\ldots,l.

We want to equip these strip-like ends with weights
\nu_{-},\nu_{+},\nu_{i},\nu^{\prime}_{j}
, which are positive real numbers
\geq 1
,
such that

(3.43)

\nu_{-}\geq\nu_{+}+\sum_{i=1}^{k}\nu_{i}+\sum_{j=1}^{l}\nu^{\prime}_{j}.

Following Definition
3.2
, we can define the notion of a Floer datum
\mathbf{D}_{S_{p}^{k,l}}
for a fixed
S_{p}^{k,l}
.
Since the boundary strata of
\bar{\mathcal{R}}_{p}^{k,l}
consist of product moduli spaces of the same type together with moduli spaces of popsicles,
the definition of a universal and conformally consistent choice of Floer data is naturally extended to the moduli spaces of such domains
\bar{\mathcal{R}}_{p}^{k,l}
, which is further required to be compatible with that choice for moduli spaces of popsicles.

Choose a Lagrangian label

L_{0},\ldots,L_{l},K_{k},\ldots,K_{0},

such that
L_{0}
is assigned to the boundary component between
z_{-}
and
z^{\prime}_{1}
,

L_{j}
is assigned to the boundary component between
z^{\prime}_{j}
and
z^{\prime}_{j+1}
, for
j=1,\ldots,l-1
,

L_{l}
is assigned to the boundary component between
z^{\prime}_{l}
and
z_{+}
,

K_{k}
is assigned to the boundary component between
z_{+}
and
z_{k}
,

K_{i}
is assigned to the boundary component between
z_{i+1}
and
z_{i}
, for
i=1,\ldots,k-1
,
and
K_{0}
is assigned to the boundary component between
z_{1}
and
z_{-}
.

Suppose we have made a universal and conformally consistent choice of Floer data for all
\bar{\mathcal{R}}_{p}^{k,l}
, all
k,l\geq 0
.
Consider smooth maps

u:S^{k,l}_{p}\to X

that satisfy the inhomogeneous Cauchy-Riemann equation

(3.44)

(du-X_{H_{S_{p}^{k,l}}}\otimes\alpha_{S_{p}^{k,l}})^{0,1}=0,

with boundary conditions given by the chosen Lagrangian labels,

(3.45)

u(z)\in(\psi^{\rho_{S_{p}^{k,l}(z)}})^{*}K_{i}\text{ or }L_{j},

and asymptotic conditions

(3.46)

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{s\to+\infty}u\circ\epsilon_{i}(s,\cdot)=(\psi^{\nu_{i}})^{*}x_{i},

(3.47)

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{s\to+\infty}u\circ\epsilon^{\prime}_{j}(s,\cdot)=(\psi^{\nu^{\prime}_{j}})^{*}x^{\prime}_{j},

(3.48)

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{s\to-\infty}u\circ\epsilon_{-}(s,\cdot)=(\psi^{\nu_{-}})^{*}x_{-},

(3.49)

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{s\to+\infty}u\circ\epsilon_{+}(s,\cdot)=(\psi^{\nu_{+}})^{*}x_{+},

for some time-one
H
-chords
x_{i}
from
K_{i}
to
K_{i-1}
,

x^{\prime}_{j}
from
L_{j-1}
to
L_{j}
,

x_{-}
from
K_{0}
to
L_{0}
,
and
x_{+}
from
L_{l}
to
K_{k}
.
Let

(3.50)

\tilde{\mathcal{R}}^{k,l}_{p}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime};x_{-},x_{+})

denote the moduli space of such maps,
generalizing
\mathcal{R}_{p}(x_{-},x_{+})
, where
k=l=0
.
Here the notations for the chords are

(3.51)

\displaystyle\mathbf{x}

\displaystyle=(x_{k},\ldots,x_{1}),

(3.52)

\displaystyle\mathbf{x}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=(x_{1},\ldots,x^{\prime}_{l}).

As in the case where
k=l=0
, there is a codimension-one subspace obtained by constraining the interior marked point
\zeta
on the geodesic between
z_{-}
and
z_{+}
:

(3.53)

\mathcal{R}^{k,l}_{p}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime};x_{-},x_{+}).

The latter space has virtual dimension

(3.54)

\dim\mathcal{R}^{k,l}_{p}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime};x_{-},x_{+})=\deg(x_{-})-\deg(x_{+})-\sum_{i=1}^{k}\deg(x_{i})-\sum_{j=1}^{l}\deg(x^{\prime}_{j})+k+l.

A standard transversality and gluing argument along the lines of Proposition
3.6
implies

Lemma 3.9
.

There exist universal and conformally consistent choices of Floer data such that the following hold:

(i)

If

\deg(x_{-})=\deg(x_{+})+\sum_{i=1}^{k}\deg(x_{i})+\sum_{j}^{l}\deg(x^{\prime}_{j})-k-l,

the moduli space
\mathcal{R}^{k,l}_{p}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime};x_{-},x_{+})
is a compact smooth manifold of dimension zero.

(ii)

If

\deg(x_{-})=\deg(x_{+})+\sum_{i=1}^{k}\deg(x_{i})+\sum_{j=1}^{l}\deg(x^{\prime}_{j})+1-k-l,

the moduli space
\mathcal{R}^{k,l}_{p}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime};x_{-},x_{+})
is a smooth manifold,
and its Gromov compactification
\bar{\mathcal{R}}^{k,l}_{p}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime};x_{-},x_{+})
is a compact smooth manifold of dimension one,
whose codimension-one boundary strata are covered by the following products of moduli spaces of dimension zero:

(3.55)

\begin{split}&\partial\bar{\mathcal{R}}^{k,l}_{p}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime};x_{-},x_{+})\\
=&\coprod\mathcal{R}^{k_{1}+l_{1}+2,\mathbf{p}_{1},\mathbf{w}_{1}}(y_{-},\mathbf{x}_{1},x_{-},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{1})\times_{0,-}\mathcal{R}^{k_{2},l_{2}}_{p}(\mathbf{x}_{2},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{2};y_{-},x_{+})\\
\cup&\coprod\mathcal{R}^{k_{1},l_{1}}_{p}(\mathbf{x}_{1},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{1};x_{-},y_{+})\times_{+,0}\mathcal{R}^{k_{2}+l_{2}+2}(y_{+},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{2},x_{+},\mathbf{x}_{2})\\
\cup&\coprod\mathcal{R}^{k-j+1,l}_{p}(\mathbf{x}_{i,j,1},\mathbf{x}^{\prime};x_{-},x_{+})\times_{i,0}\mathcal{R}^{j+1}(\mathbf{x}_{i,j,2})\\
\cup&\coprod\mathcal{R}^{k,l-j^{\prime}+1}_{p}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime},1};x_{-},x_{+})\times_{i^{\prime},0}\mathcal{R}^{j^{\prime}+1}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime},2})\end{split}

Here the popsicle structure
\sigma_{1}
of flavor
\mathbf{p}_{1}
is trivial, i.e. has no sprinkle,
and the weights
\mathbf{w}_{1}
are
-1
at the
0
-th and
(l_{1}+1)
-th punctures, and
0
at other punctures.
The notations for the chords are as follows:

(3.56)

\displaystyle\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{1}

\displaystyle=(x^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,x^{\prime}_{l_{1}}),

(3.57)

\displaystyle\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{2}

\displaystyle=(x^{\prime}_{l_{1}+1},\ldots,x^{\prime}_{l}),

(3.58)

\displaystyle\mathbf{x}_{1}

\displaystyle=(x_{k_{1}},\ldots,x_{1}),

(3.59)

\displaystyle\mathbf{x}_{2}

\displaystyle=(x_{k},\ldots,x_{k_{1}+1}),

(3.60)

\displaystyle\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime},1}

\displaystyle=(x^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,x^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}},x^{\prime}_{new},x^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}+j^{\prime}+1},\ldots,x^{\prime}_{l})

(3.61)

\displaystyle\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime},2}

\displaystyle=(x^{\prime}_{new},x^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}+1},\ldots,x^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}+j^{\prime}})

(3.62)

\displaystyle\mathbf{x}_{i,j,1}

\displaystyle=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{i},x_{new},x_{i+j+1},\ldots,x_{k})

(3.63)

\displaystyle\mathbf{x}_{i,j,2}

\displaystyle=(x_{new},x_{i+j},\ldots,x_{i+1}).

The notation
\times_{0,-}
means that the
0
-th puncture on the first disk component is attached to the
z_{-}
puncture on the second disk component, where the interior marked point is located,
similarly for other products.

3.7.
Disks with one input and two outputs

In this subsection, we are going to introduce certain moduli spaces of disks
with one distinguished input, two distinguished outputs
and arbitrary number of auxiliary inputs.
Similar moduli spaces are used in
[
ganatra
]
to construct the non-compact Calabi-Yau structure (
5.66
) for the wrapped Fukaya category,
which will be revisited in section
5.8
.
More precisely, in
[
ganatra
]
disks with two distinguished negative punctures and two distinguished positive punctures together with arbitrary other positive punctures are considered,
for the purposing of constructing
A_{\infty}
-bimodule morphisms.
For us, we shall replace the bimodule
\mathcal{W}^{!}
(
5.66
) with a different but quasi-isomorphic bimodule,
so that the disks to be introduced below will have different numbers of boundary punctures.
Another difference is that we allow the main input and one of the output to carry weights as popsicles,
such that the input turns to an output and vice versa when the weights are negative.
In particular, disks with negative weights are used to construct a different Calabi-Yau-type map in §
5.9
.
Moreover, the description of the boundary strata of these moduli spaces will be used to compare the two Calabi-Yau-type maps,
whose precise statement will be given in Proposition
5.20
.

Consider a Riemann surface
S_{2}^{1,l;r,s}
isomorphic to a disk with
l+3+r+s
boundary punctures

(3.64)

z_{-,1},z^{+,1}_{r},\ldots,z^{+,1}_{1},z,z^{+,2}_{1},\ldots,z^{+,2}_{s},z_{-,2},z^{\prime}_{l},\ldots,z^{\prime}_{1},

ordered cyclically counterclockwise along the boundary of the disk.
Here we fix the cross-ratio of
z_{-,1},z,z_{-,2}
,
e.g. place them at
i,-1,-i
on the unit circle,
and allow other punctures to vary freely between these three special punctures.
The puncture
z
is considered as an primary input,
and
z_{-,1},z_{-,2}
as two primary outputs,
and the other punctures
z^{+,1}_{r},\ldots,z^{+,1}_{1},z^{+,2}_{1},\ldots,z^{+,2}_{s}
and
z^{\prime}_{l},\ldots,z^{\prime}_{1}
as auxiliary inputs.
The moduli space of such disks is denoted by

(3.65)

\mathcal{R}^{1,l;r,s}_{2}.

See Figure
3
for a picture of a disk in the moduli space
\mathcal{R}^{1,l;r,s}_{2}
.
The Deligne-Mumford compactification

(3.66)

\bar{\mathcal{R}}^{1,l;r,s}_{2}

is obtained by adding broken disks,
such that only one disk component has the same type,
while all the other disk components are the usual disks with boundary punctures.

\bar{\mathcal{R}}^{1,l;r,s}_{2}
can be inductively defined as the space covered by products of moduli spaces of the following types:

(3.67)

\displaystyle\bar{\mathcal{R}}^{r_{2}+s_{2}+2}

\displaystyle\times_{0}\bar{\mathcal{R}}^{1,l;r_{1},s_{1}}_{2}

(3.68)

\displaystyle\bar{\mathcal{R}}^{j+1}

\displaystyle\times_{0,i}^{+,1}\bar{\mathcal{R}}^{1,l;r-j+1,s}_{2},

(3.69)

\displaystyle\bar{\mathcal{R}}^{j^{\prime}+1}

\displaystyle\times_{0,i^{\prime}}^{+,2}\bar{\mathcal{R}}^{1,l;r,s-j^{\prime}+1}_{2}

(3.70)

\displaystyle\bar{\mathcal{R}}^{j^{\prime\prime}+1}

\displaystyle\times_{0,i^{\prime\prime}}\bar{\mathcal{R}}^{1,l-j^{\prime\prime}+1;r,s}_{2}

(3.71)

\displaystyle\bar{\mathcal{R}}^{1,l_{1};r_{1},s}_{2}

\displaystyle\times_{-,1,r_{1}+1}\bar{\mathcal{R}}^{r_{2}+l_{2}+2}

(3.72)

\displaystyle\bar{\mathcal{R}}^{1,l_{1};r,s_{1}}_{2}

\displaystyle\times_{-,2,l_{1}+1}\bar{\mathcal{R}}^{s_{2}+l_{2}+2}.

Here the notation
\times_{0}
means that the
0
-th puncture on the first disk component is matched with the
z
puncture on the second component,

\times_{0,i}^{+,1}
means that the
0
-th puncture on the first component is matched with the
z^{+,1}_{i}
puncture on the second component,

\times_{0,i^{\prime}}^{+,2}
means that the
0
-th puncture on the first component is matched with the
z^{+,2}_{i^{\prime}}
puncture on the second component,

\times_{0,i^{\prime\prime}}
means that the
0
-th puncture on the first component is matched with the
z^{\prime}_{i^{\prime\prime}}
puncture on the second component,

\times_{-,1,r_{1}+1}
means that the
z_{-,1}
puncture on the first component is matched with the
(r_{1}+1)
-th puncture on the second component,
and
\times_{-,2,l_{1}+1}
means that the
z_{-,2}
puncture on the first component is matched with the
(l_{1}+1)
-th puncture on the second component.

Figure 3.
An element of
\mathcal{R}^{1,l;r,s}_{2}

We can treat such disks as popsicles in a generalized sense with no sprinkle, i.e.
\mathbf{p}=\varnothing
in the following way.
Assign the popsicle weights in the sense of §
3.2
by

(3.73)

w^{+,1}_{i}=w^{+,2}_{j}=w_{-,2}=w^{\prime}_{h}=0,

at the punctures
z^{+,1}_{i},z^{+,2}_{j},z_{-,2}
and
z^{\prime}_{h}
(so that these are all positive punctures),
where
i=1,\ldots,r,j=1,\ldots,s,h=1,\ldots,l
.
At
z_{-,1}
and
z
, we assign the weights
w_{-,1},w
so that they are either both
0
or both
-1
(so that these are either both positive or both negative).
In either case, the condition (
3.3
) implies that the number of sprinkles
|F|=0
.
Although, strictly speaking, they are not popsicles because we have not defined popsicles structures on disks with two outputs.

Choose strip-like ends near the punctures in the following way:
near
z_{-,1}
,

\epsilon_{-,1}:Z^{-1-w_{-,1}}\to S_{2}^{1,l;r,s},

near
z
,

\epsilon:Z^{w}\to S_{2}^{1,l;r,s},

near
z^{+,1}_{i}
,

\epsilon^{+,1}_{i}:Z\to S_{2}^{1,l;r,s},i=1,\ldots,r,

near
z^{+,2}_{j}
,

\epsilon^{+,2}_{j}:Z\to S_{2}^{1,l;r,s},j=1,\ldots,s,

near
z_{-,2}
,

\epsilon_{-,2}:Z^{-}\to S_{2}^{1,l;r,s},

and near
z^{\prime}_{h}
,

\epsilon^{\prime}_{h}:Z\to Z\to S_{2}^{1,l;r,s},h=1,\ldots,l.

Equip these strip-like ends with rescaling factors
\nu_{-,1},\nu,\nu^{+,1}_{i},\nu^{+,2}_{j},\nu_{-,2},\nu^{\prime}_{h}
in the sense of §
3.4
, which are positive real numbers
\geq 1
,
which should satisfy

(3.74)

(-1)^{w}\nu+\sum_{i=1}^{r}\nu^{+,1}_{i}+\sum_{j=1}^{s}+\sum_{h=1}^{l}\nu^{\prime}_{h}\leq(-1)^{w_{-,1}}\nu_{-,1}+\nu_{-,2}

Define a Floer datum on
S_{2}^{1,l;r,s}
in a way similar to Definition
3.2
.
Given the description of the boundary strata as in (
3.67
) - (
3.72
),
the notion of a universal and conformally consistent choice of Floer data can also be extended to
\bar{\mathcal{R}}^{1,l;r,s}_{2}
, where the conformal consistency condition involves choices previously made on the moduli spaces of boundary punctured disks in §
3.4
.

A Lagrangian label is a collection of Lagrangians, ordered according to the counterclockwise order on the circle as

(3.75)

K_{r},\ldots,K_{0},L_{0},\ldots,L_{r},L^{\prime}_{0},\ldots,L^{\prime}_{l},

each of which is assigned to a boundary component on
S^{1,l;r,s}
,
such that
K_{r}
is assigned to the boundary component between
z_{-,1}
and
z^{+,1}_{r}
.

Suppose we have made a universal and conformally consistent choice of Floer data for all
\bar{\mathcal{R}}^{1,l;r,s}
,
which are conformally consistent with choice of Floer data for moduli spaces of boundary-punctured disks
\bar{\mathcal{R}}^{d+1}
.
Then we can construct moduli spaces of inhomogeneous pseudoholomorphic maps from such domains
S_{2}^{1,l;r,s}
.
As noted before, there are two cases depending on the popsicle weights assigned to the punctures
z_{-,1}
and
z
(or equivalently the signs associated to
z_{-,1}
and
z
; recall that from §
3.2
that weight
-1
is assigned to negative punctures and weight
0
is assigned to positive punctures).

The first case is

(3.76)

w_{-,1}=w=0.

In this case,
z
carries a positive strip-like end and
z_{-,1}
carries a negative strip-like end.
The moduli space of inhomogeneous pseuholomorphic maps with respect to such Floer data is denoted by

(3.77)

\mathcal{R}_{2;(0,0)}^{1,l;r,s}(\mathbf{x}^{+,1},x,\mathbf{x}^{+,2},x_{-,2},\mathbf{x}^{\prime},x_{-,1})

where
x
is a time-one
H
-chord from
K_{0}
to
L_{0}
,
and
x_{-,1}
is a time-one
H
-chord from
K_{r}
to
L^{\prime}_{l}
.

The second case is

(3.78)

w_{-,1}=w=-1.

Now
z
carries a negative strip-like end
z_{-,1}
carries a positive strip-like end.
The moduli space of inhomogeneous pseuholomorphic maps with respect to such Floer data is denoted by

(3.79)

\mathcal{R}_{2;(-1,-1)}^{1,l;r,s}(\mathbf{x}^{+,1},x,\mathbf{x}^{+,2},x_{-,2},\mathbf{x}^{\prime},x_{-,1})

where
x
now represents a time-one
H
-chord from
L_{0}
to
K_{0}
,
and
x_{-,1}
a time-one
H
-chord from
L^{\prime}_{l}
to
K_{r}
.

The basic ones, where
r=s=0
, are

(3.80)

\mathcal{R}_{2;(0,0)}^{1,l}(x,x_{-,2},\mathbf{x}^{\prime},x_{-,1})

and

(3.81)

\mathcal{R}_{2;(-1,-1)}^{1,l}(x,x_{-,2},\mathbf{x}^{\prime},x_{-,1}).

By a standard index calculation, we have

Lemma 3.10
.

The virtual dimensions of (
3.77
) and (
3.79
)
are given by the common formula

(3.82)

\begin{split}&r+s+l-n+(-1)^{-1-w_{-,1}}\deg(x_{-,1})+\deg(x_{-,2})\\
&-(-1)^{w}\deg(x)-\sum_{i=1}^{r}\deg(x^{+,1}_{i})-\sum_{j=1}^{s}\deg(x^{+,2}_{j})-\sum_{h=1}^{l}\deg(x^{\prime}_{h}).\end{split}

∎

The Gromov compactifications

(3.83)

\bar{\mathcal{R}}_{2;(0,0)}^{1,l;r,s}(\mathbf{x}^{+,1},x,\mathbf{x}^{+,2},x_{-,2},\mathbf{x}^{\prime},x_{-,1})

and

(3.84)

\bar{\mathcal{R}}_{2;(-1,-1)}^{1,l;r,s}(\mathbf{x}^{+,1},x,\mathbf{x}^{+,2},x_{-,2},\mathbf{x}^{\prime},x_{-,1})

have boundary strata consisting of product moduli spaces of maps from broken domains listed in (
3.67
) - (
3.72
).
In addition, there are extra boundary strata coming from strip breaking.
In particular, when the virtual dimension equals one,
these boundary strata are given by product moduli spaces of maps whose domains are smooth.
In the second case where
w_{-,1}=w=-1
, there are some boundary strata of
\bar{\mathcal{R}}_{2;(-1,-1)}^{1,l;r,s}(\mathbf{x}^{+,1},x,\mathbf{x}^{+,2},x_{-,2},\mathbf{x}^{\prime},x_{-,1})

consisting of moduli spaces of broken popsicles with special weight conditions, to be described below.

Consider a boundary-punctured Riemann surface isomorphic to a disk with
k+l+2
boundary punctures.
Choose a Lagrangian label

K_{k},\ldots,K_{0},L_{0},\ldots,L_{l},

ordered counterclockwise along the boundary of a disk,
where we think of the Lagrangian
K_{k}
as being assigned to the boundary component between the
0
-th and the first punctures.
Let
x_{i}
be a time-one
H
-chord from
K_{i}
to
K_{i-1}
,

x^{\prime}_{j}
a time-one
H
-chord from
L_{j-1}
to
L_{j}
,

y
a time-one
H
-chord from
L_{0}
to
K_{0}
,

z
a time-one
H
-chord from
L_{l}
to
K_{k}
.
Consider the moduli space of popsicles

(3.85)

\mathcal{R}^{k+l+2,\varnothing,\mathbf{w}_{-,k,l}}(z,\mathbf{x},y,\mathbf{x}^{\prime}),

where the popsicle structure has no sprinkles, i.e. the empty map
\varnothing
,
and the weights are

(3.86)

\mathbf{w}_{-,k,l}=(-1,\underbrace{0,\ldots,0}_{k\text{ times }},-1,\underbrace{0,\ldots,0}_{l\text{ times }}).

This moduli space has virtual dimension

(3.87)

\dim\mathcal{R}^{k+l+2,\varnothing,\mathbf{w}_{-,k,l}}(z,\mathbf{x},y,\mathbf{x}^{\prime})=\deg(y)-\deg(z)-\sum_{i=1}^{k}\deg(x_{i})-\sum_{j=1}^{l}\deg(x^{\prime}_{j})+k+l-1.

Now we can give a description of the boundary strata of
\bar{\mathcal{R}}_{2;(-1,-1)}^{1,l;r,s}(\mathbf{x}^{+,1},x,\mathbf{x}^{+,2},x_{-,2},\mathbf{x}^{\prime},x_{-,1})
,
while leaving the description of the boundary strata of
\bar{\mathcal{R}}_{2;(0,0)}^{1,l;r,s}(\mathbf{x}^{+,1},x,\mathbf{x}^{+,2},x_{-,2},\mathbf{x}^{\prime},x_{-,1})
as an exercise.
The special case
r=0
plays an important role in the proof of Proposition
5.20
.

Lemma 3.11
.

Suppose the virtual dimension of the moduli space

\mathcal{R}_{2;(-1,-1)}^{1,l;r,s}(\mathbf{x}^{+,1},x,\mathbf{x}^{+,2},x_{-,2},\mathbf{x}^{\prime},x_{-,1})

is equal to one,
i.e. when (
3.82
) is equal to one.
Then there exists a universal and conformally consistent choice of Floer data compactible with the chosen Floer data for all
\bar{\mathcal{R}}^{d+1,\mathbf{p},\mathbf{w}}
, such that the Gromov compactification

\bar{\mathcal{R}}_{2;(-1,-1)}^{1,l;r,s}(\mathbf{x}^{+,1},x,\mathbf{x}^{+,2},x_{-,2},\mathbf{x}^{\prime},x_{-,1})

is a compact smooth one-dimensional manifold with boundary,
and its boundary strata consist of product moduli spaces of the following kinds

(3.88)

\begin{split}&\partial\bar{\mathcal{R}}_{2;(-1,-1)}^{1,l;r,s}(\mathbf{x}^{+,1},x,\mathbf{x}^{+,2},x_{-,2},\mathbf{x}^{\prime},x_{-,1})\\
\cong&\coprod\mathcal{R}_{2;(-1,-1)}^{1,l-l^{\prime}+1;r,s}(\mathbf{x}^{+,1},x,\mathbf{x}^{+,2},x_{-,2},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{h,l^{\prime},I},x_{-,1})\times\mathcal{R}^{l^{\prime}+1}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{h,l^{\prime},II})\\
\cup&\coprod\mathcal{R}_{2;(-1,-1)}^{1,l;r,s-s^{\prime}+1}(\mathbf{x}^{+,1},x,\mathbf{x}^{+,2}_{j,s,I},x_{-,2},\mathbf{x}^{\prime},x_{-,1})\times\mathcal{R}^{s^{\prime}+1}(\mathbf{x}^{+,2}_{j,s,II})\\
\cup&\coprod\mathcal{R}_{2;(-1,-1)}^{1,l;r-r^{\prime}+1,s}(\mathbf{x}^{+,1}_{i,r^{\prime},I},x,\mathbf{x}^{+,2},x_{-,2},\mathbf{x}^{\prime},x_{-,1})\times\mathcal{R}^{r^{\prime}+1}(\mathbf{x}^{+,1}_{i,r^{\prime},II})\\
\cup&\coprod\mathcal{R}_{2;(-1,-1)}^{1,l_{1};r_{1},s}(\mathbf{x}^{+,1}_{r_{1},II},x,\mathbf{x}^{+,2},x_{-,2},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{l_{1},II},\tilde{x}_{-,1})\times\mathcal{R}^{r_{2}+l_{2}+2,\varnothing,\mathbf{w}_{-}}(x_{-,1},\mathbf{x}^{+,1}_{r_{2},II},\tilde{x}_{-,1},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{l_{2},I})\\
\cup&\coprod\mathcal{R}^{1,l_{1};r;s}_{2;(-1,-1)}(\mathbf{x}^{+,1},x,\mathbf{x}^{+,2},\tilde{x}_{-,2},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{l_{1},I},x_{-,1})\times\mathcal{R}^{l_{2}+2}(x_{-,2},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{l_{1},II},\tilde{x}_{-,2})\\
\cup&\coprod\mathcal{R}^{r+l_{1}+s_{1}+2,\varnothing,\mathbf{w}_{-,r,s_{1}+l_{1}+1}}(x_{-,1},\mathbf{x}^{+,1},x,\mathbf{x}^{+,2}_{s_{1},I},\tilde{x}_{-,2},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{l_{1},I})\times\mathcal{R}_{2;(0,0)}^{1,l_{2};s_{2}-1,0}(\mathbf{x}^{+,2}_{s_{1},II},x_{-,2},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{l_{1},II},\tilde{x}_{-,2})\\
\cup&\coprod\mathcal{R}^{r_{2}+s_{2}+2,\varnothing,\mathbf{w}_{-,r_{2},s_{2}}}(\tilde{x},\mathbf{x}^{+,1}_{r_{2},I},x,\mathbf{x}^{+,2}_{s_{2},I})\times\mathcal{R}_{2;(-1,-1)}^{1,l;r_{1},s_{1}}(\mathbf{x}^{+,1}_{r_{1},II},\tilde{x},\mathbf{x}^{+,2}_{s_{1},II},x_{-,2},\mathbf{x}^{\prime},x_{-,1}).\end{split}

where

(3.89)

\displaystyle\mathbf{x}^{+,1}_{i,r^{\prime},I}

\displaystyle=(x^{+,1}_{r},\ldots,x^{+,1}_{i+r^{\prime}+1},x^{+,1}_{new},x^{+,1}_{i},\ldots,x^{+,1}_{1}),

(3.90)

\displaystyle\mathbf{x}^{+,1}_{i,r^{\prime},II}

\displaystyle=(x^{+,1}_{new},x^{+,1}_{i+1},\ldots,x^{+,1}_{i+r^{\prime}}),

(3.91)

\displaystyle\mathbf{x}^{+,2}_{j,s,I}

\displaystyle=(x^{+,2}_{1},\ldots,x^{+,2}_{j},x^{+,2}_{new},x^{+,2}_{j+s^{\prime}+1},\ldots,x^{+,2}_{s}),

(3.92)

\displaystyle\mathbf{x}^{+,2}_{j,s,II}

\displaystyle=(x^{\prime}_{new},x^{\prime}_{j+s^{\prime}},\ldots,x^{\prime}_{j+1}),

(3.93)

\displaystyle\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{h,l^{\prime},I}

\displaystyle=(x^{\prime}_{l},\ldots,x^{\prime}_{h+l^{\prime}+1},x^{\prime}_{new},x^{\prime}_{h},\ldots,x^{\prime}_{1}),

(3.94)

\displaystyle\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{h,l^{\prime},II}

\displaystyle=(x^{\prime}_{new},x^{\prime}_{h+l^{\prime}},\ldots,x^{\prime}_{h+1}),

(3.95)

\displaystyle\mathbf{x}^{+,1}_{i,I}

\displaystyle=(x^{+,1}_{i},\ldots,x^{+,1}_{1}),

(3.96)

\displaystyle\mathbf{x}^{+,1}_{i,II}

\displaystyle=(x^{+,1}_{r},\ldots,x^{+,1}_{{i}+1}),

(3.97)

\displaystyle\mathbf{x}^{+,2}_{j,I}

\displaystyle=(x^{+,2}_{1},\ldots,x^{+,2}_{j}),

(3.98)

\displaystyle\mathbf{x}^{+,2}_{j,II}

\displaystyle=(x^{+,2}_{j+1},\ldots,x^{+,2}_{s}),

(3.99)

\displaystyle\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{h,I}

\displaystyle=(x^{\prime}_{h},\ldots,x^{\prime}_{1}),

(3.100)

\displaystyle\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{h,II}

\displaystyle=(x^{\prime}_{l},\ldots,x^{\prime}_{h+1}).

Proof.

Smoothness follows by a standard transversality and gluing argument, similar to Proposition
3.6
.

The description of the boundary strata is a consequence of domain degenerations listed in (
3.67
) - (
3.72
) as well as strip breaking,
with further considerations on choices of Floer data compatible with strip-like ends and gluing.
The possible limit broken popsicles can be classified depending on the positions of the three special punctures
z,z_{-,1},z_{-,2}
on the nodal disk:

(i)

The three special punctures are all on the same disk component.
There are indeed three such possible configurations,
which are listed in the first three types on the right hand side of (
3.88
).

(ii)

z_{-,1}
alone is on one disk component, while
z_{-,2}
and
z
are on the other disk component.
The limit broken popsicles of such kind belong to the product moduli spaces of the fourth type.

(iii)

z_{-,2}
alone is on one disk component, while
z
and
z_{-,1}
are on the other disk component.
The limit broken popsicles of such kind belong to the product moduli spaces of either the fifth or sixth type.
These two sub-cases depend on the position of the last puncture
z^{+,2}_{s}
(when
s>0
) in the collection
\mathbf{z}^{+,2}
,
in a way such that the strip-like ends for the node are determined by the relative position of
z^{+,2}_{s}
to the node subject to the rule that the negative strip-end should be on the same disk component as
z^{+,2}_{s}
(this is possible by one particular choice of Floer data).
These two sub-cases are therefore:

(a)

s_{1}=s
.
In this case, the node is an output for the top disk component,
so that the top disk component is still of type
\mathcal{R}^{1,l_{1};r,s}_{2;(-1,-1)}
,
while the bottom disk component is an ordinary
A_{\infty}
-disk of type
\mathcal{R}^{l_{2}+2}
.

(b)

s_{1}<s
.
In this case, the node is an output for the bottom disk component.
Now the top disk component has one distinguished input
z
and output
z_{-,1}
,
while the bottom disk component has one distinguished input
z^{+,2}_{s}
and two distinguished outputs (
z_{-,2}
and the node) both carrying weight
0
,
and is therefore of type
\mathcal{R}_{2;(0,0)}^{1,l_{2};s_{2}-1,0}
.

(iv)

z
alone is on one disk component, while
z_{-,1}
and
z_{-,2}
are on the other disk component.
The limit broken popsicles of such kind belong to the product moduli spaces of the last type.

See Figure
4
for pictures of representatives of elements in each of these strata in (
3.88
).
∎

Figure 4.
Broken popsicles in the boundary strata of
\bar{\mathcal{R}}_{2;(-1,-1)}^{1,l;r,s}(\mathbf{x}^{+,1},x,\mathbf{x}^{+,2},x_{-,2},\mathbf{x}^{\prime},x_{-,1})
: the arrows indicate the distinguished input and outputs, some of which carry weight
-1
; all the other inputs carry weight
0

3.8.
A degeneration argument

The purpose of this subsection is to provide the gluing argument establishing a relationship between two types of moduli spaces of broken disks appearing as boundary of of a third moduli space,
where on one side disks with two outputs are glued to disks with zero output,
and on the other two disks with one input and output are glued.
The gluing argument leaves the framework and specific constraints of popsicles (for which (
3.4
) fails), but in a relatively mild way.
This type of argument is needed in §
5.10
for comparing the composition of a Calabi-Yau type map with two outputs, one of which is negative (defined in §
5.9
)
along with a Poincaré-duality-type pairing map (defined in §
5.6
using the disks introduced previously in §
3.6
),
to a functor from the Rabinowitz Fukaya category (Definition
4.10
),
which is a Yoneda-type map built out of a subcollection of the moduli spaces of popsicle maps defined in §
3.5
.

We will encounter a new phenomenon when we try to glue disks with two inputs (introduced in §
3.6
) and disks with two outputs (introduced in §
3.7
).
Consider the situation where we want to glue an inhomogeneous pseudoholomorphic disk
u:S_{p}^{k,l}\to X
with only inputs introduced in subsection
3.6
,
with an inhomogeneous pseudolomorphic disk
v:S_{2}^{1,l;r,s}\to X
with two outputs introduced in subsection
3.7
,
in a way such that the output of
v
at the puncture
z_{-,1}
is glued to the input of
u
at the puncture
z_{-}
.
The outcome is a one-parameter family of certain smooth disks with exactly one output.

For our purpose, we shall consider a specific situation with certain number of punctures on the boundary of each of the two disks.
To be more specific, consider the product moduli space

(3.101)

\mathcal{R}^{1,l_{1}}_{2;(-1,-1)}(x,x_{-,2},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{II},x_{-,1})\times\mathcal{R}^{l_{2},0}_{p}(\mathbf{x}_{I};x_{-},x_{+})

such that
l_{1}+l_{2}=l
, and
x_{-,1}=x_{-}
,
and the chords
\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{I},\mathbf{x}_{II}
are such that

(3.102)

(\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{II},\mathbf{x}_{I})=\mathbf{x}^{\prime}=(x^{\prime}_{l},\ldots,x^{\prime}_{1})

Here we have changed the notation for the number of punctures in the moduli space
\mathcal{R}^{l_{2},0}_{p}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{2};x_{-},x_{+})
, by letting
k=l_{2}
and
l=0
,
following the notation of (
3.53
).
Correspondingly, we call those Lagrangians
K_{i}
’s now by
L^{\prime}_{i}
’s.

If we glue a broken disk in this moduli space at the identified punctures
z_{-,1}\sim z_{-}
,
we obtain a one-parameter family of maps from smooth domains.
Each such domain is a Riemann surface
S^{1,l}_{1}
isomorphic to a disk with
l+3
boundary punctures

z_{out},z^{\prime}_{l},\ldots,z^{\prime}_{1},z_{+},z,

ordered counterclockwise along the boundary of the disk,
as well as an interior marked point
\zeta
as in the case of
S^{k,l}_{p}
in §
3.6
.
We regard
z_{out}
as a special positive output,

z_{+}
as a special positive input,
z
as a special negative input,
and
z^{\prime}_{l},\ldots,z^{\prime}_{1}
as auxiliary positive inputs.
Up to automorphism, the three points
z_{+},z,z_{out}
can be placed at
i,-1,-i
respectively.
The other points
z^{\prime}_{l},\ldots,z^{\prime}_{l}
can vary freely between
z_{+}
and
z_{out}
.
The moduli space of such domains is denoted by

(3.103)

\tilde{\mathcal{R}}_{1}^{l+3},

inside which there is a codimension-one subspace cut out by constraining the interior marked point
\zeta
to lie on a chosen geodesic connecting
z_{+}
to
z_{out}
;
denote that subspace by

(3.104)

\mathcal{R}_{1}^{l+3}.

There is a natural compactification

(3.105)

\bar{\mathcal{R}}_{1}^{l+3}

such that the boundary strata are given by product moduli spaces of various types, including
\bar{\mathcal{R}}^{d+1,\mathbf{p},\mathbf{w}},\bar{\mathcal{R}}_{p}^{l^{\prime},0}
and
\bar{\mathcal{R}}^{1,l^{\prime}}_{2;(-1,-1)}
for appropriate indices
d,l^{\prime}
.

We can define a Floer datum for the domain
S^{1,l}_{1}
in a way similar to Definition
3.2
,
with minor modifications as such a domain (even as a representative of an element in the subspace
\mathcal{R}_{1}^{l+3}
) is strictly speaking not a weighted popsicle (because (
3.4
) fails).
The only matter that we need to take care of here is the rule for assigning strip-like ends, which should obey the following rule:

•

positive strip-like ends
\epsilon:Z^{+}\to S^{1,l}_{1}
near positive inputs, which are
z^{\prime}_{l},\ldots,z^{\prime}_{1},z_{+}
;

•

negative strip-like ends
\epsilon:Z^{-}\to S^{1,l}_{1}
near the negative input
z
(which can be thought of as a positive output) and the positive output
z_{out}
.

With this rule of choosing strip-like ends, the rest of Definition
3.2
applies.
Moreover, the definition of a universal and conformally consistent choice of Floer data has a straightforward generalization to such moduli spaces
\mathcal{R}_{1}^{l+3}
, compatible with choices for other types of moduli spaces with respect to gluing,
since the boundary strata of the compactification
\bar{\mathcal{R}}_{1}^{l+3}
involve familiar moduli spaces
\bar{\mathcal{R}}^{d+1,\mathbf{p},\mathbf{w}},\bar{\mathcal{R}}_{p}^{l^{\prime},0}
and
\bar{\mathcal{R}}^{1,l^{\prime}}_{2;(-1,-1)}
on which such choices already exist.
Call this simply a universal and conformally consistent choice of Floer data for all
\bar{\mathcal{R}}_{1}^{l+3}
.

Choose a Lagrangian label that is consistent with the gluing of the two Lagrangian labels for
\mathcal{R}^{1,l_{1}}_{2;(-1,-1)}(x,x_{-,2},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{II},x_{-,1})
and
\mathcal{R}^{l_{2},0}_{p}(\mathbf{x}_{I};x_{-},x_{+})
.
Consider smooth maps

u:S^{1,l}_{1}\to X

that satisfy the inhomogeneous Cauchy-Riemann equation with respect to the chosen Floer data,
with asymptotic conditions given by time-one chords
x_{out}
at
z_{out}
,
x
at
z
,
x^{\prime}_{j}
at
z^{\prime}_{j}
and
x_{+}
at
z_{+}
.
The resulting moduli space is denoted by

(3.106)

\tilde{\mathcal{R}}_{1}^{l+3}(x_{out},\mathbf{x}^{\prime},x_{+},x),

Again, inside of this space there is a subspace of codimension one,
by constraining the interior marked point
\zeta
on the geodesic connecting
z_{+}
to
z_{out}
:

(3.107)

\mathcal{R}_{1}^{l+3}(x_{out},\mathbf{x}^{\prime},x_{+},x).

Now we suppose we have made a universal and conformally consistent choice of Floer data for all
\bar{\mathcal{R}}_{1}^{l+3}
.
Each moduli space admits a natural Gromov compactification

(3.108)

\bar{\mathcal{R}}_{1}^{l+3}(x_{out},\mathbf{x}^{\prime},x_{+},x)

whose codimension-one boundary strata contain not only (
3.101
),
but also some other types of moduli spaces of broken popsicles.
By standard transversality argument, Gromov compactness and maximum principle (similar to the proof of Proposition
3.6
, we can show that the zero-dimensional moduli spaces
\mathcal{R}_{1}^{l+3}(x_{out},\mathbf{x}^{\prime},x_{+},x)
are compact smooth manifolds, and therefore consist of finitely many points.
For one-dimensional moduli spaces, we have:

Lemma 3.12
.

There exists a universal and conformally consistent choice of Floer data
such that when the virtual dimension is one,
\bar{\mathcal{R}}_{1}^{l+3}(x_{out},\mathbf{x}^{\prime},x_{+},x)
is a compact smooth one-dimensional manifold with boundary,
whose codimension-one boundary strata consist of the following union of product moduli spaces:

(3.109)

\begin{split}&\partial\bar{\mathcal{R}}_{1}^{l+3}(x_{out},\mathbf{x}^{\prime},x_{+},x)\\
\cong&\coprod\mathcal{R}^{2,\varnothing,(-1,-1)}(\tilde{x},x)\times\mathcal{R}_{1}^{l+3}(x_{out},\mathbf{x}^{\prime},x_{+},\tilde{x})\\
\cup&\coprod\mathcal{R}^{l^{\prime}+1}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{h,l^{\prime},II})\times\mathcal{R}_{1}^{l-l^{\prime}+4}(x_{out},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{h,l^{\prime},I},x_{+},x)\\
\cup&\coprod\mathcal{R}_{1}^{l_{1}+3}(\tilde{x}_{out},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{l_{1},I},x_{+},x)\times\mathcal{R}^{l_{2}+2}(x_{out},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{l_{1},II},\tilde{x}_{out})\\
\cup&\coprod\mathcal{R}^{1,l_{1}}_{2;(-1,-1)}(x,x_{out},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{II},x_{-,1})\times\mathcal{R}^{l_{2},0}_{p}(\mathbf{x}_{I};x_{-,1},x_{+})\\
\cup&\coprod\mathcal{R}^{l_{1}+3,\mathbf{p}_{I},\mathbf{w}_{I}}(y,\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{l_{1},I},x_{+},x)\times\mathcal{R}^{l_{2}+2,\mathbf{p}_{II},\mathbf{w}_{II}}(x_{out},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{l_{1},II},y)\\
\cup&\coprod\mathcal{R}^{l_{2}+2}(\tilde{x}_{+},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{l_{2},I},x_{+})\times\mathcal{R}_{1}^{l_{1}+3}(x_{out},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{l_{2},II},\tilde{x}_{+},x)\end{split}

where the disjoint unions are taken over all possible indices and intermediate chords,
such that all the relevant moduli spaces on the right hand side are zero-dimensional.
In the last line, the moduli spaces are moduli spaces of popsicles,
where the popsicle structures
\sigma_{I},\sigma_{II}
of flavors
\mathbf{p}_{I},\mathbf{p}_{II}
and weights
\mathbf{w}_{I},\mathbf{w}_{II}
are described as follows.
For the weights, we have

(3.110)

\displaystyle\mathbf{w}_{I}

\displaystyle=(-1,\underbrace{0,\ldots,0}_{l_{1}+1\text{ times }},-1),

(3.111)

\displaystyle\mathbf{w}_{II}

\displaystyle=(0,\underbrace{0,\ldots,0}_{l_{2}\text{ times}},-1).

Then
\sigma_{I}
is a trivial popsicle structure,
and
\sigma_{II}
is the choice of one sprinkle on the geodesic connecting the
(l_{2}+1)
-th puncture to the
0
-th puncture.

Proof.

As usual, the compactification is a result of domain degenerations as well as strip breaking.

(i)

The first case is a strip breaking at the puncture
z
;
the resulting broken disks belong to the first type on the right hand side of (
3.109
).

(ii)

If strip breaking occurs at a puncture
z^{\prime}_{j},j=1,\ldots,l
, or domain degeneration occurs along some boundary component between the
z^{\prime}_{l},\ldots,z^{\prime}_{1}
punctures,
the resulting broken disks represent elements in the second kind of product moduli space in (
3.109
).
The top right broken disk in Figure
5
provides a schematic picture for both cases.

(iii)

The remaining cases occur due to domain degenerations such that the input and output
z_{+},z_{out}
are located on different disk components,
and are classified by different behaviors of the interior marked point
\zeta
.
Suppose
\zeta
goes to the first component where
z_{+}
is located.
There are two different sub-cases, further depending on the position of the special negative input
z
,
in a way that the node should always be an output for the disk component on which
z
lies.
The weight assigned to the node is uniquely determined by the rule,
which is
0
if
z
is on the top disk component (containing
\zeta
), and
-1
otherwise.
Such broken disks represent elements in the third (where the weight at the node
=0
) and the fourth (where the weight at the node
=-1
) types on the right hand side of (
3.109
).
The pictures for these two cases are the middle two broken disks in Figure
5
.

(iv)

Suppose
\zeta
goes to the second component where
z_{out}
is located.
Again, there are two sub-cases depending on the position of the special negative input
z
,
in a way that the node is an input for the disk component containing
\zeta
.
The weight assigned to the node is determined by the same rule as in (iii).
Such broken disks represent elements in the fifth (where the weight at node
=0
) and sixth (where the weight at node
=-1
) types on the right hand side of (
3.109
).
See the last two broken disks in Figure
5
.

∎

Figure 5.
A representative in
\mathcal{R}_{1}^{l+3}
, and broken disks in the boundary strata of
\bar{\mathcal{R}}_{1}^{l+3}
; the arrows indicate input vs. output

4.
The Rabinowitz wrapped Fukaya category

The goal of this section is to define the Rabinowitz Fukaya category, reviewing and fixing conventions for (wrapped) Lagrangian Floer theory along the way.
First we recall the definition of the wrapped Floer complex using a quadratic Hamiltonian, and the
A_{\infty}
-structure it possesses,
following the framework of
[
abouzaid1
,
ganatra
]
.
Then we introduce the Rabinowitz Floer complex and construct on it an
A_{\infty}
-structure.
Our construction of these
A_{\infty}
-operations uses the popsicle-moduli spaces of §
3
and particularly §
3.5
as a means of geometrically defining operations on mapping cones, following
[
abouzaidseidel
,
seidel6
]
adapted here to the setting of wrapped Floer theory.

4.1.
The wrapped Fukaya category

Fix an admissible Hamiltonian
H\in\mathcal{H}(M)
and define

\chi(L_{0},L_{1})=\chi(L_{0},L_{1};H)

to be the set of all time-one chords for
H
from admissible Lagrangians
L_{0}
to
L_{1}
.
The choice of grading on
L_{0}
and
L_{1}
gives each
x
a well-defined Maslov index, which we will denote by
\deg(x)
.
To each chord
x\in\mathcal{X}(L_{0},L_{1};H)
, denote by
o_{x}
its orientation line,
and
|o_{x}|_{{\mathbf{k}}}
the associated
{\mathbf{k}}
-normalized orientation space.
The
wrapped Floer cochain space
over
{\mathbf{k}}
is defined to be, as a vector space,

(4.1)

CW^{i}(L_{0},L_{1};H)=\bigoplus_{x\in\chi(L_{0},L_{1}),\deg(x)=i}|o_{x}|_{{\mathbf{k}}}.

There is a canonical basis, corresponding to the chord
x
,
and we will denote each such element by
[x]
.

Consider the moduli space of maps

\mathcal{R}^{d+1}(\mathbf{x})

defined in (
3.30
), where the degrees satisfy

(4.2)

\deg(x_{0})-\deg(x_{1})-\cdots-\deg(x_{d})=2-d,

so that all the elements are rigid for generic choices of Floer data.
Each rigid element
u\in\mathcal{R}^{d+1}(\mathbf{x})
induces an isomorphism

\mathcal{R}^{d+1}_{u}:o_{x_{d}}\otimes\cdots\otimes o_{x_{1}}\to o_{x_{0}},

thus also an isomorphism of
{\mathbf{k}}
-normalized orientation space,

\mathcal{R}^{d+1}_{u}:|o_{x_{d}}|_{{\mathbf{k}}}\otimes\cdots\otimes|o_{x_{1}}|_{{\mathbf{k}}}\to|o_{x_{0}}|_{{\mathbf{k}}},

Define a map of degree
2-d

(4.3)

\begin{split}\mu^{d}:&CW^{*}(L_{d-1},L_{d};H)\otimes\cdots\otimes CW^{*}(L_{0},L_{1};H)\to CW^{*}(L_{0},L_{d};H)[2-d]\\
\mu^{d}([x_{d}]\otimes\cdots\otimes[x_{1}])&=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x_{0}\\
\deg(x_{0})=\deg(x_{1})+\cdots+\deg(x_{d})+2-d\end{subarray}}\sum_{u\in\mathcal{R}^{d+1}(\mathbf{x})}(-1)^{*_{d}}\mathcal{R}^{d+1}_{u}([x_{d}]\otimes\cdots\otimes[x_{1}]),\end{split}

where

(4.4)

*_{d}=\sum_{j=1}^{d}j\deg(x_{j}).

The fact that this map is well-defined follows from Proposition
3.6
plus an action estimate (see e.g.,
[
abouzaid1
]
*§B) which guarantees that for a given
x_{1},\ldots,x_{d}
there are only finitely many
x_{0}
for which
\mathcal{R}^{d+1}(\mathbf{x})
are non-empty. The same action estimate tells us that there are only finitely many
x_{0}
for which the one-dimensional components of the compactified moduli spaces
\bar{\mathcal{R}}^{d+1}(\mathbf{x})
are non-empty, and by looking at the codimension-one boundary strata of these compact one-dimensional moduli spaces Proposition
3.6
implies that

Lemma 4.1
.

The maps
\mu^{d}
satisfy the
A_{\infty}
-relations. ∎

In particular,
\mu^{1}
is a differential; call the resulting cohomology group the
wrapped Floer cohomology
, and denote it by
HW^{*}(L_{0},L_{1})
, which turns out to be independent of choices of
H
and
J_{t}
by a standard continuation argument.

Fix an at most countable collection
\mathbf{L}
of admissible exact Lagrangians that are cylindrical at infinity,
such that (
3.18
) holds for these Lagrangians.
The
wrapped Fukaya category

\mathcal{W}=\mathcal{W}(\mathbf{L})
of
(X,\lambda)
is the
A_{\infty}
-category with objects in
\mathbf{L}
,
morphism spaces

(4.5)

\mathcal{W}(L_{0},L_{1})=\hom_{\mathcal{W}}(L_{0},L_{1}):=CW^{*}(L_{0},L_{1};H),

and
A_{\infty}
-structure maps given by
\mu^{d}
(
4.3
).
In case where no confusion may occur, we refer to this also as the wrapped Fukaya category of
X
, and denote it simply by
\mathcal{W}=\mathcal{W}(X)
.

4.2.
The Rabinowitz Floer complex

Consider the Hamiltonian
-H
on
X
.
It is not an admissible Hamiltonian for the definition of the wrapped Fukaya category,
but we can still define a Floer cochain space for this Hamiltonian.
There is a natural one-to-one correspondence between
H
-chords from
L_{1}
to
L_{0}
of Maslov index
n-i
and
(-H)
-chords from
L_{0}
to
L_{1}
of Maslov index
i
.
For each
(-H)
-chord
x\in\chi(L_{0},L_{1};-H)
,
let
\bar{x}\in\chi(L_{1},L_{0};H)
be the corresponding
H
-chord,
and let
o^{-}_{\bar{x}}
be the negative orientation line for the chord
\bar{x}
,
such that there is a canonical isomorphism
o_{x}\cong o^{-}_{\bar{x}}
.
We define

(4.6)

CW^{i}(L_{0},L_{1};-H)=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in\chi(L_{0},L_{1};-H)\\
\deg(x)=i\end{subarray}}|o_{x}|_{{\mathbf{k}}}=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\bar{x}\in\chi(L_{1},L_{0};H)\\
\deg(\bar{x})=n-i\end{subarray}}|o^{-}_{\bar{x}}|_{{\mathbf{k}}}.

Here the direct product can be understood to be the completion of the direct sum with respect to the action filtration:
The action spectrum of
(-H)
-chord is a discrete subset of
{\mathbb{R}}
, which is bounded below but goes off to
+\infty
(compared to the case of
H
-chords),
and we allow infinite sums as long as the generators are corresponding to chords with action
\to+\infty
.
In the
{\mathbf{k}}
-normalized orientation space
|o^{-}_{\bar{x}}|_{{\mathbf{k}}}
for the negative orientation line
o^{-}_{\bar{x}}
,
there is a canonical generator, denoted by
[\bar{x}]^{-}
, corresponding to each chord
\bar{x}
.

When the Lagrangians are oriented, there is a Poincaré duality isomorphism

(4.7)

\bar{I}:CW^{*}(L_{0},L_{1};-H)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\to}}\hom_{{\mathbf{k}}}(CW^{n-*}(L_{1},L_{0};H),{\mathbf{k}}),

induced by pairings
o^{-}_{\bar{x}}\otimes o_{\bar{x}}\to\Lambda^{top}T_{p}L_{0}\cong{\mathbf{k}}
for some point
p=x(0)\in L_{0}
,
where the determinant line of
TL_{0}
is canonically isomorphic to the trivial bundle if and only if
L_{0}
is oriented, and similarly for
L_{1}
.
Note that the linear dual of the direct sum (
4.1
) is naturally identified with a direct product with the dual basis.

We could define the Floer differential in the usual way,
by counting rigid elements in moduli spaces of inhomogeneous pseudoholomorphic strips similar to
\mathcal{R}^{2}(x_{0},x_{1})
,
with
H
replaced by
-H
in the Floer’s equation.
Alternatively, we think of such strips as popsicles with weights
w_{0}=w_{1}=-1
,
which carry no sprinkles.
Each rigid element
u\in\mathcal{R}^{2,\varnothing,(-1,-1)}(x_{0},x_{1})
induces an isomorphism of negative orientation lines:

(4.8)

\mathcal{R}^{2,\varnothing,(-1,-1)}_{u}:o^{-}_{x_{1}}\to o^{-}_{x_{0}}.

Recall that
x_{0},x_{1}
are by convention time-one
H
-chords from
L_{1}
to
L_{0}
,
which naturally corresponds to unique time-one
(-H)
-chords from
L_{0}
to
L_{1}
of complementary degrees
n-*
.
For notational convenience, denote by
x_{0}^{-},x_{1}^{-}
the corresponding
(-H)
-chords,
such that under the canonical isomorphism
o_{x_{j}}^{-}\cong o_{x_{j}^{-}}
,
the induced isomorphism on the normalized orientation space is given by

(4.9)

\begin{split}|o_{x_{j}}^{-}|_{{\mathbf{k}}}&\to|o_{x_{j}^{-}}|_{{\mathbf{k}}},\\
[o_{x_{j}}]^{-}&\mapsto[o_{x_{j}}^{-}].\end{split}

Summing over all rigid elements in
\mathcal{R}^{2,\varnothing,(-1,-1)}(x_{0},x_{1})
,
and taking the direct product over all possible
x_{0}
, we define a map

(4.10)

d_{-}:CW^{*}(L_{0},L_{1};-H)\to CW^{*}(L_{0},L_{1};-H)[1]

which on a basis element is given by

(4.11)

\begin{split}d_{-}([x_{1}^{-}])&=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}x_{0}\\
\deg(x_{1})-\deg(x_{0})=1\end{subarray}}\sum_{u\in\mathcal{R}^{2,(-1,-1)}(x_{0},x_{1})}(-1)^{n-\deg(x_{1})}\mathcal{R}^{2,\varnothing,(-1,-1)}_{u}([x_{1}^{-}])\\
&=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}x_{0}^{-}\\
\deg(x_{0}^{-})-\deg(x_{1}^{-})=1\end{subarray}}\sum_{u\in\mathcal{R}^{2,(-1,-1)}(x_{0},x_{1})}(-1)^{\deg(x_{1}^{-})}\mathcal{R}^{2,\varnothing,(-1,-1)}_{u}([x_{1}^{-}]).\end{split}

Lemma 4.2
.

d_{-}
is well-defined on the whole of
CW^{*}(L_{0},L_{1};-H)
, i.e. on the direct product as in (
4.6
).

Proof.

Observe that the Floer differential increases the action of chords,
which means that for every
[x_{0}^{-}]
appearing as an output for
[x_{1}^{-}]
, the corresponding
(-H)
-chords
x_{0}^{-}
and
x_{1}^{-}
satisfy

(4.12)

\mathcal{A}_{-H}(x_{0}^{-})\geq\mathcal{A}_{-H}(x_{1}^{-}).

Order the elements in the natural basis for
CW^{*}(L_{0},L_{1};-H)
according to an increasing order on the action of the corresponding chords.
With respect to that basis, the operation
d_{-}
can be written as an lower triangular matrix,
which in particular implies that for any basis vector
v_{i}
, there can be at most finitely many other basis vectors that are mapped to
v_{i}
.
It follows that the operation
d_{-}
defined on basis as in (
4.11
) can be extended to the full operation
d_{-}
on the direct product (
4.6
).
∎

When the Lagrangians are oriented,
we can alternatively define the differential
d_{-}
by first defining a differential
\partial
of degree
-1
:

(4.13)

\partial:CW^{*}(L_{1},L_{0};H)\to CW^{*}(L_{1},L_{0};H)[-1]

by counting inhomogeneous pseudoholomorphic strips with input and output interchanged,
and putting

(4.14)

d_{-}=\bar{I}^{-1}\circ\partial^{*}\circ\bar{I},

where

\partial^{*}:\hom_{{\mathbf{k}}}(CW^{n-*}(L_{1},L_{0};H),{\mathbf{k}})\to\hom_{{\mathbf{k}}}(CW^{n-*}(L_{1},L_{0};H),{\mathbf{k}})[1]

is the linear dual of
\partial
. Defining
d_{-}
this way requires the almost complex structure chosen for
-H
to agree with that previously chosen for
H
; however, for the direct definition of
d_{-}
the almost complex structures need not coincide.

There is a canonical
continuation map

(4.15)

c:CW^{*}(L_{0},L_{1};-H)\to CW^{*}(L_{0},L_{1};H)

given by an increasing homotopy from
-H
to
H
.
Below we give a definition suited to our analytic framework using popsicles.
Consider the following moduli space

\mathcal{R}^{2,\mathbf{p},(0,-1)}(x_{0},x_{1})

with weights
w_{0}=0,w_{1}=-1
,
where the popsicle structure of flavor
\mathbf{p}
is a choice of a sprinkle on the geodesic connecting
z_{1}
to
z_{0}
.
Each rigid element
u\in\mathcal{R}^{2,\mathbf{p},(0,-1)}(x_{0},x_{1})
induces an isomorphism

\mathcal{R}^{2,\mathbf{p},(0,-1)}_{u}:o_{x_{1}}^{-}\to o_{x_{0}},

and thus also an isomorphism of
{\mathbf{k}}
-normalized orientation spaces following the identification (
4.9
),

\mathcal{R}^{2,\mathbf{p},(0,-1)}_{u}:|o_{x_{1}}^{-}|_{{\mathbf{k}}}\to|o_{x_{0}}|_{{\mathbf{k}}},

Summing over all rigid elements, and taking the direct product, we define

(4.16)

c([x_{1}^{-}])=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}x_{0}\\
\deg(x_{0})=n-\deg(x_{1})\end{subarray}}\sum_{u\in\mathcal{R}^{2,\mathbf{p},(0,-1)}(x_{0},x_{1})}\mathcal{R}^{2,\mathbf{p},(0,-1)}_{u}([x_{1}^{-}])

Since the Lagrangians are oriented, we can alternatively define
c
by first defining a
copairing

(4.17)

\hat{c}:{\mathbf{k}}\to CW^{n-*}(L_{1},L_{0};H)\otimes CW^{*}(L_{0},L_{1};H)

and setting

(4.18)

c(\cdot)=(\bar{I}(\cdot)\otimes\mathrm{id})(\hat{c}).

The copairing (
4.17
) is also defined by counting rigid elements in the moduli space of popsicles

\mathcal{R}^{2,\mathbf{p},(0,-1)}(x_{0},x_{1})

Note that by (
3.31
),
the moduli space has virtual dimension

(4.19)

\dim\mathcal{R}^{2,\mathbf{p},(0,-1)}(x_{0},x_{1})=-n+\deg(x_{0})+\deg(x_{1}),

and this is why the copairing (
4.17
) has the desired degree.

Definition 4.3
.

The Rabinowitz Floer cochain space is defined to be the mapping cone of the continuation map (
4.15
)

(4.20)

\begin{split}RC^{*}(L_{0},L_{1})&:=\mathrm{Cone}(c:CW^{*}(L_{0},L_{1};-H)\to CW^{*}(L_{0},L_{1};H))\\
&=CW^{*}(L_{0},L_{1};-H)[1]\oplus CW^{*}(L_{0},L_{1};H)).\end{split}

The differential is the cone differential

(4.21)

\mu^{1}_{RC}=\begin{pmatrix}d_{-}&c[1]\\
0&\mu^{1}\end{pmatrix}

Lemma 4.4
.

(\mu^{1}_{RC})^{2}=0
.

Proof.

The only thing to check is
c\circ d_{-}+\mu^{1}\circ c=0
, which is to say that
c
defined above is a chain map.
This follows by looking at the boundary strata of the one-dimensional moduli spaces
\mathcal{R}^{2,\mathbf{p},(0,-1)}(x_{0},x_{1})
,
where two different types of strata contribute to
c\circ d_{-}
and
\mu^{1}\circ c
,
with the opposite signs.
∎

We denote the cohomology of
\mu^{1}_{RC}
on
RC^{*}(L_{0},L_{1})
by
RW^{*}(L_{0},L_{1})
.

Lemma 4.5
.

If either of
L_{0}
or
L_{1}
is compact, then
RW^{*}(L_{0},L_{1})
is zero.

Sketch.

It is a well known (and a manifestation of Poincaré duality in Floer theory) that if one of
L_{0}
or
L_{1}
is compact, then (
4.15
) induces a quasi-isomorphism between the Floer complexes for
-H
and
H
.
∎

4.3.
The
A_{\infty}
structure on the Rabinowitz Floer complex

The goal of this subsection is to define the
A_{\infty}
-structure on the Rabinowitz Floer cochain complexes (
4.20
), which takes the form of a series of maps

(4.22)

\mu^{d}_{\mathcal{RW}}:RC^{*}(L_{d-1},L_{d})\otimes\cdots\otimes RC^{*}(L_{0},L_{1})\to RC^{*}(L_{0},L_{d}),

extending
\mu^{1}
defined in the previous section, satisfying the
A_{\infty}
equations. Given the form of
RC^{*}(L_{i},L_{j})
as defined in (
4.20
), our final map (
4.22
) will be a suitable sum of maps of the form

CW^{*}(L_{d-1},L_{d};s_{d}H)\otimes\cdots\otimes CW^{*}(L_{0},L_{1};s_{1}H)\to CW^{*}(L_{0},L_{d};s_{0}H)

for each sequence
\{s_{i}\ |\ s_{i}\in\{+,-\}\}
. We first proceed to describe these constituent maps.

Consider various kinds of moduli spaces of popsicles
\mathcal{R}^{d+1,\mathbf{p},\mathbf{w}}(\mathbf{x})
as defined in §
3.5
with weights satisfying

(4.23)

w_{j}\in\{-1,0\},j=0,\ldots,d.

Suppose we have made a universal and conformally consistent choice of Floer data for all weighted popsicles with weights
\leq 0
to construct the moduli spaces
\mathcal{R}^{d+1,\mathbf{p},\mathbf{w}}(\mathbf{x})
.
In addition, suppose Floer data are chosen generically such that all the zero and one dimensional components of the moduli spaces are regular.
Now suppose
w_{j}\in\{-1,0\}
, and

d-2+|F|+n(1+\sum_{j=0}^{d}\delta_{j})-\sum_{j=0}^{d}(-1)^{\delta_{j}}\deg(x_{j})=0,

where the
\delta_{j}
’s are defined in (
3.8
).
The moduli space
\mathcal{R}^{d+1,\mathbf{p},\mathbf{w}}(\mathbf{x})
is a compact smooth manifold of dimension zero.
Each element
u\in\mathcal{R}^{d+1,\mathbf{p},\mathbf{w}}(\mathbf{x})
is rigid and induces an isomorphism

(4.24)

\mathcal{R}^{d+1,\mathbf{p},\mathbf{w}}_{u}:\bigotimes_{j=1}^{k}o_{x_{j}}^{s_{j}}\to o_{x_{0}}^{s_{0}},

where the signs
s_{j}\in\{+,-\}
’s are defined in (
3.9
).
Its induced map on normalized orientation space is

(4.25)

\mathcal{R}^{d+1,\mathbf{p},\mathbf{w}}_{u}:\bigotimes_{j=1}^{k}|o_{x_{j}}^{s_{j}}|_{{\mathbf{k}}}\to|o_{x_{0}}^{s_{0}}|_{{\mathbf{k}}}.

Equivalently, using negative orientation lines, we may give an alternative description of the isomorphism (
4.24
).
Define inputs

(4.26)

\mathbf{x}_{in}=(x_{j})_{j:\delta_{j}=0},

and outputs

(4.27)

\mathbf{x}_{out}=(x_{j})_{j:\delta_{j}=-1},

where
j
varies in
0,1,\ldots,d
.
These are understood to be associated to the given collection of weights
\mathbf{w}
in
\{-1,0\}
as well as the popsicle flavor
\mathbf{p}
.
With these notations, we may rewrite (
4.24
) as

(4.28)

\mathcal{R}^{d+1,\mathbf{p},\mathbf{w}}_{u}:o_{\mathbf{x}_{in}}:=\bigotimes_{j:\delta_{j}=0}o_{x_{j}}\to o_{\mathbf{x}_{out}}:=\bigotimes_{j:\delta_{j}=-1}o_{x_{j}}.

Remark 4.6
.

Passing from (
4.24
) to (
4.28
) involves a lot of changes in the order of the tensor factors of the orientation lines appearing in (
4.24
),
as well as choices of trivializations of determinant lines for the tangent bundles of several Lagrangians,
which will result in lots of sign changes which are too tedious to keep track of.
Thus, when we actually define
A_{\infty}
-operations, we will only use (
4.24
) for a rigorous definition with the correct signs,
while refer to (
4.28
) as a geometric interpretation, without specifying the signs.

Summing over all rigid elements
u\in\mathcal{R}^{d+1,\mathbf{p},\mathbf{w}}(\mathbf{x})
,
and taking the direct product over all possible
x_{0}
,
we obtain a map of degree
2-d+|F|
of the following form

(4.29)

\mu^{d,\mathbf{p},\mathbf{w}}:CW^{*}(L_{d-1},L_{d};s_{d}H)\otimes\cdots\otimes CW^{*}(L_{0},L_{1};s_{1}H)\to CW^{*}(L_{0},L_{d};s_{0}H),

which on basis elements is given by

(4.30)

\begin{split}&\mu^{d,\mathbf{p},\mathbf{w}}([x_{d}^{s_{d}}]\otimes\cdots\otimes[x_{1}^{s_{1}}])\\
=&\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}x_{0}\\
d-2+|F|+n(1+\sum_{j=0}^{d}\delta_{j})-\sum_{j=0}^{d}(-1)^{\delta_{j}}\deg(x_{j})=0\end{subarray}}\sum_{u\in\mathcal{R}^{d+1,\mathbf{p},\mathbf{w}}(\mathbf{x})}(-1)^{*_{d,\mathbf{p},\mathbf{w}}+\Diamond_{d,\mathbf{p},\mathbf{w}}}\mathcal{R}^{d+1,\mathbf{p},\mathbf{w}}_{u}([x_{d}^{s_{d}}]\otimes\cdots\otimes[x_{1}^{s_{1}}]),\end{split}

where the isomorphism
\mathcal{R}^{d+1,\mathbf{p},\mathbf{w}}_{u}
are understood as (
4.24
),
and basis vector
[x_{j}^{s_{j}}]
of the normalized orientation space
|o_{x_{j}}^{s_{j}}|_{{\mathbf{k}}}
for
s_{j}=-
is defined in (
4.9
).
Here the signs
*_{d,\mathbf{p},\mathbf{w}}
are

(4.31)

*_{d,\mathbf{p},\mathbf{w}}=\sum_{j=1}^{d}(j+w_{1}+\cdots+w_{j-1})\deg(x_{j}^{s_{j}})+\sum_{j=1}^{d}(d-j)w_{j},

(4.32)

\Diamond_{d,\mathbf{p},\mathbf{w}}=\sum_{j=1}^{d}|\mathbf{p}^{-1}(\{j+1,\ldots,d\})|(w_{j}+|\mathbf{p}^{-1}(j)|).

Since we have chosen Floer data to be invariant under the action of
Aut(\mathbf{p})
(in §
3.5
), following
[
abouzaidseidel
]
we have the following vanishing result for non-injective
\mathbf{p}
:

Lemma 4.7

(
[
abouzaidseidel
]
Lemma 3.7)
.

If
Aut(\mathbf{p})
is nontrivial, then
\mu^{d,\mathbf{p},\mathbf{w}}([x_{d}^{s_{d}}]\otimes\cdots\otimes[x_{1}^{s_{1}}])
as in (
4.30
) vanishes.

To obtain the desired map (
4.29
), we also need:

Lemma 4.8
.

The operations
\mu^{d,\mathbf{p},\mathbf{w}}
defined on basis elements as in (
4.30
) can be extended to the whole of the tensor product

CW^{*}(L_{d-1},L_{d};s_{d}H)\otimes\cdots\otimes CW^{*}(L_{0},L_{1};s_{1}H)\to CW^{*}(L_{0},L_{d};s_{0}H).

Proof.

Again, this follows by an action argument, similar to Lemma
4.2
.
To spell this out, note that each operation
\mu^{d,\mathbf{p},\mathbf{w}}
(
4.30
) increases the action,

(4.33)

\mathcal{A}_{s_{0}H}(x_{0}^{s_{0}})\geq\sum_{j=1}^{d}\mathcal{A}_{s_{j}H}(x_{j}^{s_{j}}).

The sum of the actions of basis elements can be extended to an action filtration on the algebraic tensor product

CW^{*}(L_{d-1},L_{d};s_{d}H)\otimes\cdots\otimes CW^{*}(L_{0},L_{1};s_{1}H),

which allows us to give an order on the basis vectors, i.e. simple tensors of orientation basis vectors for the corresponding chords, by the sum of actions.
With respect to the ordered basis
\mu^{d,\mathbf{p},\mathbf{w}}
(
4.30
) can be written lower triangular,
which implies that it can be extended to the completed tensor product.
In particular, since the algebraic tensor product embeds into the completed tensor product as a filtered subspace,
we have a well-defined map (
4.29
).
∎

Since the Lagrangians are oriented, we may alternatively define operations

(4.34)

\begin{split}\hat{\mu}^{d,\mathbf{p},\mathbf{w}}:&CW^{*}(L_{d},L_{0};H)^{\otimes(\delta_{0}+1)}\otimes CW^{*}(L_{d-1},L_{d};H)^{\otimes(\delta_{d}+1)}\otimes\cdots\otimes CW^{*}(L_{0},L_{1};H)^{\otimes(\delta_{1}+1)}\\
\to&CW^{*}(L_{0},L_{d};H)^{\otimes-\delta_{0}}\otimes CW^{*}(L_{1},L_{0};H)^{\otimes-\delta_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes CW^{*}(L_{d},L_{d-1};H)^{\otimes-\delta_{d}},\\
\hat{\mu}^{d,\mathbf{p},\mathbf{w}}([\mathbf{x}_{in}])&=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{x}_{out}\\
d-2+|F|+n(1+\sum_{j=0}^{d}\delta_{j})-\sum_{j=0}^{d}(-1)^{\delta_{j}}\deg(x_{j})=0\end{subarray}}\sum_{u\in\mathcal{R}^{d+1,\mathbf{p},\mathbf{w}}(\mathbf{x})}(-1)^{*}\mathcal{R}^{d+1,\mathbf{p},\mathbf{w}}_{u}([\mathbf{x}_{in}]),\end{split}

where the isomorphism
\mathcal{R}^{d+1,\mathbf{p},\mathbf{w}}_{u}
are understood as (
4.28
).
Note that by Remark
4.6
, we do not want to specify the sign here, so this formula only serves an an exposition for geometric meaning of the count and degrees, etc.
The degree of this map is

(4.35)

2-d-|F|+n(1+\sum_{j=0}^{d}\delta_{j})=n+1-d+(n-1)(\delta_{0}+\cdots+\delta_{d}),

where the equality follows from that fact that
w_{j}\in\{-1,0\}
,
\delta_{0}=-1-w_{0}
and
\delta_{j}=w_{j}
for
j=1,\ldots,d
.
The conditions (
3.3
) and (
3.4
) ensure that there is always at least one output.
For each of such maps (
4.34
), using the isomorphism
\bar{I}
(
4.7
) we may define a corresponding map

(4.36)

\mu^{d,\mathbf{p},\mathbf{w}}:CW^{*}(L_{d-1},L_{d};s_{d}H)\otimes\cdots\otimes CW^{*}(L_{0},L_{1};s_{1}H)\to CW^{*}(L_{0},L_{d};s_{0}H)

(which has the same form as and in fact agrees with (
4.29
))
in a way similar to the definition of the differential
\delta_{-}
via
\partial
,
or the definition of the continuation map
c
(
4.15
) via the copairing
\hat{c}
(
4.17
).
The rule is that conjugating by the isomorphism
\bar{I}
allows us to transfer any output in
CW^{*}(L_{j},L_{j-1};H)
to an input from
CW^{*}(L_{j-1},L_{j};-H)
.
For every output in
CW^{*}(L_{j},L_{j-1};H)
turned into an input from
CW^{*}(L_{j-1},L_{j};-H)
, the degree of the map is shifted by
n
.
And if an input from
CW^{*}(L_{d},L_{0};H)
is turned into an output in
CW^{*}(L_{0},L_{d};-H)
,
the degree of the map is shifted by
-n
.
Thus, the degree of the map (
4.36
)

(4.37)

1-d-(\delta_{0}+\cdots+\delta_{d})=2-d+w_{0}-w_{1}-\cdots-w_{d}=2-d+|F|,

as one would expect from the moduli of popsicles in the usual sense.

Using the definition of the Rabinowitz complexes (
4.20
),
we combine all these maps (
4.29
) to define the overall
\mu^{d}
map (
4.22
),
as on a given component taking the form (
4.29
).
Because of the shift of
CW^{*}(L_{0},L_{1};-H)
in the mapping one (
4.20
), the degree works out as follows.
If for some
1\leq j\leq d
,
w_{j}=-1
, the input is from
CW^{*}(L_{j-1},L_{j};-H)
, whose absolute degree given by the Maslov index equals one plus the degree as a generator of
RC^{*}(L_{j-1},L_{j})
,
so that the degree of a map from there is reduced by
1
, i.e. added by
w_{j}
.
If
w_{0}=-1
, the output is in
CW^{*}(L_{0},L_{d};-H)
, and the same degree shift applies.
Thus, each of the maps (
4.29
), when extended to a map on the Rabinowitz complexes, has degree

(4.38)

2-d+|F|+w_{1}+\cdots+w_{k}-w_{0}=2-d,

as desired.
In case
d=1
, we use interchangeably the notation
\mu^{1}_{RC}=\mu^{1}_{\mathcal{RW}}
.

Lemma 4.9
.

The maps
\mu^{d}_{\mathcal{RW}}
(
4.22
) satisfy the
A_{\infty}
-relations.

Proof.

The proof is a standard argument by matching the terms in the
A_{\infty}
