-associativity equations with contributions from pieces of the boundary strata of the compactification
\bar{\mathcal{R}}^{d+1,\mathbf{p},\mathbf{w}}(\mathbf{x})
.
The only trouble is that there are broken popsicles appearing the in boundary strata carrying weights
<-1
,
which would
a priori
break the
A_{\infty}
-associativity equations,
because such popsicles are not counted in the definition of
\mu^{d}_{\mathcal{RW}}
.
However, by Lemma
3.1
, those contributions are zero:

(i)

either the popsicle is not injective on the first component, so that the algebraic operation coming from that component is zero by Lemma
4.7
;

(ii)

or the broken popsicle is the common boundary point of exact two moduli spaces
\bar{\mathcal{R}}^{d+1,\mathbf{p},\mathbf{w}}
which differ only in the flavors
\mathbf{p}
as described in the case (ii) of Lemma
3.1
,
and the counts of them are in the opposite signs according to (
4.31
) and (
4.32
).

The two resulting operations
\mu^{d,\mathbf{p},\mathbf{w}}
are added in the definition of
\mu^{d}_{\mathcal{RW}}
,
so that their compositions with some other
\mu
’s in the
A_{\infty}
-equations cancel each other.
∎

Definition 4.10
.

Fix an at most countable collection
\mathbf{L}
of admissible exact Lagrangians that are cylindrical at infinity,
such that (
3.18
) holds for these Lagrangians.
The Rabinowitz (wrapped) Fukaya category
\mathcal{RW}=\mathcal{RW}(\mathbf{L})
of
(X,\lambda)
is the
A_{\infty}
-category with objects in
\mathbf{L}
,
morphism spaces

(4.39)

\mathcal{RW}(L_{0},L_{1})=\hom_{\mathcal{RW}}(L_{0},L_{1}):=RC^{*}(L_{0},L_{1}),

and
A_{\infty}
-structure maps given by
\mu^{d}_{\mathcal{RW}}
(
4.22
).

When there is no confusion about the specific collection
\mathbf{L}
of Lagrangians,
we also call it the Rabinowitz Fukaya category of
X
, and denote it by
\mathcal{RW}=\mathcal{RW}(X)
.

By the definition of the Rabinowitz complex
RC^{*}(L_{0},L_{1})
as in (
4.20
),
the wrapped Floer complex
CW^{*}(L_{0},L_{1};H)
is a subcomplex.
Define maps

(4.40)

j_{\mathcal{RW}}^{d}:CW^{*}(L_{d-1},L_{d};H)\otimes\cdots\otimes CW^{*}(L_{0},L_{1};H)\to RC^{*}(L_{0},L_{d})[1-d]

by
j_{\mathcal{RW}}^{1}
the inclusion of of the subcomplex
CW^{*}(L_{0},L_{1};H)
into
RC^{*}(L_{0},L_{1})
, and
j_{\mathcal{RW}}^{d}=0
for all
d\geq 2
.

Lemma 4.11
.

The maps (
4.40
) define an
A_{\infty}
-functor

(4.41)

j_{\mathcal{RW}}:\mathcal{W}(X)\to\mathcal{RW}(X)

Proof.

If all the inputs of
\mu^{d}_{\mathcal{RW}}
are from
\hom_{\mathcal{W}}(L_{i-1},L_{i})
,
then any popsicle with these inputs must have a trivial popsicle structure (i.e. without sprinkles),
and is therefore an ordinary
A_{\infty}
-disk counted in the definition of
\mu^{d}_{\mathcal{W}}
.
∎

5.
The functor from
\mathcal{RW}
to
\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{\infty}

The main objective of this section is to construct an
A_{\infty}
-functor

(5.1)

\Phi:\mathcal{RW}\to\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{\infty},

and prove that it is a quasi-equivalence when the Liouville manifold is non-degenerate (a notion recalled in Definition
5.5
).
On each Lagrangian
L\in\mathrm{ob\ }\mathcal{W}
,
\Phi
acts as the identity,
\Phi(L)=L
.
To define the action of the functor on morphism spaces, let us first recall that the definition of the formal punctured neighborhood at infinity, as described in (
2.44
).
There is a natural equivalence,

(5.2)

\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{\infty}(K,L)\cong\mathrm{Cone}(\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{W}^{op},(Y^{r}_{K})^{*}\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}Y^{r}_{L})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle ev\circ}}{{\to}}\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{W}^{op},\hom_{{\mathbf{k}}}(Y^{r}_{K},Y^{r}_{L}))),

with the
A_{\infty}
-structures described as:
\mu^{1}
the cone differential on Hochschild complexes, and
\mu^{2}
in the induced Yoneda product, given in (
2.49
).
The
A_{\infty}
-functor
\Phi
will formally constructed in terms of operations
\Phi_{-}
landing in the first (shifted) factor of the cone (
5.2
), constructed in §
5.2
and operations
\Phi_{+}
landing in the second (unshifted) factor of the cone (
5.2
), constructed in §
5.1
.
The verification of the
A_{\infty}
functor equations and hence a proof of Theorem
1.1
(i) / Theorem
5.4
then occurs in §
5.3
. In §
5.4
, Theorem
1.1
(ii) / Theorem
5.6
is restated, and the remaining subsections are devoted to its proof. The strategy of the proof is to observe that the linear term of the
A_{\infty}
-functor
\Phi^{1}
respects the natural two step cone-induced filtrations on both sides, and therefore will be a quasi-isomorphism if the maps from the shifted respectively unshifted parts of the Rabinowitz complex to the shifted respectively unshifted parts of (
5.2
) are each quasi-isomorphisms. The map on positive parts is essentially the Yoneda map as shown in §
5.7
. The map on negative parts is comparable to a Calabi-Yau type morphism of the form studied in
[
ganatra
]
; the precise comparison occurs in §
5.8
, §
5.9
, and §
5.10
. Both of these maps are known to be quasi-isomorphisms, the first always and the second assuming non-degeneracy. All together these arguments are combined to prove Theorem
1.1
(ii)/Theorem
5.6
at the end of §
5.10
.

5.1.
The positive part

Let
k\geq 1
be a positive integer, and let
L_{0},\cdots,L_{k}
be
k+1
objects of
\mathcal{RW}
.
We shall construct a sequence of maps

(5.3)

\Phi^{k}_{+}:RC^{*}(L_{k-1},L_{k})\otimes\cdots\otimes RC^{*}(L_{0},L_{1})\to\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{W}^{op},\hom_{{\mathbf{k}}}(Y_{L_{0}}^{r},Y_{L_{k}}^{r}))

of degree
1-k
.
To write these maps out in a more concrete way, let
L^{\prime}_{1},\cdots,L^{\prime}_{l}
be another
l+1
testing objects.
For each
c_{i}\in RC^{*}(L_{i-1},L_{i})
, we want to define the value of
\Phi^{k}_{+}
on the
k
-tensor

c_{k}\otimes\cdots\otimes c_{1}

to be the Hochschild cochain whose
l
-th order term is the a
{\mathbf{k}}
-linear homomorphism between the wrapped Floer cochain spaces:

(5.4)

\begin{split}\Phi^{k,l}_{+}(c_{k}\otimes\cdots\otimes c_{1}):&CW^{*}(L^{\prime}_{1},L^{\prime}_{0};H)\otimes\cdots\otimes CW^{*}(L^{\prime}_{l},L^{\prime}_{l-1};H)\\
&\to\hom_{{\mathbf{k}}}(CW^{*}(L^{\prime}_{0},L_{0};H),CW^{*}(L^{\prime}_{l},L_{k};H)),\end{split}

for all
L^{\prime}_{0},\ldots,L^{\prime}_{l}
.

Note that by (
4.20
), each element
c_{i}
has two components, with respect to the decomposition

RC^{*}(L_{i-1},L_{i})=CW^{*}(L_{i-1},L_{i};-H)[-1]\oplus CW^{*}(L_{i-1},L_{i};H).

Thus each of the maps (
5.4
) is first defined on components,
corresponding to generators
c_{i}=[x_{i}]
for some time-one
H
-chord
x_{i}
from
L_{i-1}
to
L_{i}
, as an input from
CW^{*}(L_{i-1},L_{i};H)
,
or a time-one
H
-chord from
L_{i}
to
L_{i-1}
, as an output in
CW^{*}(L_{i},L_{i-1};H)
,
which can be turned into an input from
CW^{*}(L_{i-1},L_{i};-H)
using the isomorphism
\bar{I}
(
4.7
).

Consider the moduli spaces of popsicles

(5.5)

\mathcal{R}^{k+l+2,\mathbf{p}_{+},\mathbf{w}_{+}}(y^{\prime}_{out},\mathbf{x}^{\prime},y_{in},\mathbf{x}),

where we rename the
0
-th puncture as
z^{\prime}_{out}
,
the
j
-th puncture as
z^{\prime}_{l-j+1}
, for
j=1,\ldots,l
,
the
(l+1)
-th puncture as
z_{in}
,
and the
(l+1+i)
-th puncture as
z_{i}
, for
i=1,\ldots,k
.
Here the weights are

(5.6)

\mathbf{w}_{+}=(0,\underbrace{0,\ldots 0}_{l\text{ times}},0,\mathbf{w}),

such that

(5.7)

\mathbf{w}=(w_{1},\ldots,w_{k}),\text{ and }w_{i}\in\{-1,0\}.

and a popsicle structure
\sigma_{+}
of flavor
\mathbf{p}_{+}
is the choice of sprinkles,
one on each geodesic connecting the
(l+1+i)
-th puncture
z_{i}
to the
0
-th puncture
z^{\prime}_{out}
,
whenever

w_{i}=-1.

The Lagrangian label is chosen in the following order:

(5.8)

L^{\prime}_{l},\ldots,L^{\prime}_{0},L_{0},\ldots,L_{k}

counterclockwise along the boundary of the punctured disk,
such that
L^{\prime}_{l}
is assigned to the boundary component between the
0
-th puncture
z^{\prime}_{out}
and the
1
-st puncture
z^{\prime}_{l}
.

By (
3.31
), the relevant moduli spaces have dimension zero whenever the degrees of the chords satisfy

(5.9)

\deg(y^{\prime}_{out})=\deg(y_{in})+\sum_{i=1}^{k}(-1)^{\delta_{i}}\deg(x_{i})+\sum_{j=1}^{l}\deg(x^{\prime}_{j})+1-k-l-(n+1)\sum_{i=1}^{k}\delta_{i}.

For notational convenience, let us write

(5.10)

o_{\mathbf{x}}^{\mathbf{s}}=o_{x_{k}}^{s_{k}}\otimes\cdots\otimes o_{x_{1}}^{s_{1}},

and

(5.11)

o_{\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}=o_{x^{\prime}_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes o_{x^{\prime}_{l}}.

Each rigid element
u\in\mathcal{R}^{k+l+2,\mathbf{p}_{+},\mathbf{w}_{+}}(y^{\prime}_{out},\mathbf{x}^{\prime},y_{in},\mathbf{x})
induces an isomorphism between the orientation lines,

(5.12)

\mathcal{R}^{k+l+2,\mathbf{p}_{+},\mathbf{w}_{+}}_{u}:o_{\mathbf{x}}^{\mathbf{s}}\otimes o_{y_{in}}\otimes o_{\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}\to o_{y^{\prime}_{out}}.

Summing over all rigid elements
u\in\mathcal{R}^{k+l+2,\mathbf{p}_{+},\mathbf{w}_{+}}(y^{\prime}_{out},\mathbf{x}^{\prime},y_{in},\mathbf{x})
,
and taking the direct product over all possible outputs
y^{\prime}_{out}
and
\mathbf{x}_{out}
,
we obtain a map of degree

1-k-l-(n+1)\sum_{i=1}^{k}\delta_{i}

of the following form

(5.13)

\begin{split}\Phi_{+}^{k,l;\mathbf{p}_{+},\mathbf{w}_{+}}:&CW^{*}(L_{k-1},L_{k};s_{k}H)\otimes\cdots\otimes CW^{*}(L_{0},L_{1};s_{0}H)\\
\to&\hom_{{\mathbf{k}}}(CW^{*}(L^{\prime}_{1},L^{\prime}_{0};H)\otimes\cdots\otimes CW^{*}(L^{\prime}_{l},L^{\prime}_{l-1};H),\\
&\hom_{{\mathbf{k}}}(CW^{*}(L^{\prime}_{0},L_{0};H),CW^{*}(L^{\prime}_{l},L_{k};H))),\\
\end{split}

which on basis elements takes the following values

(5.14)

\begin{split}&\Phi_{+}^{k,l;\mathbf{p}_{+},\mathbf{w}_{+}}([\mathbf{x}^{\mathbf{s}}]\otimes[y_{in}]\otimes[\mathbf{x}^{\prime}])\\
=&\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}y^{\prime}_{out}\\
\deg(y^{\prime}_{out})=\deg(y_{in})+\sum_{i=1}^{k}(-1)^{\delta_{i}}\deg(x_{i})+\sum_{j=1}^{l}\deg(x^{\prime}_{j})+1-k-l\end{subarray}}\\
&\sum_{u\in\mathcal{R}^{k+l+2,\mathbf{p}_{+},\mathbf{w}_{+}}(y^{\prime}_{out},\mathbf{x}^{\prime},y_{in},\mathbf{x})}(-1)^{*_{k,l;\mathbf{p}_{+},\mathbf{w}_{+}}+\Diamond_{k,l;\mathbf{p}_{+},\mathbf{w}_{+}}}\mathcal{R}^{k+l+2,\mathbf{p}_{+},\mathbf{w}_{+}}_{u}([\mathbf{x}^{\mathbf{s}}]\otimes[y_{in}]\otimes[\mathbf{x}^{\prime}]).\end{split}

where the signs
*_{k,l;\mathbf{p}_{+},\mathbf{w}_{+}}
and
\Diamond_{k,l;\mathbf{p}_{+},\mathbf{w}_{+}}
follows from the general formulas (
4.31
) and (
4.32
),
by taking
d=k+l+1
and matching the popsicle structures and weights.

Lemma 5.1
.

The operations
\Phi_{+}^{k,l;\mathbf{p}_{+},\mathbf{w}_{+}}
(
5.14
) extend to well-defined maps (
5.13
) on the whole Floer complexes.

Proof.

Since the formula in (
5.14
) defines the output as a sum,
when there is some
i\in\{1,\ldots,k\}
with
w_{i}=-1
so that
s_{i}=-
,
we must show that the operation
\Phi_{+}^{k,l;\mathbf{p}_{+},\mathbf{w}_{+}}
vanishes for all but finitely many basis elements in the infinite direct product
CW^{*}(L_{i-1},L_{i};-H)
.
This follows from an action argument: when we define the operation
\Phi_{+}^{k,l;\mathbf{p}_{+},\mathbf{w}_{+}}
,
the input
[x_{i}^{-}]
is treated as an output
H
-chord
x_{i}
for any inhomogeneous pseudoholomorphic map counted.
It follows that

(5.15)

\mathcal{A}_{H}(y^{\prime}_{out})+\sum_{i:w_{i}=-1}\mathcal{A}_{H}(x_{i})\geq\mathcal{A}_{H}(y_{in})+\sum_{i:w_{i}=0}\mathcal{A}_{H}(x_{i}).

Since the action spectrum of
H
-chords are bounded above and can only go to
-\infty
,
it follows that for fixed
y_{in}
and
x_{i}
’s with
w_{i}=0
,
there are finitely many possibilities for the outputs
y^{\prime}_{out}
and
x_{i}
’s with
w_{i}=-1
.
In particular, for any
x_{i}
with
w_{i}=-1
and sufficiently negative action, the moduli space is empty, which implies that the operation (
5.14
) takes zero value on the tensor having that factor
[x_{i}^{-}]
.
∎

Then we extend these maps to maps on the Rabinowitz complexes (
4.20
),

(5.16)

\begin{split}\Phi_{+}^{k,l}:&RC^{*}(L_{k-1},L_{k})\otimes\cdots\otimes RC^{*}(L_{0},L_{1})\\
\to&\hom_{{\mathbf{k}}}(CW^{*}(L^{\prime}_{1},L^{\prime}_{0};H)\otimes\cdots\otimes CW^{*}(L^{\prime}_{l},L^{\prime}_{l-1};H),\\
&\hom_{{\mathbf{k}}}(CW^{*}(L^{\prime}_{0},L_{0};H),CW^{*}(L^{\prime}_{l},L_{k};H))),\end{split}

which has degree
1-k-l-\sum_{i=1}^{k}\delta_{i}
.
whose restriction to components determined by
\mathbf{p}_{+}
and
\mathbf{w}_{+}
are (
5.13
),
in a way similar to extending
\mu^{d,\mathbf{p},\mathbf{w}}
(
4.29
) to
\mu^{d}_{\mathcal{RW}}
(
4.22
).
This map has the desired degree
1-k-l
.

To get the map with values in the desired Hochschild cochain complex,
we take the direct product over all testing objects
L^{\prime}_{0},\cdots,L^{\prime}_{l}
, and over all
l\geq 0
,
and note that the
l
-th fold tensor product
CW^{*}(L^{\prime}_{1},L^{\prime}_{0};H)\otimes\cdots\otimes CW^{*}(L^{\prime}_{l},L^{\prime}_{l-1};H)

is precisely the morphism spaces in the opposite category

(5.17)

\mathcal{W}^{op}(L^{\prime}_{0},\ldots,L^{\prime}_{l})=\mathcal{W}(L^{\prime}_{l},\cdots,L^{\prime}_{0})=CW^{*}(L^{\prime}_{1},L^{\prime}_{0};H)\otimes\cdots\otimes CW^{*}(L^{\prime}_{l},L^{\prime}_{l-1};H).

This completes the construction of the map (
5.3
).

5.2.
The negative part

The way of defining
\Phi_{-}
is slightly different from that of
\Phi_{+}
,
as it is part of the
A_{\infty}
-functor that lands in the degree one shift of a complex.
This degree one shift suggests that it should be defined as a count of broken popsicles with two disk components.

Let
L_{0},\cdots,L_{k}
be
k+1
objects of
\mathcal{RW}
.
We shall construct a sequence of maps

(5.18)

\Phi^{k}_{-}:RC^{*}(L_{k-1},L_{k})\otimes\cdots\otimes RC^{*}(L_{0},L_{1})\to\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{W}^{op},(Y_{L_{0}}^{r})^{\vee}\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}Y_{L_{k}}^{r})[1]

of degree
1-k
. To the unshifted complex, this map has degree
2-k
.

Let
L^{\prime}_{0},\cdots,L^{\prime}_{l}
be
l+1
testing objects.
Similar to
\Phi_{+}^{k,l}
, we are going to define the map by components

(5.19)

\begin{split}\Phi_{-}^{k,l}(c_{k}\otimes\cdots\otimes c_{1}):&CW^{*}(L^{\prime}_{1},L^{\prime}_{0};H)\otimes\cdots\otimes CW^{*}(L^{\prime}_{l},L^{\prime}_{l-1};H)\\
&\to\hom_{{\mathbf{k}}}(CW^{*}(L^{\prime}_{0},L_{0};H),CF^{*}(L^{\prime}_{l},L_{k};H))[1],\end{split}

on generators
c_{i}=[x_{i}]
represented by either a time-one chord
x_{i}
from
L_{i-1}
to
L_{i}
,
or a time-one chord from
L_{i}
to
L_{i-1}
.

Consider the compactified moduli space of popsicles:

(5.20)

\bar{\mathcal{R}}^{k+l+2,\mathbf{p}_{+},\mathbf{w}_{+}}(y^{\prime}_{out},\mathbf{x}^{\prime},y_{in},\mathbf{x}),

in which there are codimension boundary strata consisting of the union of the following products:

(5.21)

\begin{split}&\partial_{1}\bar{\mathcal{R}}^{k+l+2,\mathbf{p}_{+},\mathbf{w}_{+}}(y^{\prime}_{out},\mathbf{x}^{\prime},y_{in},\mathbf{x})\\
=&\coprod_{y^{\prime}_{I,out}=y_{II,in}}\mathcal{R}^{k_{1}+l_{1}+2,\mathbf{p}_{I},\mathbf{w}_{I}}(y^{\prime}_{I,out},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{I},y_{in},\mathbf{x}_{I})\times\mathcal{R}^{k_{2}+k_{2}+2,\mathbf{p}_{II},\mathbf{w}_{II}}(y^{\prime}_{out},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{II},y_{II,in},\mathbf{x}_{II}).\end{split}

for all
k_{1},k_{2},l_{1},l_{2}
such that
k_{1}+k_{2}=k
and
l_{1}+l_{2}=l
.
These are moduli spaces of broken popsicles with two disk components,
where the
0
-th puncture
z^{\prime}_{I,out}
of the first disk component is glued to the
(l_{2}+1)
-th puncture
z_{II,in}
of the second disk component.
The induced popsicle structures
\sigma_{I},\sigma_{II}
of flavors
\mathbf{p}_{I},\mathbf{p}_{II}
and weights
\mathbf{w}_{I},\mathbf{w}_{II}
are similar to those of flavors
\mathbf{p}_{+}
and weights
\mathbf{w}_{+}
,
except that we must require that the weight assigned to the
0
-th puncture
z^{\prime}_{I,out}
of the first disk component be
-1
,

(5.22)

w^{\prime}_{I,out}=-1,

such that the number of sprinkles in the popsicle structure
\sigma_{I}
of flavor
\mathbf{p}_{I}
goes down by
1
compared to popsicle structures
\sigma_{+}
of flavor
\mathbf{p}_{+}
with ‘output’ weight
w^{\prime}_{out}=0
.

We are mainly interested in the case where the virtual dimension of (
5.21
) is zero,
which holds if the degrees of the chords satisfy:

(5.23)

\deg(y^{\prime}_{out})=\deg(y_{in})+\sum_{i=1}^{k}(-1)^{\delta_{i}}\deg(x_{i})+\sum_{j=1}^{l}(-1)^{\delta^{\prime}_{j}}\deg(x^{\prime}_{j})+2-k-l-(n+1)\sum_{i=1}^{k}\delta_{i}.

Note that in particular this is independent of the ‘intermediate’ chord
y^{\prime}_{I,out}=y_{II,in}
,
as a result of canceling the common term
\deg(x_{new})
in the dimension formulas for the two components.
However, we have implicitly assumed that Floer data are chosen generically so that all the relevant moduli spaces are regular,
in which case we only take those products such that all the moduli spaces in the right hand side of (
5.21
) are zero-dimensional.
Each rigid broken popsicle
\mathbf{u}
in (
5.21
) induces an isomorphism of orientation lines,
by composing the isomorphisms induced by the two disk components:

(5.24)

\partial_{1}\bar{\mathcal{R}}^{k+l+2,\mathbf{p}_{+},\mathbf{w}_{+}}_{\mathbf{u}}:o_{\mathbf{x}}^{\mathbf{s}}\otimes o_{y_{in}}\otimes o_{\mathbf{x}^{\prime}}\to o_{y^{\prime}_{out}}.

Summing over all
\mathbf{u}
and then over all possible outputs
y^{\prime}_{out},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{out}
as well as ‘intermediate’ chords
y^{\prime}_{I,out}=y_{II,in}
subject to (
5.23
),
with the signs determined by the induced boundary orientations on the product moduli space (
5.21
),
we obtain a map

(5.25)

\begin{split}\Phi_{-}^{k,l;\mathbf{p}_{+},\mathbf{w}_{+}}:&CW^{*}(L_{k-1},L_{k};s_{k}H)\otimes\cdots\otimes CW^{*}(L_{0},L_{1};s_{1}H)\\
\to&\hom_{{\mathbf{k}}}(CW^{*}(L^{\prime}_{1},L^{\prime}_{0};H)\otimes\cdots\otimes CW^{*}(L^{\prime}_{l},L^{\prime}_{l-1};H),\\
&\hom_{{\mathbf{k}}}(CW^{*}(L^{\prime}_{0},L_{0};H),CW^{*}(L^{\prime}_{l},L_{k};H))),\end{split}

which has degree
2-k-l-\sum_{i=1}^{k}\delta_{i}
.
The well-definedness of this map follows from the same argument as that of Lemma
5.1
.

Finally, we follow the same way as in the case of
\Phi_{+}^{k,l}
to extend these maps to maps on Rabinowitz complexes:

(5.26)

\begin{split}\Phi_{-}^{k,l}:&RC^{*}(L_{k-1},L_{k})\otimes\cdots\otimes RC^{*}(L_{0},L_{1})\\
\to&\hom_{{\mathbf{k}}}(CW^{*}(L^{\prime}_{1},L^{\prime}_{0};H)\otimes\cdots\otimes CW^{*}(L^{\prime}_{l},L^{\prime}_{l-1};H),\\
&\hom_{{\mathbf{k}}}(CW^{*}(L^{\prime}_{0},L_{0};H),CW^{*}(L^{\prime}_{l},L_{k};H)))[1].\end{split}

This has degree
1-k-l
because we have shifted the degree of the target cochain complex by
1
.
A priori, we only obtain a map with values in Hochschild cochains with coefficients in the space of
{\mathbf{k}}
-linear homomorphisms

\hom_{{\mathbf{k}}}(CW^{*}(L^{\prime}_{0},L_{0};H),CW^{*}(L^{\prime}_{l},L_{k};H)),

To obtain the correct map
\Phi_{-}^{k}
that takes value in the desired tensor product bimodule
(Y^{r}_{K})^{*}\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}Y^{r}_{L}
,
we must verify that the map defined above indeed lands in the homomorphism space of finite rank,

(5.27)

\hom_{{\mathbf{k}}}^{fin}(CW^{*}(L^{\prime}_{0},L_{0};H),CW^{*}(L^{\prime}_{l},L_{k};H))\cong(CW^{*}(L^{\prime}_{0},L_{0};H))^{\vee}\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}CW^{*}(L^{\prime}_{l},L_{k};H).

To see why this is the case, note that the map
\Phi_{-}^{k,l}
is defined by counting broken popsicles in the moduli spaces (
5.21
).
Thus it suffices to show that these moduli spaces are empty except for finitely many pairs of inputs and outputs.

Lemma 5.2
.

For fixed chords
x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}
and
x^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,x^{\prime}_{l}
, the moduli spaces (
5.21
) are all empty except for finitely many pairs of chords
(y_{in},y^{\prime}_{out})
.

Proof.

Consider the second component of a broken popsicle in the moduli space (
5.21
).
Note that
y_{II,in}
is required to be a time-one
H
-chord but regarded as an output (despite its notation) for the second disk component because of the constraint (
5.22
).
Since the other chords
\mathbf{x}_{II},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{II}
are fixed,
the total action of
y_{II,in}
and
y^{\prime}_{out}
, both of which are outputs for the second disk component,
has an upper bound.
This implies that there are finitely many possible pairs
(y_{II,in},y^{\prime}_{out})
for which the moduli space is non-empty.
For each
y_{II,in}=y^{\prime}_{I,out}
, as an input for the first disk component, there are finitely many possible
y_{in}
for which the moduli space is non-empty, as all the other chords
\mathbf{x}_{I},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{I}
are fixed.
∎

It follows from Lemma
5.2
that the map (
5.26
) indeed takes values in the finite-rank
\hom
-space, or equivalently the tensor product
(CW^{*}(L^{\prime}_{l},L_{0}))^{\vee}\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}CW^{*}(L^{\prime}_{0},L_{k})
,

(5.28)

\begin{split}\Phi^{k,l}_{-}:&RC^{*}(L_{0},L_{1})\otimes\cdots\otimes RC^{*}(L_{k-1},L_{k})\\
\to&\hom_{{\mathbf{k}}}(CW^{*}(L^{\prime}_{1},L^{\prime}_{0};H)\otimes\cdots\otimes CW^{*}(L^{\prime}_{l},L^{\prime}_{l-1};H),(CW^{*}(L^{\prime}_{0},L_{0};H))^{\vee}\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}CW^{*}(L^{\prime}_{l},L_{k};H))[1].\end{split}

Taking the direct product over all
l
-tuples of Lagrangians
L^{\prime}_{0},\cdots,L^{\prime}_{l}
and all
l\geq 0
,
we get the desired map (
5.18
).

5.3.
A_{\infty}
functor equations

The last step in the construction of the functor
\Phi
is to verify that the sequence of maps
\Phi^{k}:=(\Phi_{-}^{k},\Phi_{+}^{k})
satisfy the
A_{\infty}
-functor equations.
Recall that the formal punctured neighborhood at infinity
\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{\infty}
is in fact a dg category,
which implies that all higher
A_{\infty}
-products of order greater than or equal to three vanish.
So the equations will only involve
\mu^{1}
and
\mu^{2}
products of outcomes of
\Phi
.
Recall also the definition of
\mu^{2}
in
\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{\infty}
given in (
2.49
).

Lemma 5.3
.

The maps
\Phi_{+}^{k,l;\mathbf{p}_{+},\mathbf{w}_{+}}
and
\Phi_{-}^{k,l;\mathbf{p}_{+},\mathbf{w}_{+}}
satisfy the following equations

(5.29)

\begin{split}&\sum_{i,j}(-1)^{*_{i}}\Phi_{+}^{k-j,l;\mathbf{p}_{+,i,j},\mathbf{w}_{+,i,j}}([x_{k}^{s_{k}}],\ldots,[x_{i+j+1}^{s_{i+j+1}}],\mu^{j,\mathbf{p}_{i,j},\mathbf{w}_{i,j}}([x_{i+j}^{s_{i+j}}],\ldots,[x_{i+1}^{s_{i+1}}]),\\
&[x_{i}^{s_{i}}],\ldots,[x_{1}^{s_{1}}])([x^{\prime}_{1}],\ldots,[x^{\prime}_{l}])([y_{in}])\\
+&\sum_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}}(-1)^{*_{i^{\prime}}}\Phi_{+}^{k,l-j^{\prime};\mathbf{p}^{\prime}_{+,i^{\prime},j^{\prime}},\mathbf{w}^{\prime}_{+.i^{\prime},j^{\prime}}}([x_{k}^{s_{k}}],\ldots,[x_{1}^{s_{1}}])([x^{\prime}_{1}],\ldots,[x^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}}],\mu^{j^{\prime}}([x^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}+1}],\ldots,[x^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}+j^{\prime}}]),\\
&[x^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}+j^{\prime}+1}],\ldots,[x^{\prime}_{l}])([y_{in}])\\
+&\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{1}+k_{2}=k\\
l_{1}+l_{2}=l\end{subarray}}[\Phi_{+}^{k_{2},l_{2};\mathbf{p}_{+,II},\mathbf{w}_{+,II}}([x_{k}^{s_{k}}],\ldots,[x_{k_{1}+1}^{s_{k_{1}+1}}])([x^{\prime}_{l_{1}+1}],\ldots,[x^{\prime}_{l}])\\
&\circ\Phi_{+}^{k_{1},l_{1};\mathbf{p}_{+,I},\mathbf{w}_{+,I}}([x_{k_{1}}^{s_{k_{1}}}],\ldots,[x_{1}^{s_{1}}])([x^{\prime}_{1}],\ldots,[x^{\prime}_{l_{1}}])]([y_{in}])\\
+&\Phi_{-}^{k,l;\mathbf{p}_{+},\mathbf{w}_{+}}([x_{k}^{s_{k}}],\ldots,[x_{1}^{s_{1}}])([x^{\prime}_{1}],\ldots,[x^{\prime}_{l}])([y_{in}])=0,\end{split}

and

(5.30)

\begin{split}&\sum_{i,j}(-1)^{*_{i}}\Phi_{-}^{k-j,l;\mathbf{p}_{+,i,j},\mathbf{w}_{+,i,j}}([x_{k}^{s_{k}}],\ldots,[x_{i+j+1}^{s_{i+j+1}}],\mu^{j,\mathbf{p}_{i,j},\mathbf{w}_{i,j}}([x_{i+j}^{s_{i+j}}],\ldots,[x_{i+1}^{s_{i+1}}]),\\
&[x_{i}^{s_{i}}],\ldots,[x_{1}^{s_{1}}])([x^{\prime}_{1}],\ldots,[x^{\prime}_{l}]([y_{in}])\\
+&\sum_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}}(-1)^{*_{i^{\prime}}}\Phi_{-}^{k,l-j^{\prime};\mathbf{p}^{\prime}_{+,i^{\prime},j^{\prime}},\mathbf{w}^{\prime}_{+,i^{\prime},j^{\prime}}}([x_{k}^{s_{k}}],\ldots,[x_{1}^{s_{1}}])([x^{\prime}_{1}],\ldots,[x^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}}],\mu^{j^{\prime}}([x^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}+1}],\ldots,[x^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}+j^{\prime}}]),\\
&[x^{\prime}_{i^{\prime}+j^{\prime}+1}],\ldots,[x^{\prime}_{l}])([y_{in}])\\
+&\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{1}+k_{2}=k\\
l_{1}+l_{2}=l\end{subarray}}[\Phi_{-}^{k_{2},l_{2};\mathbf{p}_{+,II},\mathbf{w}_{+,II}}([x_{k}^{s_{k}}],\ldots,[x_{k_{1}+1}^{s_{k_{1}+1}}])([x^{\prime}_{l_{1}+1}],\ldots,[x^{\prime}_{l}])\\
&\circ\Phi_{+}^{k_{1},l_{1};\mathbf{p}_{+,I},\mathbf{p}_{+,I}}([x_{k_{1}}^{s_{k_{1}}}],\ldots,[x_{1}^{s_{1}}])([x^{\prime}_{1}],\ldots,[x^{\prime}_{l_{1}}])]([y_{in}])\\
+&\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{1}+k_{2}=k\\
l_{1}+l_{2}=l\end{subarray}}[\Phi_{+}^{k_{2},l_{2};\mathbf{p}_{+,II},\mathbf{w}_{+,II}}([x_{k}^{s_{k}}],\ldots,[x_{k_{1}+1}^{s_{k_{1}+1}}])([x^{\prime}_{l_{1}+1}],\ldots,[x^{\prime}_{l}])\\
&\circ\Phi_{-}^{k_{1},l_{1};\mathbf{p}_{+,I},\mathbf{p}_{+,I}}([x_{k_{1}}^{s_{k_{1}}}],\ldots,[x_{1}^{s_{1}}])([x^{\prime}_{1}],\ldots,[x^{\prime}_{l_{1}}])]([y_{in}])=0.\end{split}

Here the signs are defined as in (
2.3
).

The popsicle structure
\sigma^{\prime}_{+}
of flavor
\mathbf{p}^{\prime}_{+}
is such that there are fewer punctures
z^{\prime}_{j}
,
but there are no effects on the number of sprinkles since there are no sprinkles on geodesics connecting them to the puncture
z^{\prime}_{out}
.

Proof.

This is a direct consequence of the study of certain boundary strata of the compactification of the moduli space of popsicles

\bar{\mathcal{R}}^{k+l+2,\mathbf{p}_{+},\mathbf{w}_{+}}.

The first equation follows by looking at all the codimension-one boundary strata of the moduli space of dimension one.

The second equation follows by looking at the codimension-one boundary strata of codimension-one boundary stratum

\partial_{1}\bar{\mathcal{R}}^{k+l+2,\mathbf{p}_{+},\mathbf{w}_{+}}(y^{\prime}_{out},\mathbf{x}^{\prime},y_{in},\mathbf{x}),

inside the moduli space of dimension two.
∎

The above two lemmas give preliminary versions of the
A_{\infty}
-functor equations.
Taking the direct product over all
L^{\prime}_{0},\ldots,L^{\prime}_{l}
for all
l\geq 1
,
we obtain the following:

Theorem 5.4

(Theorem
1.1
(i))
.

The sequence of maps
\Phi^{k}=(\Phi^{k}_{-},\Phi^{k}_{+})
form an
A_{\infty}
-functor

\Phi:\mathcal{RW}\to\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{\infty}.

Concretely, this means that the collection
\{(\Phi^{k}_{-},\Phi^{k}_{+})\}_{k\geq 1}
satisfy the following equations:

(5.31)

\begin{split}&\mu^{1}_{\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{\infty}}\circ\Phi^{k}_{+}(c_{k},\ldots,c_{1})+\mu^{2}_{\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{\infty}}(\Phi^{k_{2}}_{+}(c_{k},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{k_{1}+1}),\Phi^{k_{1}}_{+}(c_{k_{1}},\ldots,c_{1}))\\
=&\sum(-1)^{*_{i}}\Phi^{k}_{+}(c_{k},\ldots,c_{i+j+1},\mu^{j}_{\mathcal{RW}}(c_{i+j},\ldots,c_{i+1}),\mathbf{x}_{i},\ldots,c_{1})+\Phi^{k}_{-}(c_{k},\ldots,c_{1}),\end{split}

and

(5.32)

\begin{split}&\mu^{1}_{\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{\infty}}\circ\Phi^{k}_{-}(c_{k},\ldots,c_{1})+\mu^{2}_{\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{\infty}}(\Phi^{k_{2}}_{+}(c_{k},\ldots,c_{k_{1}+1}),\Phi^{k_{1}}_{-}(c_{k_{1}},\ldots,c_{1}))\\
&+\mu^{2}_{\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{\infty}}(\Phi^{k_{2}}_{-}(c_{k},\ldots,c_{k_{1}+1}),\Phi^{k_{1}}_{+}(c_{k_{1}},\ldots,c_{1}))\\
=&\sum(-1)^{*_{i}}\Phi^{k}_{-}(c_{k},\ldots,c_{i+j+1},\mu^{j}_{\mathcal{RW}}(c_{i+j},\ldots,c_{i+1}),c_{i},\ldots,c_{1}).\end{split}

for every generator
c_{i}\in RC^{*}(L_{i-1},L_{i})
, where the signs are defined as in (
2.3
).

Proof.

By (
2.49
), the structure maps
\mu^{1}_{\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{\infty}}
and
\mu^{2}_{\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{\infty}}
are given by combinations of
A_{\infty}
-structure maps
\mu_{\mathcal{W}}
of the wrapped Fukaya category,
and composition of linear maps in the
\hom
-space.
More precisely,
\mu^{1}_{\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{\infty}}
is the cone differential of two Hochschild cochain complexes,
which involves all those operations
\mu^{j^{\prime}}
appearing in the second term of each of (
5.29
) and (
5.30
).
And
\mu^{2}_{\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{\infty}}
is induced by the composition in
\hom_{{\mathbf{k}}}(\mathcal{W}^{op}_{\Delta},\mathcal{W}^{op}_{\Delta})
and the action of
\hom_{{\mathbf{k}}}(\mathcal{W}^{op}_{\Delta},\mathcal{W}^{op}_{\Delta})
on
(\mathcal{W}^{op})^{\vee}\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}\mathcal{W}^{op}_{\Delta}
.
For the two Hochschild cochains

\Phi^{k_{1}}_{+}(c_{k_{1}},\ldots,c_{1})

and

\Phi^{k_{2}}_{+}(c_{k},\ldots,c_{k_{1}+1}),

their product

\mu^{2}_{\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{\infty}}(\Phi^{k_{2}}_{+}(c_{k},\ldots,c_{k_{1}+1}),\Phi^{k_{1}}_{+}(c_{k_{1}},\ldots,c_{1}))

is defined to be the Hochschild cochain whose
l
-th order component acting on

[x^{\prime}_{1}]\otimes\cdots\otimes[x^{\prime}_{l}]\in CW^{*}(L^{\prime}_{1},L^{\prime}_{0};H)\otimes\cdots\otimes CW^{*}(L^{\prime}_{l},L^{\prime}_{l-1};H)

is the composition of linear maps that takes an input
[y_{in}]
to the following output:

\begin{split}&\sum\Phi_{+}^{k_{2},l_{2};\mathbf{p}_{+,II},\mathbf{w}_{+,II}}([x_{k}^{s_{k}}],\ldots,[x_{k_{1}+1}^{s_{k_{1}+1}}])([x^{\prime}_{l_{1}+1}],\ldots,[x^{\prime}_{l}])\\
&\circ\Phi_{+}^{k_{1},l_{1};\mathbf{p}_{+,I},\mathbf{w}_{+,I}}([x_{k_{1}}^{s_{k_{1}}}],\ldots,[x_{1}^{s_{1}}])([x^{\prime}_{1}],\ldots,[x^{\prime}_{l_{1}}])]([y_{in}]),\end{split}

where is sum is taken over all
l_{1},l_{2}
such that
l_{1}+l_{2}=l
.

With these observations, if we restrict the equations (
5.31
) and (
5.32
) to their
l
-th order terms of the Hochschild cochains,
and evaluate the Hochschild cochains on the corresponding wrapped Floer cochain spaces,
we get exactly (
5.29
) and (
5.30
).
∎

5.4.
Statement of Equivalence

While for any Liouville manifold there is such an
A_{\infty}
-functor

\Phi:\mathcal{RW}\to\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{\infty},

we can only expect it to have best features when the wrapped Fukaya category
\mathcal{W}
does not behave too wildly.
A natural condition is that the Liouville manifold
(X,\lambda)
has ‘enough Lagrangians’,
which can be formally stated as the ‘non-degeneracy’ condition, in the sense of
[
ganatra
]
.
Let us first recall the definition.

Definition 5.5
.

A Liouville manifold
(X,\lambda)
is said to be non-degenerate,
if it admits a finite collection of Lagrangians
\{L_{i}\}
which are objects of
\mathcal{W}
,
such that the open-closed map

(5.33)

\mathcal{OC}:\mathrm{HH}_{*-n}(\mathcal{W}(X),\mathcal{W}(X))\to SH^{*}(X)

restricted to the subcategory of the wrapped Fukaya category having
\{L_{i}\}
as objects hits the identity element in symplectic cohomology.

The main result regarding the functor
\Phi
is the following:

Theorem 5.6

(Theorem
1.1
(ii))
.

Suppose the Liouville manifold
(X,\lambda)
is non-degenerate.
Then the
A_{\infty}
-functor

\Phi:\mathcal{RW}\to\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{\infty}

is a quasi-equivalence of
A_{\infty}
-categories.

It suffices to check that the linear term
\Phi^{1}
induces an isomorphism on cohomology,

H^{*}(\Phi^{1}):H^{*}(\mathcal{RW}(K,L))=H^{*}(RC^{*}(K,L),\mu^{1}_{RC})\to H^{*}(\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{\infty}(K,L)).

The cohomology of
\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{\infty}
is computed to be the cohomology of the cone of Hochschild cohomology cochain complexes:

H^{*}(\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{\infty}(K,L))=H^{*}(\mathrm{Cone}(\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{W}^{op},(Y_{K}^{r})^{*}\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}Y_{L}^{r})\to\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{W}^{op},\hom_{{\mathbf{k}}}(Y_{K}^{r},Y_{L}^{r}))).

Our strategy is to compute the cohomology of
\mathcal{RW}
by a cone complex of
A_{\infty}
-bimodules,
and construct a quasi-isomorphism between the two cones.
In fact,
\mathcal{RW}
as a bimodule over
\mathcal{W}
(or rather
\mathcal{W}^{op}
) via the natural functor
\mathcal{W}\to\mathcal{RW}
, is quasi-equivalent to the cone of bimodules

\mathrm{Cone}(\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{W}^{op},(\mathcal{W}^{op})^{\vee}\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}\mathcal{W}^{op}_{\Delta})\to\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{W}^{op},\hom_{{\mathbf{k}}}(\mathcal{W}^{op}_{\Delta},\mathcal{W}^{op}_{\Delta}))

which is briefly mentioned at the beginning of §
5
,
although for our purpose of proving Theorem
5.6
we do not need this generality.
In the remaining subsections,
we shall construct the various
A_{\infty}
-bimodule maps involved in comparing the two cones,
culminating (in §
5.10
) in the proof of Theorem
5.6
.

5.5.
The cohomology of
\mathcal{RW}
as a bimodule

By definition, the Rabinowitz complex (
4.20
) is a cone complex.
This can be upgraded to the statement that
\mathcal{RW}
, as a bimodule over
\mathcal{W}^{op}
, is a cone of a morphism between certain
A_{\infty}
-bimodules.
To describe this cone, we shall introduce an
A_{\infty}
-bimodule over
\mathcal{W}^{op}
with underlying chain complexes being
CW^{*}(K,L;-H)
, denoted by

(5.34)

\mathcal{W}_{-},

called the
negative bimodule
of
\mathcal{W}^{op}
.
For each pair of objects
(K,L)\in\mathrm{ob\ }\mathcal{W}^{op}\times\mathrm{ob\ }\mathcal{W}^{op}=\mathrm{ob\ }\mathcal{W}\times\mathrm{ob\ }\mathcal{W}
, define

(5.35)

\mathcal{W}_{-}(K,L):=CW^{*}(K,L;-H).

The
A_{\infty}
-bimodule structure maps

(5.36)

\begin{split}\mu^{k,l}_{\mathcal{W}_{-}}:&CW^{*}(K_{1},K_{0};H)\otimes\cdots\otimes CW^{*}(K_{k},K_{k-1};H)\otimes\mathcal{W}_{-}(K_{0},L_{0})\\
&\otimes CW^{*}(L_{l-1},L_{l};H)\otimes\cdots\otimes CW^{*}(L_{0},L_{1};H)\to\mathcal{W}_{-}(K_{k},L_{l})\end{split}

are defined by counting rigid elements in the moduli spaces of popsicles
\mathcal{R}^{d+1,\mathbf{p},\mathbf{w}}(\mathbf{x})
(
3.29
)
with several constraints on the popsicle flavors
\mathbf{p}
, the weights
\mathbf{w}
and the chords
\mathbf{x}
,
plus a suitable sign twisted.
(The order of tensor products does not appears in the usual way because here we are defining
\mathcal{W}_{-}
as a bimodule over the opposite category
\mathcal{W}^{op}
.)
More concretely, the bimodule structure maps are defined as follows.
Let
x_{i}
be a time-one
H
-chord from
K_{i}
to
K_{i-1}
,

x^{\prime}_{j}
a time-one
H
-chord from
L_{j-1}
to
L_{j}
,

y
a time-one
H
-chord from
L_{0}
to
K_{0}
,

z
a time-one
H
-chord from
L_{l}
to
K_{k}
.
Consider the moduli space of popsicles

\mathcal{R}^{k+l+2,\varnothing,\mathbf{w}_{-,k,l}}(z,\mathbf{x},y,\mathbf{x}^{\prime}),

introduced in (
3.85
).
Counting rigid elements in the zero-dimensional moduli spaces, with the following sign specification, yields a map

(5.37)

\begin{split}\mu^{k,l}_{\mathcal{W}_{-}}:&CW^{*}(K_{1},K_{0};H)\otimes\cdots\otimes CW^{*}(K_{k},K_{k-1};H)\otimes CW^{*}(K_{0},L_{0};-H)\\
&\otimes CW^{*}(L_{l-1},L_{l};H)\otimes\cdots\otimes CW^{*}(L_{0},L_{1};H)\to CW^{*}(K_{k},L_{l};-H),\\
\mu^{k,l}([\mathbf{x}]\otimes[y^{-}]\otimes[\mathbf{x}^{\prime}])=&\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}y\\
\deg(y)=\deg(z)+\sum_{i=1}^{k}\deg(x_{i})+\sum_{j=1}^{l}\deg(x^{\prime}_{j})+1-k-l\end{subarray}}\\
&\sum_{u\in\mathcal{R}^{k+l+2,\varnothing,\mathbf{w}_{-,k,l}}(z,\mathbf{x},y,\mathbf{x}^{\prime})}(-1)^{\dagger_{k,l}}\mathcal{R}^{k+l+2,\varnothing,\mathbf{w}_{-}}_{u}([\mathbf{x}]\otimes[y^{-}]\otimes[\mathbf{x}^{\prime}]),\end{split}

where the sign is

(5.38)

\dagger_{k,l}=\sum_{i=1}^{k}i\deg(x_{k-i+1})+k\deg(y)+\sum_{j=1}^{l}(k+j)\deg(x^{\prime}_{j}).

Well-definedness of the maps (
5.37
) follows from a proof similar to those of Lemma
4.2
and Lemma
4.8
.

A standard argument by looking at the boundary strata of the one-dimensional moduli spaces implies the following:

Lemma 5.7
.

The maps
\mu^{k,l}_{\mathcal{W}_{-}}
satisfy the equations for an
A_{\infty}
-bimodule structure.

Recall that there is a continuation map (
4.15
) with source being
CW^{*}(K,L;-H)
,
the underlying cochain complex for the negative bimodule
\mathcal{W}_{-}
.
This continuation map can be upgraded to a map of
A_{\infty}
-bimodules.
The
diagonal bimodule

(5.39)

\mathcal{W}_{\Delta}

of
\mathcal{W}
is the
A_{\infty}
-bimodule over
(\mathcal{W},\mathcal{W})
whose underlying cochain complexes are

(5.40)

\mathcal{W}_{\Delta}(K,L)=\mathcal{W}(L,K)=CW^{*}(L,K;H),

and whose structure maps are given by the
A_{\infty}
-structure maps of
\mathcal{W}
,
i.e. given by counting rigid elements in the moduli space

\mathcal{R}^{k+l+2}(y,\mathbf{x}^{\prime},z,\mathbf{x}),

with a sign twist in the form of (
5.38
).
Similarly, the diagonal module
\mathcal{W}^{op}_{\Delta}
of the opposite category
\mathcal{W}^{op}
has cochain spaces being the wrapped Floer cochain spaces in the ’correct’ direction,

(5.41)

\mathcal{W}^{op}_{\Delta}(K,L)=CW^{*}(K,L;H).

Lemma 5.8
.

The continuation map (
4.15
) extends to a natural (closed) morphism of
A_{\infty}
-bimodules

(5.42)

\mathfrak{c}:\mathcal{W}_{-}\to\mathcal{W}^{op}_{\Delta}.

Proof.

The construction of the
A_{\infty}
-bimodule morphism uses the moduli spaces of popsicles

(5.43)

\mathcal{R}^{k+l+2,\mathbf{p}_{-+},\mathbf{w}_{-+}}(z,\mathbf{x},y,\mathbf{x}^{\prime}),

where the popsicle structure
\sigma_{-+}
of flavor
\mathbf{p}_{-+}
carries exactly one sprinkle on the geodesic connecting the
0
-th puncture
z^{\prime}_{out}
to the
(l+1)
-th puncture
z_{in}
,
and the weights are

(5.44)

\mathbf{w}_{-+}=(0,\underbrace{0,\ldots,0}_{l\text{ times }},-1,\underbrace{0,\ldots,0}_{k\text{ times }}).

A priori
, counting rigid elements defines a pre-morphism of
A_{\infty}
-bimodules,
and closedness follows from an analysis of the codimension-one boundary strata of one-dimensional moduli spaces of the same kind.
∎

The mapping cone of the morphism
\mathfrak{c}
is an
A_{\infty}
-bimodule over
\mathcal{W}^{op}
,

(5.45)

\mathrm{Cone}(\mathfrak{c}).

Our definition and computation immediately implies that its cohomology agree with the cohomology of
\mathcal{RW}
.

Lemma 5.9
.

There is a natural quasi-isomorphism of complexes

(5.46)

\iota:\mathrm{Cone}(\mathfrak{c})(K,L)\to RC^{*}(K,L).

Proof.

On the level of underlying chain complexes,
both sides of (
5.46
) are the following Floer complexes

CW^{*}(K,L;-H)[1]\oplus CW^{*}(K,L;H).

We define
\iota
to be the identity map.
The differentials can also be identified,
as we can make the same choices of Floer data for the strip
Z
,
as well as for the popsicle with two positive punctures carrying a sprinkle on the geodesic connecting the punctures,
in both the setups of
\mathcal{W}_{-}
and
\mathcal{RW}
.
∎

Remark 5.10
.

In fact, if we regard
\mathcal{RW}
as a bimodule over
\mathcal{W}^{op}
via the natural functor
\mathcal{W}\to\mathcal{RW}
,
then one can further upgrade (
5.46
) to a quasi-isomorphism of bimodules over
\mathcal{W}^{op}
.

5.6.
Bimodule Poincaré duality

Wrapped Floer homology is known to linear dual to wrapped Floer cohomology over a field
{\mathbf{k}}
, see for example
[
CO
]
.
Combined with Poincaré duality, this says that the isomorphism (
4.7
) is compatible with differentials,
which we have verified in the construction of the differential on
CW^{*}(K,L;-H)
.
The isomorphism
\bar{I}
(
4.7
) can be further upgraded to a quasi-isomorphism of
A_{\infty}
-bimodules from
\mathcal{W}_{-}
to the linear dual diagonal bimodule
(\mathcal{W}^{op})^{\vee}
(Definition
2.1
).

The linear duality between wrapped Floer homology (computed as the cohomology of
-H
) and cohomology can be rephrased in terms of an equivalence of bimodules:

Proposition 5.11
.

There is a quasi-isomorphism of
A_{\infty}
-bimodules

(5.47)

I:\mathcal{W}_{-}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}(\mathcal{W}^{op})^{\vee}[-n]

The grading on
(\mathcal{W}^{op})^{\vee}
is such that

(\mathcal{W}^{op})^{\vee}[-n](K,L)=\hom_{{\mathbf{k}}}(CW^{-(*-n)}(L,K),{\mathbf{k}})=\hom_{{\mathbf{k}}}(CW^{n-*}(L,K),{\mathbf{k}}).

While the
(0,0)
-th order term is given by the map
\bar{I}
(
4.7
),
we want to give it a different interpretation in terms of disk counts,
so that the construction is naturally generalized to higher order terms by inserting more punctures on the boundary.
The relevant moduli spaces have been introduced in subsection
3.6
.
First, consider the moduli space

\mathcal{R}_{p}(x_{-},x_{+})

defined in (
3.38
).
Counting rigid elements in zero-dimensional moduli spaces, with the sign twist
(-1)^{\deg(x_{+})}
, yields a map

(5.48)

\hat{I}^{0,0}:CW^{*}(L,K;H)\to CW^{*}(L,K;H).

Using the isomorphism
\bar{I}
(
4.7
), this is equivalent to a map

(5.49)

I^{0,0}:CW^{*}(K,L;-H)\otimes CW^{n-*}(L,K;H)\to{\mathbf{k}},

which by adjunction is in turn equivalent to

(5.50)

I^{0,0}:CW^{*}(K,L;-H)\to\hom_{{\mathbf{k}}}(CW^{n-*}(L,K;H),{\mathbf{k}}),

by abuse of notation.

Lemma 5.12
.

For sufficiently small perturbations of the one-parameter family
J_{t}
as part of the Floer data,
the map
\hat{I}^{0,0}
is the identity map,
so that the map
\bar{I}
agrees with the map
I^{0,0}
.

Proof.

This follows immediately from Lemma
3.8
.
∎

For
k,l\geq 0
, consider the moduli spaces

\mathcal{R}_{p}^{k,l}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime};x_{-},x_{+})

defined in (
3.53
).
When the virtual dimension of the moduli space is zero, i.e. when

\deg(x_{-})=\deg(x_{+})+\sum_{i=1}^{k}\deg(x_{i})+\sum_{j=1}^{l}\deg(x^{\prime}_{j})-k-l,

we define a map

(5.51)

\begin{split}I^{k,l}:&CW^{*}(K_{1},K_{0})\otimes\cdots\otimes CW^{*}(K_{k},K_{k-1})\otimes\mathcal{W}_{-}(K_{0},L_{0})\\
&\otimes CW^{*}(L_{l-1},L_{l})\otimes\cdots\otimes CW^{*}(L_{0},L_{1})\to\hom_{{\mathbf{k}}}(CW^{n+k+l-*}(L_{l},K_{k}),{\mathbf{k}}),\end{split}

by its action on basis elements:

(5.52)

\begin{split}&I^{k,l}([\mathbf{x}]\otimes[x_{-}^{-}]\otimes[\mathbf{x}^{\prime}])\\
=&\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x_{-}\\
\deg(x_{-})=\deg(x_{+})-\sum_{i=1}^{k}\deg(x_{i})-\sum_{j=1}^{l}\deg(x^{\prime}_{j})-k-l\end{subarray}}\sum_{u\in\mathcal{R}_{p}^{k,l}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{\prime};x_{-},x_{+})}(-1)^{\dagger_{k,l}}(\mathcal{R}_{p}^{k,l})_{u}([\mathbf{x}]\otimes[x_{-}^{-}]\otimes[\mathbf{x}^{\prime}]),\end{split}

where the sign is (
5.38
).
Again, we use an action filtration argument to show that the operation (
5.52
) extended to a well-defined map (
5.51
).

Lemma 5.13
.

The maps
I^{k,l}
form a morphism of
A_{\infty}
-bimodules

I:\mathcal{W}_{-}\to(\mathcal{W}^{op})^{\vee}[-n].

Proof.

This follows Lemma
3.9
.
The first line on the right hand side of (
3.55
) contributes to the
A_{\infty}
-bimodule structure maps of
\mathcal{W}_{-}
.
The second line contributes to the
A_{\infty}
-bimodule structure maps of
(\mathcal{W}^{op})^{\vee}[-n]
.
The third and the last lines contribute to the extra terms
corresponding to the action of the
A_{\infty}
-structure maps of
\mathcal{W}^{op}

on the bimodules
\mathcal{W}_{-}
and
(\mathcal{W}^{op})^{\vee}[-n]
.
The fact that all these moduli spaces on the right hand side of (
3.55
) appear in the boundary strata of some moduli space of one dimension higher
implies that the sum of these algebraic terms is zero.
It follows that the maps
I^{k,l}
form a pre-morphism of
A_{\infty}
-bimodules,
which is closed under the differential on the category of
A_{\infty}
-bimodules.
That is,
I
is a bimodule morphism.
∎

Proof of Proposition
5.11
.

Lemma
5.13
constructs the desired bimodule morphism
I
.
Since the
(0,0)
-th order term
I^{0,0}
is an isomorphism of complexes,
it follows that
I
is a quasi-isomorphism.
∎

5.7.
The Yoneda map

Recall that the morphism space in the category of right modules over
\mathcal{W}
can be computed by the following Hochschild cohomology cochain complex

(5.53)

\hom_{\mathrm{Fun}(\mathcal{W}^{op},\mathrm{Ch}_{\mathbf{k}})}(Y_{K}^{r},Y_{L}^{r})=\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{W}^{op},\hom_{{\mathbf{k}}}(Y_{K}^{r},Y_{L}^{r})).

Recall that there is a cohomologically full and faithful Yoneda embedding (see e.g.,
[
seidel_book
]
)

y:\mathcal{W}\to\mathrm{Fun}(\mathcal{W}^{op},\mathrm{Ch}_{{\mathbf{k}}})

whose associated chain map on morphism spaces, for each pair of objects
(K,L)

(5.54)

y_{+}:\mathcal{W}(K,L)=CW^{*}(K,L;H)\to\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{W}^{op},\hom_{{\mathbf{k}}}(Y_{K}^{r},Y_{L}^{r}))

is given in terms of the
A_{\infty}
-structure maps of the wrapped Fukaya category
\mathcal{W}
with a suitable sign twist:

(5.55)

y_{+}(b)(c^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,c^{\prime}_{l})(a)=(-1)^{\deg(b)+\sum_{j=1}^{l}\deg(c^{\prime}_{j})-l-1}\mu^{l+2}_{\mathcal{W}}(b,a,c^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,c^{\prime}_{l}).

Consider the following composition of maps

(5.56)

\mathcal{W}^{op}_{\Delta}(K,L)=CW^{*}(K,L;H)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\iota_{++}}}{{\to}}RC^{*}(K,L)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Phi_{+}^{1}}}{{\to}}\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{W}^{op},\hom_{{\mathbf{k}}}(Y_{K}^{r},Y_{L}^{r})),

where the first map

(5.57)

\iota_{++}:CW^{*}(K,L;H)\to RC^{*}(K,L)

is the component of the quasi-isomorphism (
5.46
) with image contained in
CW^{*}(K,L;H)
,
which is the identity map on
CW^{*}(K,L;H)
,
and
\Phi_{+}^{1}
is defined in (
5.3
), for
k=1
.
The following observation and corollary constitute essentially half
of the proof of Theorem
5.6
.

Lemma 5.14
.

The map (
5.56
) agrees with the Yoneda map
y_{+}
(
5.54
).

Since the Yoneda embedding is cohomologically fully faithful, it follows from this Lemma that

Corollary 5.15
.

The map (
5.56
) is a quasi-isomorphism. ∎

Proof of Lemma
5.14
.

The composition of
\Phi_{+}^{1}
with
\iota_{++}
is a map which is defined by counting rigid elements in the moduli space of popsicles
\mathcal{R}^{l+3,\mathbf{p}_{+},\mathbf{w}_{+}}(y^{\prime}_{out},\mathbf{x}^{\prime},y_{in},x)
,
such that the weight
w=w_{1}
at the puncture
z=z_{1}
is required to be
0
,
so that all the weights are
0
.
This condition implies that the popsicle structure does not carry any sprinkle,
and that the moduli space is just the moduli space of the usual inhomogeneous pseudoholomorphic disks that are counted in the definition of the
A_{\infty}
-structure maps of the wrapped Fukaya category.
∎

Corollary 5.16
.

The composition
\Phi\circ j_{\mathcal{RW}}
of
j_{\mathcal{RW}}:\mathcal{W}\to\mathcal{RW}
(
4.41
) with
\Phi:\mathcal{RW}\to\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{\infty}
(
5.1
) agrees with the induced Yoneda functor
\bar{y}:\mathcal{W}\to\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{\infty}
, up to homotopy.

Proof.

Since the only nonzero terms of
j_{\mathcal{RW}}
are the linear terms, this immediately follows from Lemma
5.14
.
∎

5.8.
The smooth Calabi-Yau structure

The second remaining key step of the proof of Theorem
5.6
is to show that the other composition

(5.58)

\mathcal{W}_{-}(K,L)[1]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\iota_{--}}}{{\to}}RC^{*}(K,L)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Phi_{-}^{1}}}{{\to}}\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{W}^{op},(Y^{r}_{K})^{*}\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}Y^{r}_{L})[1].

is also a quasi-isomorphism,
where

(5.59)

\iota_{--}:\mathcal{W}_{-}(K,L)[1]=CW^{*}(K,L;-H)[1]\to RC^{*}(K,L)

is the component of the quasi-isomorphism (
5.46
) landing in
CW^{*}(K,L;-H)[1]
,
which is by definition the inclusion on
CW^{*}(K,L;-H)[1]
.
To prove that this composition (
5.58
) is a quasi-isomorphism,
we need the
non-degeneracy
condition on the Liouville manifold
X
,
and the proof will involve a full package of algebraic structures related to the
(weak) smooth Calabi-Yau structure
of
\mathcal{W}(X)
, proven to exist in
[
ganatra
]
.

Recall from (
2.54
) that the inverse dualizing bimodule of
\mathcal{W}
is by definition the bimodule dual of the diagonal bimodule
\mathcal{W}_{\Delta}
of
\mathcal{W}
,

(5.60)

\mathcal{W}^{!}:=\hom_{\mathcal{W}-\mathcal{W}}(\mathcal{W}_{\Delta},\mathcal{W}_{\Delta}\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}\mathcal{W}_{\Delta}),

In this paper, we will take a different but quasi-equivalent model of
\mathcal{W}^{!}
as the following Hochschild complex:

(5.61)

\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{W},\mathcal{W}_{\Delta}\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}\mathcal{W}_{\Delta}).

Counting rigid elements in the moduli space

\mathcal{R}_{2;(0,0)}^{1,l;r,s}(\mathbf{x}^{+,1},x,\mathbf{x}^{+,2},x_{-,2},\mathbf{x}^{\prime},x_{-,1})

given in (
3.77
) in §
3.7
,
we obtain maps of the following form

(5.62)

\begin{split}\mathcal{CY}^{l;r,s}:&CW^{*}(K_{1},K_{0};H)\otimes\cdots\otimes CW^{*}(K_{r},K_{r-1};H)\otimes\mathcal{W}^{op}_{\Delta}(K_{0},L_{0})\\
&\otimes CW^{*}(L_{s-1},L_{s};H)\otimes\cdots\otimes CW^{*}(L_{0},L_{1};H)\\
&\to\hom_{{\mathbf{k}}}(CW^{*}(L^{\prime}_{1},L^{\prime}_{0};H)\otimes\cdots\otimes CW^{*}(L^{\prime}_{l},L^{\prime}_{l-1};H),\mathcal{W}^{op}_{\Delta}(K_{r},L^{\prime}_{0})\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}\mathcal{W}^{op}_{\Delta}(L^{\prime}_{l},L_{s}))[n],\end{split}

by the formula

(5.63)

\begin{split}&\mathcal{CY}^{l;r,s}([\mathbf{x}^{+,1}]\otimes[x]\otimes[\mathbf{x}^{+,2}])([\mathbf{x}^{\prime}])\\
=&\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x_{-,1},x_{-,2}\\
\deg\end{subarray}}\\
&\sum_{u\in\mathcal{R}_{2;(0,0)}^{1,l;r,s}(\mathbf{x}^{+,1},x,\mathbf{x}^{+,2},x_{-,2},\mathbf{x}^{\prime},x_{-,1})}(-1)^{\dagger_{l,r,s}}(\mathcal{R}_{2;(0,0)}^{1,l;r,s})_{u}([\mathbf{x}^{+,1}]\otimes[x]\otimes[\mathbf{x}^{+,2}]\otimes[x^{\prime}]).\end{split}

where the sign is

(5.64)

\dagger_{l,r,s}=\sum_{h=1}^{l}h\deg(x^{\prime}_{l-h+1})\sum_{i=1}^{r}i\deg(x^{+,1}_{r-i+1})+r\deg(x)+\sum_{j=1}^{s}(r+j)\deg(x^{+,2}_{j})

for all
r,s,l\geq 0
and all possible testing objects
L^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,L^{\prime}_{l}
.
Taking the direct over all possible testing objects
L^{\prime}_{0},\ldots,L^{\prime}_{l}
for all
l\geq 0
,
we get
(r,s)
-term of a pre-morphism of
A_{\infty}
-bimodules.
Then it is a book-keeping exercise,
by looking at the codimension-one boundary strata of the relevant moduli spaces,
to check that this pre-morphism is closed, i.e. a morphism of bimodules.

Lemma 5.17
.

The maps
\mathcal{CY}^{l;r,s}
form a morphism of
A_{\infty}
-bimodules:

(5.65)

\mathcal{CY}:\mathcal{W}^{op}_{\Delta}\to\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{W}^{op},\mathcal{W}^{op}_{\Delta}\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}\mathcal{W}^{op}_{\Delta})[n].

∎

One of the main results of
[
ganatra
]
implies that:

Proposition 5.18

(essentially
[
ganatra
]
, Theorem 1.3)
.

Suppose the Liouville manifold
(X,\lambda)
is non-degenerate.
Then the morphism
\mathcal{CY}
(
5.65
) is a quasi-isomorphism of
A_{\infty}
-bimodules.

Sketch.

It suffices to show that the underlying map of chain complexes

\mathcal{CY}_{K,L}:\hom(K,L)\to\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{W},Y^{l}_{K}\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}Y^{r}_{L})[n]

is a quasi-isomorphism for each pair of objects
K,L
.
We will sketch how the result from
[
ganatra
]
*Thm. 1.3, which involves slightly different moduli spaces, implies the desired result. The reference

loc. cit.
proves that for the wrapped Fukaya category
\mathcal{W}
of a non-degenerate Liouville manifold,
there is a quasi-isomorphism of bimodules

(5.66)

{}_{2}\mathcal{CY}:\mathcal{W}_{\Delta}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\mathcal{W}^{!}[n],

(called
\mathcal{CY}
in
loc. cit.
) where
\mathcal{W}^{!}
is the complex (
5.60
) rather than (
5.61
). Let’s call the resulting map of chain complexes
{}_{2}\mathcal{CY}_{K,L}
for each pair of objects
K,L
. The map
{}_{2}\mathcal{CY}
is defined by counting moduli spaces of discs with four distinguished points of fixed cross ratio, alternating input and output, along with arbitrary many other inputs in between; for
{}_{2}\mathcal{CY}_{K,L}
there are no additional inputs before or after one of the distinguished input points. Now there is a quasi-isomorphism of chain complexes
\mathbf{\Psi}_{K,L}:\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{W},Y^{l}_{K}\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}Y^{r}_{L})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\mathcal{W}^{!}(K,L)
(see e.g.,
[
ganatra
]
*Prop. 2.5). Finally a variation on the argument of
[
ganatra
]
*Prop. 5.6 which follows by considering the moduli spaces associated to
{}_{2}\mathcal{CY}_{K,L}
and degenerating the fixed cross ratio of the four special points to
\infty
, implies that there is a chain homotopy
{}_{2}\mathcal{CY}_{K,L}\simeq\mathbf{\Psi}_{K,L}\circ\mathcal{CY}_{K,L}
. Since
\mathbf{\Psi}_{K,L}
and
{}_{2}\mathcal{CY}_{K,L}
are quasi-isomorphisms, the result follows.
∎

We refer to the above as the fact that
\mathcal{W}
has a
smooth Calabi-Yau structure
,
and call this map the
Calabi-Yau map
.

5.9.
A different Calabi-Yau-type map

There is a variant of the Calabi-Yau map (
5.66
),
by considering the moduli spaces

\mathcal{R}_{2;(-1,-1)}^{1,l;r,s}(\mathbf{x}^{+,1},x_{+},\mathbf{x}^{+,2},x_{-,2},\mathbf{x}^{\prime},x_{-,1})

as in (
3.79
).
The outcome of counting rigid elements is a map of the following form

(5.67)

\mathcal{CY}_{-}:\mathcal{W}_{-}\to\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{W}^{op},\mathcal{W}_{-}\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}\mathcal{W}^{op}_{\Delta})[n]

as a map of bimodules,
and is specialized to the following map of complexes

(5.68)

\mathcal{CY}_{-}:\mathcal{W}_{-}(K,L)\to\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{W}^{op},\mathcal{W}_{-}(K,\cdot)\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}Y^{r}_{L})[n]

as the
(0,0)
-th term of the bimodule morphism.
Here the coefficient of the Hochschild cochains is the bimodule given as
the algebraic tensor product of the right module
\mathcal{W}_{-}(K,\cdot)
with the left module
Y^{r}_{L}
, both as modules over
\mathcal{W}^{op}
.

Concretely, the bimodule morphism (
5.67
) has components

(5.69)

\begin{split}\mathcal{CY}_{-}^{l;r,s}:&CW^{*}(K_{1},K_{0};H)\otimes\cdots\otimes CW^{*}(K_{r},K_{r-1};H)\otimes\mathcal{W}_{-}(K_{0},L_{0})\\
&\otimes CW^{*}(L_{s-1},L_{s};H)\otimes\cdots\otimes CW^{*}(L_{0},L_{1};H)\\
&\to\hom_{{\mathbf{k}}}(CW^{*}(L^{\prime}_{1},L^{\prime}_{0};H)\otimes\cdots\otimes CW^{*}(L^{\prime}_{l},L^{\prime}_{l-1};H),\mathcal{W}_{-}(K_{r},L^{\prime}_{l})\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}\mathcal{W}^{op}_{\Delta}(L^{\prime}_{1},L_{s})),\end{split}

which is defined by counting rigid elements in the moduli space

\mathcal{R}_{2;(-1,-1)}^{1,l;r,s}(\mathbf{x}^{+,1},x,\mathbf{x}^{+,2},x_{-,2},\mathbf{x}^{\prime},x_{-,1}),

similar to the construction of the maps (
5.62
).
The formula follows the similar pattern as (
5.63
),
and the sign is the same as (
5.64
).
The
(0,0)
-th term, i.e. the chain map (
5.68
) is the map defined by counting rigid elements in the moduli spaces

\mathcal{R}_{2;(-1,-1)}^{1,l}(x,x_{-,2},\mathbf{x}^{\prime},x_{-,1}).

The main result concerning this map
\mathcal{CY}_{-}
is similar to Proposition
5.18
;
that is, under non-degeneracy hypotheses
\mathcal{CY}_{-}
is also a quasi-isomorphism of
A_{\infty}
-bimodules:

Proposition 5.19
.

Suppose the Liouville manifold
(X,\lambda)
is non-degenerate. Then
the map

\mathcal{CY}_{-}:\mathcal{W}_{-}\to\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{W}^{op},\mathcal{W}_{-}\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}\mathcal{W}^{op}_{\Delta})[n]

is a quasi-isomorphism of bimodules.

The idea of this proof will be to reduce to Proposition
5.18
by a degeneration argument which relates
\mathcal{CY}_{-}
to
\mathcal{CY}
followed by multiplication in the bimodule
\mathcal{W}_{-}
(as operations on the bimodule
\mathcal{W}^{op}_{\Delta}\otimes_{\mathcal{W}^{op}}\mathcal{W}_{-}
that is quasi-isomorphic to
\mathcal{W}_{-}
). In order to state the needed commutative diagram precisely, we will define several chain maps.

First we define the map

(5.70)

\mathcal{CY}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{W}_{-}}:\mathcal{W}^{op}_{\Delta}\otimes_{\mathcal{W}^{op}}\mathcal{W}_{-}\to\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{W}^{op},\mathcal{W}^{op}_{\Delta}\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}\mathcal{W}^{op}_{\Delta})[n]\otimes_{\mathcal{W}}\mathcal{W}_{-}

to be the one-sided tensor product over
\mathcal{W}^{op}
of the Calabi-Yau map
\mathcal{CY}
(
5.66
) with the identity map of the negative bimodule
\mathcal{W}_{-}
.
The domain of (
5.70
)
is quasi-equivalent to the negative bimodule
\mathcal{W}_{-}
by the collapse map (
2.30
).

Under the stated hypotheses
\mathcal{W}
is smooth
[
ganatra
]
*Thm 1.3; hence
by Proposition
2.10
there is a canonical quasi-isomorphism

(5.71)

\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{W}^{op},\mathcal{W}^{op}_{\Delta}\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}\mathcal{W}^{op}_{\Delta})[n]\otimes_{\mathcal{W}}\mathcal{W}_{-}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\to}}\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{W}^{op},(\mathcal{W}^{op}_{\Delta}\otimes_{\mathcal{W}^{op}}\mathcal{W}_{-})\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}\mathcal{W}^{op}_{\Delta})[n].

Now recall that for any bimodule
\mathcal{B}
over
\mathcal{W}^{op}
,
the collapse map (
2.30
)

\mu_{\Delta}:\mathcal{W}^{op}_{\Delta}\otimes_{\mathcal{W}^{op}}\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{B}

is a quasi-isomorphism of bimodules, by Lemma
2.6
.
This can be regarded as a bimodule version of Proposition 2.2 of
[
ganatra
]
.

In cases where
\mathcal{B}
is defined geometrically by disk counts,
such that the cochain space
\mathcal{B}(K,L)
is a Floer complex,
the collapse map is also geoemtrically defined as the bimodule structure maps for
\mathcal{B}
; see
(
2.32
).
In particular, this holds for
\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{W}_{-}
,
and the relevant moduli spaces are

\mathcal{R}^{k+l+2,\varnothing,\mathbf{w}_{-}}(y,\mathbf{x}^{\prime},z,\mathbf{x})

as in (
3.85
),
i.e. the moduli spaces of popsicles defining the bimodule structure maps
\mu^{k,l}_{\mathcal{W}_{-}}
(
5.36
) for
\mathcal{W}_{-}
.

Composing with the collapse map for the negative bimodule
\mathcal{W}_{-}
also induces a quasi-isomorphism on the Hochschild cochain complexes:

(5.72)

\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{W}^{op},(\mathcal{W}^{op}_{\Delta}\otimes_{\mathcal{W}^{op}}\mathcal{W}_{-})\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}\mathcal{W}^{op}_{\Delta})[n]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\to}}\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{W}^{op},\mathcal{W}_{-}\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}\mathcal{W}^{op}_{\Delta})[n].

The composition of (
5.72
) with (
5.71
) and (
5.70
) defines a map from
\mathcal{W}^{op}_{\Delta}\otimes_{\mathcal{W}^{op}}\mathcal{W}_{-}
to
\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{W}^{op},\mathcal{W}_{-}\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}\mathcal{W}^{op}_{\Delta})[n]
.

On the other hand, the composition of the collapse map for
\mathcal{W}_{-}
with
\mathcal{CY}_{-}

\mathcal{W}^{op}_{\Delta}\otimes_{\mathcal{W}^{op}}\mathcal{W}_{-}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mu_{\Delta}}}{{\to}}\mathcal{W}_{-}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{CY}_{-}}}{{\to}}\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{W}^{op},\mathcal{W}_{-}\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}\mathcal{W}^{op}_{\Delta})[n]

gives another morphism from
\mathcal{W}^{op}_{\Delta}\otimes_{\mathcal{W}^{op}}\mathcal{W}_{-}
to
\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{W}^{op},\mathcal{W}_{-}\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}\mathcal{W}^{op}_{\Delta})[n]
.

After specializing to the underlying complexes of the bimodules for each pair of objects
(K,L)
, the above discussion produces two chain maps from
\mathcal{W}^{op}_{\Delta}\otimes_{\mathcal{W}^{op}}\mathcal{W}_{-}(K,L)
to
\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{W}^{op},\mathcal{W}_{-}(K,\cdot)\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}Y^{r}_{L})[n]
. The commutative diagram below,
which makes precise the relationship needed between
\mathcal{CY}
and
\mathcal{CY}_{-}
, asserts that these two chain maps are chain homotopic:

Proposition 5.20
.

The following diagram commutes up to chain homotopy:

(5.73)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{W}^{op}_{\Delta}\otimes_{\mathcal{W}^{op}}\mathcal{W}_{-}(K,L)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{W}^{op},\mathcal{W}^{op}_{\Delta}\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}\mathcal{W}^{op}_{\Delta})[n]\otimes_{\mathcal{W}^{op}}\mathcal{W}_{-}(K,L)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{W}^{op},(\mathcal{W}^{op}_{\Delta}\otimes_{\mathcal{W}^{op}}\mathcal{W}_{-})\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}\mathcal{W}^{op}_{\Delta})(K,L)[n]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{W}_{-}(K,L)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{W}^{op},\mathcal{W}_{-}(K,\cdot)\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}Y^{r}_{L})[n]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{CY}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{W}_{-}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{\Delta}}
(
5.71
)
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mu_{\Delta}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{W}^{op}_{\Delta}})_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{CY}_{-}}

Assuming this Proposition for a moment, we complete the proof of Proposition
5.19
:

Proof of Proposition
5.19
.

It suffices to prove that
\mathcal{CY}_{-}
induces a quasi-isomorphism of chain complexes for each pair of objects
(K,L)
.
Consider the commutative diagram stated in Proposition
5.20
. The two vertical maps are always quasi-isomorphisms by the discussion above Proposition
5.20
, and Proposition
5.18
implies that the top horizontal map is a quasi-isomorphism under the stated hypotheses. It follows that (under the same hypotheses) the remaining bottom horizontal map,

\mathcal{CY}_{-}
is also a quasi-isomorphism.
∎

It remains only to establish the needed commutative diagram:

Proof of Proposition
5.20
.

Consider the moduli space

(5.74)

\mathcal{R}_{2;(-1,-1)}^{1,l;0,s+1}(x_{+},\tilde{\mathbf{x}}^{+,2},x_{-,2},\mathbf{x}^{\prime},x_{-,1})

as special cases of (
3.79
) where
r=0
and
s
is replaced by
s+1
.
Here we think of
z^{+,2}_{s+1}
as an extra distinguished input and set

(5.75)

\tilde{\mathbf{x}}^{+,2}=(\mathbf{x}^{+,2},x_{0}),

where
x_{0}
is a Floer cochain for the diagonal bimodule
\mathcal{W}^{op}_{\Delta}
.
Counting rigid elements in the above moduli space,
with the sign
\dagger_{l,0,s+1}
as in (
5.64
) with
r=0
and
s
replaced by
s+1
,
yields a sequence of multilinear maps between various tensor products of Floer cochain spaces,
which can be collected as the data for a map of the following form:

(5.76)

S:\mathcal{W}^{op}_{\Delta}\otimes_{\mathcal{W}^{op}}\mathcal{W}_{-}(K,L)\to\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{W}^{op},\mathcal{W}_{-}(K,\cdot)\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}Y^{r}_{L})[n-1].

To see that it is the desired chain homotopy,
we study the boundary strata of the relevant moduli spaces by looking at the right hand side of (
3.88
) in Lemma
3.11
(see Figure
4
for the pictures),
with
r=0
and
s
replaced by
s+1
such that the last auxiliary input
z^{+,2}_{s+1}
becomes a distinguished input for the diagonal bimodule
\mathcal{W}^{op}_{\Delta}
.

•

Since
r=0
, the product moduli spaces of the third type in (
3.88
) (middle left in Figure
4
) are all empty,
and the first (top left) and the second (top right) contribute to parts of
d_{\mathrm{CC}^{*}}\circ S
and
S\circ\mu^{0,0}_{\mathcal{W}^{op}_{\Delta}\otimes_{\mathcal{W}^{op}}\mathcal{W}_{-}}
, respectively.

•

The fourth type of boundary strata in (
3.88
) (middle right picture in Figure
4
) contributes to part of
d_{\mathrm{CC}^{*}}\circ S
as well,
by the definition of the differential on the Hochschild cochain complex
\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{W}^{op},\mathcal{W}_{-}(K,\cdot)\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}Y^{r}_{L})[n]
.

•

The fifth type of boundary strata in (
3.88
) (bottom left of Figure
4
with
s_{1}=s+1
, in which case the node is an output for the
top
disk component) again contributes to part of
d_{\mathrm{CC}^{*}}\circ S
,
since the top disk component is of type
\mathcal{R}_{2;(-1,-1)}^{1,l_{1};0,s+1}
defining the chain homotopy
S
,
and the bottom disk component is an ordinary
A_{\infty}
-disk which contributes to the differential on the Hochschild cochain complex.

•

For a broken disk of the six type in (
3.88
) (still bottom left of Figure
4
but with
s_{1}<s+1
, in which case the node is an output for the
bottom
disk component),
the bottom component carries the one extra distinguished input
z^{+,2}_{s+1}
and two distinguished outputs,
and therefore contributes to
\mathcal{CY}
.
Since
r=0
, and the top disk component carries one distinguished negatively weighted input and one output,
it follows that this disk component contributes to the induced map by
\mu_{\Delta}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{W}^{op}_{\Delta}}
on the Hochschild cochain complex.
By the pattern how these two disk components are glued together,
it follows that such a broken disk contributes to
(\mu_{\Delta}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{W}^{op}_{\Delta}})_{*}\circ(\mathcal{CY}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{W}_{-}})
.

•

Finally, for a broken disk of the seventh i.e., last type in (
3.88
) (bottom right of Figure
4
),
the left component contributes to
\mu_{\Delta}
since
r=0
and the distinguished input and output carry negative weights,
and the right component contributes to
\mathcal{CY}_{-}
.

This proves (modulo sign checking by comparing (
4.31
) and (
4.32
) and (
5.64
)) that

(5.77)

(\mu_{\Delta}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{W}^{op}_{\Delta}})_{*}\circ(\mathcal{CY}\otimes\mathrm{id}_{\mathcal{W}_{-}})-\mathcal{CY}_{-}\circ\mu_{\Delta}=d_{\mathrm{CC}^{*}}\circ S+S\circ\mu^{0,0}_{\mathcal{W}^{op}_{\Delta}\otimes_{\mathcal{W}^{op}}\mathcal{W}_{-}},

as desired.
∎

5.10.
A homotopy argument

The key step in proving Theorem
5.6
is to observe that
the broken popsicles needed for the construction of the negative part
\Phi_{-}
of the functor
\Phi

can be compared to other types of broken popsicles described in §
3.7
,
via a gluing-degeneration argument.
This subsection translates that geometric argument into the algebraic outcome,
which establishes the needed relation between
\mathcal{CY}_{-}
and the composition

\mathcal{W}_{-}(K,L)[1]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\iota_{--}}}{{\to}}RC^{*}(K,L)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Phi_{-}^{1}}}{{\to}}\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{W}^{op},(Y^{r}_{K})^{*}\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}Y^{r}_{L})[1].

Recall that Proposition
5.11
asserts that the bimodules
\mathcal{W}_{-}
and
(\mathcal{W}^{op})^{\vee}[-n]
are quasi-equivalent by the map
I
(
5.47
).
The quasi-isomorphism
I
induces, by evaluating on the left at the object
K
, a quasi-isomorphism of modules

I_{K,-}:\mathcal{W}_{-}(K,\cdot)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}(Y^{r}_{K})^{*}
(whose higher-order terms are
I^{l,0}
), and hence for any
L
a quasi-isomorphism of bimodules
I_{K,-}\otimes id_{Y^{r}_{L}}:\mathcal{W}_{-}(K,\cdot)\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}Y^{r}_{L}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}(Y^{r}_{K})^{*}\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}Y^{r}_{L}
. There is therefore an induced quasi-isomorphism on the associated Hochschild cochain complexes

(5.78)

I_{*}:\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{W}^{op},\mathcal{W}_{-}(K,\cdot)\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}Y^{r}_{L})[n]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{W}^{op},(Y^{r}_{K})^{*}\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}Y^{r}_{L}).

By the definition of the pushforward map between Hochschild co-chain complexes associated to a morphism of bimodules we obtain a formula for
I_{*}
:

Lemma 5.21
.

For a Hochschild cochain
\phi\in\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{W}^{op},\mathcal{W}_{-}(K,\cdot)\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}Y^{r}_{L})[n]
, and each testing input
c_{+}\in Y^{r}_{K}(L^{\prime}_{l})
, the output
I_{*}(\phi)\in\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{W}^{op},(Y^{r}_{K})^{*}\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}Y^{r}_{L})
is a Hochschild cochain whose length
l
term is given by the following formula

(5.79)

I_{*}(\phi)^{l}(c^{\prime}_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes c^{\prime}_{l})(c_{+})=\sum_{l_{1}+l_{2}=l}I^{l_{2},0}(c^{\prime}_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes c^{\prime}_{l_{2}}\otimes\phi^{l_{1}}(c^{\prime}_{l_{2}+1}\otimes\cdots\otimes c^{\prime}_{l}))(c_{+}).

∎

With this formula, we can now study the relation between the two maps
I_{*}\circ\mathcal{CY}_{-}
and
\Phi_{-}^{1}\circ\iota_{--}
,
both with source
\mathcal{W}_{-}(K,L)
and target
\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{W}^{op},(Y^{r}_{K})^{*}\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}Y^{r}_{L})
.
We define a chain homotopy

(5.80)

T:\mathcal{W}_{-}(K,L)\to\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{W}^{op},(Y^{r}_{K})^{*}\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}Y^{r}_{L})[-1]

by counting rigid elements in the moduli spaces

\mathcal{R}_{1}^{l+3}(x_{out},\mathbf{x}^{\prime},x_{+},x)

as in (
3.107
) introduced in §
3.8
,
where
x\in\chi(K,L;-H)
,

x^{\prime}_{j}\in\chi(L^{\prime}_{j},L^{\prime}_{j-1};H)
,

x_{+}\in\chi(L^{\prime}_{l},K;H)
,
and
x_{out}\in\chi(L^{\prime}_{0},L;H)
.
The formula for the chain homotopy (
5.80
) takes a form similar to the formulas for
\Phi_{+}
in (
5.4
) with
k=1
,
and but sign for the count is
(-1)^{\Diamond_{l}}
where

(5.81)

\Diamond_{l}=\sum_{i=1}^{l}i\deg(x^{\prime}_{l+1-i})+(l+1)\deg(x_{+})+(l+2)\deg(x)-1.

Lemma 5.22
.

The following diagram commutes up to chain homotopy:

(5.82)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{W}_{-}(K,L)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{W}^{op},(Y^{r}_{K})^{*}\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}Y^{r}_{L})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{W}^{op},\mathcal{W}_{-}(K,\cdot)\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}Y^{r}_{L})[n]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{W}^{op},(Y^{r}_{K})^{*}\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}Y^{r}_{L})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi_{-}^{1}\circ\iota_{--}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{CY}_{-}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I_{*}}

with the chain homotopy given by the map
T
(
5.80
).

Proof.

The proof that
T
gives the desired chain homotopy is similar to the proof of Proposition
5.20
.
Consider the codimension-one boundary strata (
3.109
) of one-dimensional compactified moduli spaces
\bar{\mathcal{R}}_{1}^{l+3}(x_{out},\mathbf{x}^{\prime},x_{+},x)
(
3.108
).
We want to show that each type of boundary stratum contributes to a term in the chain homotopy equation

(5.83)

\Phi^{1}_{-}\circ\iota_{--}-I_{*}\circ\mathcal{CY}_{-}=T\circ\mu^{0,0}_{\mathcal{W}_{-}}+d_{\mathrm{CC}^{*}}\circ T,

where
d_{\mathrm{CC}*}
standards for the differential on the Hochschild cochain complex
\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{W}^{op},(Y^{r}_{K})^{*}\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}Y^{r}_{L})
.
Now we match the types of boundary strata with the algebraic terms that they contribute to when rigid elements are counted.

(i)

Product moduli spaces of the first type on the right hand side of (
3.109
) contribute to the term
T\circ\mu^{0,0}_{\mathcal{W}_{-}}
;

(ii)

By the formula of
d_{\mathrm{CC}^{*}}
on
\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{W}^{op},(Y^{r}_{K})^{*}\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}Y^{r}_{L})
,
where we identify

(Y^{r}_{K})^{*}\otimes_{{\mathbf{k}}}Y^{r}_{L}\cong\hom_{{\mathbf{k}}}^{fin}(Y^{r}_{K},Y^{r}_{L}),

product moduli spaces of the second, the third and the sixth types on the right hand side of (
3.109
) all contribute to the term
d_{\mathrm{CC}*}\circ T
;

(iii)

By Lemma
5.21
we find that the map
I_{*}
,
is defined by counting rigid elements in the moduli spaces

\mathcal{R}^{l,0}_{p}(\mathbf{x};x_{-},x_{+}),l\geq 0

which are special sub-collections of of (
3.53
).
Therefore, the composition
I_{*}\circ\mathcal{CY}_{-}
is defined by counting rigid broken popsicles in the product moduli space

\mathcal{R}^{1,l_{1}}_{2;(-1,-1)}(x,x_{out},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{II},x_{-,1})\times\mathcal{R}^{l_{2},0}_{p}(\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{I};x_{-},x_{+})

appearing in the fourth type of boundary stratum on the right hand side of (
3.109
).

(iv)

Note that the map
\Phi_{-}^{1}\circ\iota_{--}
is defined by counting rigid broken popsicles in the moduli space of broken popsicles

\partial_{1}\bar{\mathcal{R}}^{k+l+2,\mathbf{p}_{+},\mathbf{w}_{+}}(x_{out},\mathbf{x}^{\prime},x_{+},x)

which is a special case of (
5.21
) where
k=1
and
y^{\prime}_{out}=x_{out},y_{in}=x_{+}
,
with certain specific weight constraints.
Comparing (
5.21
) to the moduli space of the fifth type of the right hand side of (
3.109
),
we see that these two product moduli spaces agree.

The last step is to verify the signs are consistent,
which geometrically speaking requires matching the induced boundary orientation on

\partial_{1}\bar{\mathcal{R}}^{k+l+2,\mathbf{p}_{+},\mathbf{w}_{+}}(y^{\prime}_{out},\mathbf{x}^{\prime},y_{in},x_{1})

and the orientation on

\mathcal{R}^{1,l_{1}}_{2;(-1,-1)}(x,x_{-,2},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{II},x_{-,1})\times\mathcal{R}^{l_{2},0}_{p}(\mathbf{x}_{I};x_{-},x_{+})

with the induced boundary orientation on
\partial\bar{\mathcal{R}}_{1}^{l+3}(x_{-,2},\mathbf{x}^{\prime},x_{+},x)
.
In terms of the signs in the relevant algebraic maps, this is a routine computation by comparing (
5.81
) with the signs as (
5.38
) in the formula for the bimodule map
I
as in (
5.52
),
(
5.64
) in the formula for the map
\mathcal{CY}_{-}
,
and the standard signs for counting broken popsicles determined by the boundary orientations on the moduli space of broken popsicles from moduli space of smooth popsicles,
for which the signs are given in (
4.31
) and (
4.32
).
∎

Proof of Theorem
5.6
/Theorem
1.1
(ii).

By Proposition
5.19
, under the stated hypotheses the map
\mathcal{CY}_{-}
is a quasi-isomorphism. Also,
I_{*}
, the induced map (
5.78
) associated to a quasi-isomorphism of bimodules, is a quasi-isomorphism. So the composition is as well, which implies by Lemma
5.22
that
that
\Phi_{-}^{1}\circ\iota_{--}
is a quasi-isomorphism.
Recall that Corollary
5.15
previously showed that
\Phi_{+}^{1}\circ\iota_{++}
was a quasi-isomorphism.

Now, observe that the linear map
\Phi^{1}
is upper-triangular with respect to the
\{-,+\}
decompositions of the Rabinowitz Floer complex (
4.20
) and that of
\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{\infty}
as in (
5.2
).
That is, if one restricts
\Phi^{1}
to the subspace
CW^{*}(K,L;H)
,
which is also a subcomplex with respect to the Floer differential,
the image must be contained in

\mathrm{CC}^{*}(\mathcal{W}^{op},\hom_{{\mathbf{k}}}(Y_{K}^{r},Y_{L}^{r})).

In particular,
\Phi^{1}
is a filtered map with respect to the filtration
\{+\}\subseteq\{+,-\}
on both sides. Since the associated graded parts of
\Phi^{1}
, which are
\Phi_{-}^{1}\circ\iota_{--}
and
\Phi_{+}^{1}\circ\iota_{++}
, are quasi-isomorphisms, a standard argument shows that
\Phi^{1}
is as well.
∎

6.
Applications and examples

6.1.
Situating Rabinowitz Fukaya categories into mirror symmetry

The goal of this subsection is to review Efimov’s geometric descriptions of the formal punctured neighborhood of infinity of coherent sheaf categories,
and show how these along with our main Theorem
1.1
lead to a proof of Corollary
1.2
.

Recall that in algebraic geometry, the category
\mathrm{Perf}(Y)
of perfect complexes on a variety
Y
is proper if and only if
Y
is proper; in particular, the categorical formal punctured neighborhood
\widehat{\mathrm{Perf}(Y)}_{\infty}
is a category whose non-triviality obstructs properness of
Y
. Work of Efimov
[
efimov
]
gives a purely algebro-geometric model of this category if
Y
is a smooth variety over
{\mathbb{C}}
, defined as follows:
by Hironaka’s resolution of singularities, we can find a smooth compactification
\bar{Y}
with
D=\bar{Y}-Y
a normal crossings divisor. Consider first the formal completion of
\bar{Y}
along
D
, denoted
\hat{\bar{Y}}_{D}
.
This has a well-defined (dg) category of perfect complexes
\mathrm{Perf}(\hat{\bar{Y}}_{D})
, defined as follows.
Let
D_{n}
be the
n
-th infinitesimal neighborhood of
D
, defined by taking the
n
-th power of the sheaf of ideals
\mathcal{I}_{D}^{n}\subset\mathcal{O}_{\bar{Y}}
.
There are natural inclusion morphisms

i_{n}:D_{n}\to\bar{Y}

and

i_{n,m}:D_{n}\to D_{m}

for
n<m
.
We define
\mathrm{Perf}(\hat{\bar{Y}}_{D})
to be the homotopy limit

(6.1)

\mathrm{Perf}(\hat{\bar{Y}}_{D}):=\mathrm{holim}_{n}\mathrm{Perf}(D_{n}).

The formal scheme
\hat{\bar{Y}}_{D}
itself can be defined in a similar way,
in which the local pieces are given by taking homotopy colimit

(6.2)

\hat{\bar{Y}}_{D}(U)=\mathrm{hocolim}_{n}\mathrm{Spec}\mathcal{O}_{\bar{Y}}(U)/\mathcal{I}_{D}^{n}(U)

for any Zaraski open
U\subset\bar{Y}
.
The inclusion
D\hookrightarrow\bar{Y}
factors through
i:D\hookrightarrow\hat{\bar{Y}}_{D}
, inducing a pushforward on perfect complexes:

i_{*}:\mathrm{Perf}(D)\to\mathrm{Perf}(\hat{\bar{Y}}_{D}),

which can also be seen as the homotopy limit of functors

i_{n,*}:\mathrm{Perf}(D)\to\mathrm{Perf}(D_{n}).

Next one attempts to “puncture” (obtaining an algebraic version of a “punctured tubular neighborhood”) by removing the image of
D
from
\hat{\bar{Y}}_{D}
. Although the result isn’t well-defined as a formal scheme (it would have no points), Efimov observes that by emulating on the level of perfect complexes the process of puncturing (i.e., taking the quotient of the image), one can associate a well-defined category, called the
perfect complexes on the formal punctured neighborhood of infinity of
Y
, which is in fact only depends on the open locus
Y
:

(6.3)

\mathrm{Perf}(\hat{Y}_{\infty}):=\mathrm{Perf}(\hat{\bar{Y}}_{D})/i_{*}(\mathrm{Perf}(D)).

To clarify, we have not defined the space
\hat{Y}_{\infty}
(which should morally be thought of as the algebro-geometric analogue of a small punctured tubular neighborhood around the locus at infinity in any compactification of
Y
, though this is not well-defined as a formal scheme), simply its associated category directly. There is a natural “restriction-to-infinity” functor

(6.4)

j^{*}:\mathrm{Perf}(Y)\to\mathrm{Perf}(\hat{Y}_{\infty}),

whose essential image, denoted

(6.5)

\mathrm{Perf}_{alg}(\hat{Y}_{\infty}):=j^{*}(\mathrm{Perf}(Y))

is called the category of
algebraizable perfect complexes on the formal punctured neighborhood of infinity
. (As an example, for
Y=\mathbb{A}^{1}_{\mathbb{C}}=\mathrm{Spec}({\mathbb{C}}[t])
, the image of
\mathcal{O}_{Y}
in
\mathrm{Perf}(\hat{Y}_{\infty})
has homological endomorphisms
{\mathbb{C}}((t^{-1}))
).
Note first that if
Y
is proper that
\mathrm{Perf}(\hat{Y}_{\infty})=0
and more generally that any compactly supported sheaf is sent by
j^{*}
to 0.

The first main result of Efimov is that the categorical construction of the formal punctured neighborhood matches with the above algebro-geometric construction.

Theorem 6.1

(Theorem 3.5 of
[
efimov
]
)
.

In the above situation, one has a quasi-equivalence
\mathrm{Perf}_{alg}(\hat{Y}_{\infty})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\widehat{\mathrm{Coh}(Y)}_{\infty}
.

Remark 6.2
.

Efimov
[
efimov
]
in fact describes a larger category
\mathrm{Perf}_{top}(\hat{Y}_{\infty})
of perfect complexes on the formal punctured neighborhood of infinity, with the subcategory
\mathrm{Perf}_{alg}(\hat{Y}_{\infty})
defined as the essential image of the restriction from
\mathrm{Perf}(Y)
. Although
[
efimov
, Thm 3.5]
concerns the larger category
\mathrm{Perf}_{top}(\hat{Y}_{\infty})
, Theorem
6.1
as stated follows immediately from compatibility of loc. cit. with restriction from
\mathrm{Perf}(Y)
.

Now consider a proper but not necessarily smooth variety
Y
over any perfect field
{\mathbf{k}}
.
Recall that the category of singularities
\mathrm{Sing}(Y)
is defined as the (dg) quotient

\mathrm{Sing}(Y)=\mathrm{Coh}(Y)/\mathrm{Perf}(Y).

In this case, Efimov relates it to the category of algebraizable perfect complexes on the formal punctured neighborhood in the following way:

Theorem 6.3

(Theorem 0.4 of
[
efimov
]
)
.

Let
Y
be a proper scheme over a perfect field
{\mathbf{k}}
.
Then there is a natural quasi-equivalence
\mathrm{Sing}(Y)^{op}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\widehat{\mathrm{Coh}(Y)}_{\infty}
.

The strategy of the proof of Theorem
6.3
uses algebraic Koszul duality,
which we have formulated in §
2.4
.
The key observation is that when
Y
is proper,
the inclusion of the subcategory of
dual perfect
complexes, i.e. those complexes
E
such that
{\mathbb{R}}\mathcal{H}om_{\mathcal{O}_{Y}}(E,D_{Y})
where
D_{Y}
is the dualizing complex,
into the coherent sheaf category is a Koszul dualizing subcategory.

Proof of Corollary
1.2
.

Combine Theorems
1.1
,
6.1
and
6.3
.
∎

6.2.
Computations in
T^{*}S^{1}

Here we spell out our main result in a simple example in which the category
\mathcal{RW}(X)
can be computed rather directly:
T^{*}S^{1}
.
This subsection will be mostly expository with computations matching with our main result Theorem
1.1
;
and we will take
{\mathbf{k}}=\mathbb{F}_{2}
to keep the demonstration concise and clean without sign concerns.
Recall that there is a well-known HMS equivalence

\mathcal{W}(T^{*}S^{1};\mathbb{F}_{2})\cong Coh(\mathbb{G}_{m}),

where
\mathbb{G}_{m}=\mathrm{Spec}(\mathbb{F}_{2}[t,t^{-1}])
.
In particular, if
L_{0}
is a cotangent fiber and
H=r^{2}
is radial in the fiber, there is an isomorphism of
A_{\infty}
algebras

CF^{*}(L_{0},L_{0};H)=\mathbb{F}_{2}[t,t^{-1}],

with
\mu_{k}=0
for all
k\neq 2
.

Efimov’s geometric definition of the category
\mathrm{Perf}_{alg}(\hat{Y}_{\infty})
as in (
6.5
) requires the existence of a smooth proper compactification (which holds in general in characteristic zero); once such a compactification exists
[
efimov
]
*Theorem 3.5 (Theorem
6.1
above plus Remark
6.2
) compares the geometric definition (
6.5
) to the categorical definition
\widehat{\mathrm{Coh}(Y)}_{\infty}
. It follows that the resulting geometric definition given is independent of choice of compactification, provided one exists (note a direct geometric proof of this fact given in
[
efimov
]
*Cor. 2.16 requires one to further know about the existence of “common refinements” of any two such choices of compactification, as holds in characteristic zero). Here for the case of
\mathbb{G}_{m}
over
\mathbb{F}_{2}
, we define the category
\mathrm{Perf}_{alg}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}_{m}}_{\infty})

by compactifying
\mathbb{G}_{m}
to
\mathbb{P}^{1}_{\mathbb{F}_{2}}
.
Corollary
1.2
of our main theorem implies an equivalence of
\mathcal{RW}(T^{*}S^{1})
over
\mathbb{F}_{2}
with the image of
\mathrm{Perf}(\mathbb{F}_{2}[t,t^{-1}])
in
