\mathrm{Perf}(\mathbb{F}_{2}((t^{-1}))\times\mathbb{F}_{2}((t)))
.
We will demonstrate this equivalence directly by explicit computation of the endomorphisms of
L_{0}
in
\mathcal{RW}(T^{*}S^{1})
, which corresponds via mirror symmetry to the endormophisms of the image of
\mathcal{O}
in
\mathrm{Perf}_{alg}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}_{m}}_{\infty})
.

The Rabinowitz complex of
L_{0}
is

RC^{*}(L_{0},L_{0})\simeq\prod_{r\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{F}_{2}\langle\phi_{r}\rangle\oplus\bigoplus_{r\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{F}_{2}\langle\tau^{r}\rangle

with both
|t^{r}|=0
and
|\phi_{r}|=0
. We will show that there is a (non-canonical!)
A_{\infty}
-algebra isomorphism.

(6.6)

RC^{*}(L_{0},L_{0})\simeq\mathbb{F}_{2}((t^{-1}))\oplus\mathbb{F}_{2}((t)).

By degree considerations,
\mu_{k}=0
for all
k\neq 2
. It therefore suffices to show that (
6.6
) is a ring isomorphism. Working with a single Lagrangian yields mild non-genericity that is unwieldy in computation, and so we instead work with three isomorphic, disjoint Lagrangians.

Let
L_{0}
,
L_{1}
, and
L_{2}
be pairwise disjoint cotangent fibers. A choice of ordering of the Lagrangians propagates into the chain-level product structure, and so we fix a choice: assume that the isotopies taking
L_{0}
to
L_{1}
and
L_{1}
to
L_{2}
are positive on the end of
T^{*}S^{1}
that contains Reeb chords corresponding to positive powers of
t
. There are chain isomorphisms

RC^{*}(L_{0},L_{0};H)\simeq RC^{*}(L_{i},L_{j};H)\simeq\prod_{r\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{F}_{2}\langle\phi_{r}\rangle\oplus\bigoplus_{r\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{F}_{2}\langle\tau^{r}\rangle

for all
0\leq i,j\leq 2
, such that the following diagram commutes

(6.7)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces RC^{*}(L_{0},L_{0};H)\otimes RC^{*}(L_{0},L_{0};H)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces RC^{*}(L_{0},L_{0};H)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces RC^{*}(L_{1},L_{2};H)\otimes RC^{*}(L_{0},L_{1};H)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces RC^{*}(L_{0},L_{2};H)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{2}}

Define maps

\displaystyle\Phi_{ij}:RC^{*}(L_{i},L_{j};H)

\displaystyle\rightarrow\mathbb{F}_{2}((t^{-1}))\oplus\mathbb{F}_{2}((t))

\displaystyle\tau^{r}

\displaystyle\mapsto(t^{r},t^{r})

\displaystyle\phi_{r}

\displaystyle\mapsto(0,t^{r})

\displaystyle r\geq 0

\displaystyle\phi_{r}

\displaystyle\mapsto(t^{r},0)

\displaystyle r<0

We show that

(6.8)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces RC^{*}(L_{1},L_{2};H)\otimes RC^{*}(L_{0},L_{1};H)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces RC^{*}(L_{0},L_{2};H)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{F}_{2}((t^{-1}))\oplus\mathbb{F}_{2}((t))\otimes\mathbb{F}_{2}((t^{-1}))\oplus\mathbb{F}_{2}((t))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{F}_{2}((t^{-1}))\oplus\mathbb{F}_{2}((t))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi_{21}\otimes\Phi_{10}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi_{02}}

commutes by analyzing algebraic operations on path spaces of
S^{1}
.

Define
\mathcal{P}_{ij}
to be the pathspace on
S^{1}
of paths starting at
L_{i}\cap\{\text{the zero section}\}
and ending at
L_{j}\cap\{\text{the zero section}\}
. We denote by

C_{0}(\mathcal{P}_{ij};\mathbb{F}_{2})=\bigoplus_{r\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{F}_{2}\langle\gamma_{r}\rangle

the degree-zero chains on
\mathcal{P}_{ij}
, with coefficients in
{\mathbb{C}}
. We denote its dual by

\hom(C_{0}(\mathcal{P}_{ij};\mathbb{F}_{2}),\mathbb{F}_{2})=\prod_{r\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{F}_{2}\langle\delta_{r}\rangle,

where

\delta_{r}(\gamma_{s})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}1&s=-r\\
0&\text{else}\end{array}\right.

Cieliebak-Hingston-Oancea explored the loopspace analogue of

\mathcal{C}_{ij}:=\hom(C_{0}(\mathcal{P}_{ij};\mathbb{F}_{2}),\mathbb{F}_{2})\oplus C_{0}(\mathcal{P}_{ij};\mathbb{F}_{2})

in
[
CHOcoproduct
]
and showed that it carries the structure of a ring. Their results extend to path spaces, yielding a map

\mathcal{C}_{jk}\otimes\mathcal{C}_{ij}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}_{ik}.

We describe this product structure on generators. It relies on the Goresky-Hingston coproduct, which is the decomposition

(6.9)

\displaystyle C:C_{0}(\mathcal{P}_{ik};\mathbb{F}_{2})

\displaystyle\rightarrow C_{0}(\mathcal{P}_{jk};\mathbb{F}_{2})\otimes C_{0}(\mathcal{P}_{ij};\mathbb{F}_{2})

(6.10)

\displaystyle\gamma_{r}

\displaystyle\mapsto\sum_{0\leq s\leq r}\gamma_{s}\otimes\gamma_{r-s}

\displaystyle r\geq 0

(6.11)

\displaystyle\gamma_{r}

\displaystyle\mapsto\sum_{0>s>r}\gamma_{s}\otimes\gamma_{r-s}

\displaystyle r<0

•

The pairing

C_{0}(\mathcal{P}_{jk};\mathbb{F}_{2})\otimes C_{0}(\mathcal{P}_{ij};\mathbb{F}_{2})\rightarrow\hom(C_{0}(\mathcal{P}_{ik};\mathbb{F}_{2}),\mathbb{F}_{2})\oplus C_{0}(\mathcal{P}_{ik};\mathbb{F}_{2})

is given by the usual product induced by path concatenation, and it lands in
C_{0}(\mathcal{P}_{ik};\mathbb{F}_{2})
.

•

The pairing

\hom(C_{0}(\mathcal{P}_{jk};\mathbb{F}_{2}),\mathbb{F}_{2})\otimes\hom(C_{0}(\mathcal{P}_{ij};\mathbb{F}_{2}),\mathbb{F}_{2}))\rightarrow\hom(C_{0}(\mathcal{P}_{ik};\mathbb{F}_{2}),\mathbb{F}_{2})\oplus C_{0}(\mathcal{P}_{ik};\mathbb{F}_{2})

lands in
\hom(C_{0}(\mathcal{P}_{ik};\mathbb{F}_{2}),\mathbb{F}_{2})
and is given by the tensor product

(\delta_{r}\cdot\delta_{s})(\gamma_{N})=\delta_{r}\otimes\delta_{s}(C(\gamma_{N}))=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}\delta_{r+s}&r,s\geq 0\text{ or }r,s<0\\
0&\text{else}\end{array}\right.

•

The pairings

C_{0}(\mathcal{P}_{jk};\mathbb{F}_{2})\otimes\hom(C_{0}(\mathcal{P}_{ij};\mathbb{F}_{2}),\mathbb{F}_{2}))\rightarrow\hom(C_{0}(\mathcal{P}_{ik};\mathbb{F}_{2}),\mathbb{F}_{2})\oplus C_{0}(\mathcal{P}_{ik};\mathbb{F}_{2})

and

\hom(C_{0}(\mathcal{P}_{jk};\mathbb{F}_{2}),\mathbb{F}_{2}))\otimes C_{0}(\mathcal{P}_{ij};\mathbb{F}_{2})\rightarrow\hom(C_{0}(\mathcal{P}_{ik};\mathbb{F}_{2}),\mathbb{F}_{2})\oplus C_{0}(\mathcal{P}_{ik};\mathbb{F}_{2})

are given by the formulas

(6.12)

\displaystyle t^{s}\cdot\delta_{r}

\displaystyle=\phi_{r}\circ\mu_{2}(t^{s},-)+\mathrm{id}\otimes\phi_{r}(C(t^{s}))

\displaystyle=\delta_{r+s}+\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}t^{s+r}&0<-r\leq s\text{ or }0\geq-r>s\\
0&\text{else}\end{array}\right.

and

(6.15)

\displaystyle\delta_{r}\cdot t^{s}

\displaystyle=\phi_{r}\circ\mu_{2}(-,t^{s})+\phi_{r}\otimes\mathrm{id}(C(t^{s}))

\displaystyle=\delta_{r+s}+\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}t^{s+r}&0<-r\leq s\text{ or }0\geq-r>s\\
0&\text{else}\end{array}\right.

There are isomorphisms

\displaystyle CW^{*}(L_{i},L_{j};H)

\displaystyle\simeq C_{0}(\mathcal{P}_{ij};\mathbb{F}_{2})

\displaystyle\tau^{r}

\displaystyle\mapsto\gamma_{r}

intertwining
\mu_{2}
with path concatenation, and dual isomorphisms

\displaystyle CW^{*}(L_{i},L_{j};-H)[1]

\displaystyle\simeq\hom(C_{0}(\mathcal{P}_{ij};\mathbb{F}_{2}),\mathbb{F}_{2})

\displaystyle\phi_{r}\mapsto\delta_{r}.

induced by Poincaré duality. As sketched in
[
abouzaid_loop
]
or
[
CHOcoproduct
]
the induced correspondence

RC^{*}(L_{i},L_{j})\simeq\mathcal{C}_{ij}

is a ring isomorphism.

Showing that (
6.8
) commutes is now a straight-forward algebra computation.

•

(6.18)

\displaystyle\Phi_{ik}\left(\tau^{r}\cdot\tau^{s}\right)

\displaystyle=\Phi_{ik}\left(\tau^{r+s}\right)=(t^{r+s},t^{r+s})

(6.19)

\displaystyle=(t^{r},t^{r})\cdot(t^{s},t^{s})=\Phi_{jk}(\tau^{r})\cdot\Phi_{ij}(\tau^{s})

•

\displaystyle\Phi_{ik}(\phi_{r}\cdot\phi_{s})

\displaystyle=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}\Phi_{ik}(\phi_{r+s})&r,s\geq 0\text{ or }r,s<0\\
0&\text{else}\end{array}\right.

\displaystyle=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}(t^{r+s},0)&r,s<0\\
(0,t^{r+s})&r,s\geq 0\\
0&\text{else}\end{array}\right.

\displaystyle=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}(t^{r},0)\cdot(t^{s},0)&r,s<0\\
(0,t^{r})\cdot(0,t^{s})&r,s\geq 0\\
(t^{r},0)\cdot(0,t^{s})&\text{else}\end{array}\right.

(6.28)

\displaystyle=\Phi_{jk}(\phi_{r})\cdot\Phi_{ij}(\phi_{s})

•

\displaystyle\Phi_{ik}(\phi_{r}\cdot\tau^{s})

\displaystyle=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}\Phi_{ik}(\phi_{r+s}+t^{r+s})&0<-r\leq s\text{ or }0\geq-r>s\\
\Phi_{ik}(\phi_{r+s})&\text{else}\end{array}\right.

\displaystyle=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}(t^{r+s},0)+(t^{r+s},t^{r+s})&r\geq 0,s+r<0\\
(0,t^{r+s})+(t^{r+s},t^{r+s})&r<0,s+r\geq 0\\
(0,t^{r+s})&r\geq 0,s+r\geq 0\\
(t^{r+s},0)&r<0,s+r<0\end{array}\right.

\displaystyle=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}(0,t^{r})\cdot(t^{s},t^{s})&r\geq 0\\
(t^{r},0)\cdot(t^{s},t^{s})&r<0\end{array}\right.

(6.39)

\displaystyle=\Phi_{jk}(\phi_{r})\cdot\Phi_{ij}(\tau^{s}).

6.3.
Koszul duality

The compact Fukaya category
\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{F}(X)
of
X
has closed exact Lagrangians of
X
as objects,
and embeds fully faithfully in the wrapped Fukaya category
\mathcal{W}
.
Every closed exact Lagrangian
K
is a (both left and right) proper object in the wrapped Fukaya category,
as both wrapped Floer cohomology groups
HW^{*}(K,L)
and
HW^{*}(L,K)
are finite-dimensional for any other
L
.
Notice that the Rabinowitz Floer homology groups
RW^{*}(K,L)=H^{*}(RC^{*}(K,L))
and
RW^{*}(L,K)
vanish for such a closed exact Lagrangian
K
by Lemma
4.5
. It follows that there is a map from
\mathcal{W}/\mathcal{F}
to
\mathcal{RW}
, and one can ask how well this map characterizes
\mathcal{RW}
in terms of
\mathcal{W}
and
\mathcal{F}
.
Compact Lagrangians are special cases of (right) proper objects, so one can similarly ask:
do right proper objects of
\mathcal{W}
also vanish in
\mathcal{RW}
and if so how far is the
Rabinowitz Fukaya category
\mathcal{RW}
from the quotient category
\mathcal{W}/\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{prop}}
?

Theorem
1.1
provides an algebraic formula for
\mathcal{RW}
in terms of
\mathcal{W}
and thereby reduces the above question to a purely algebraic one which can be studied using Lemma
2.13
(due to Efimov). All together one obtains:

Proposition 6.4
.

If
X
is non-degenerate then the natural functor
j_{\mathcal{RW}}:\mathcal{W}\to\mathcal{RW}
(
4.41
) sends
\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{prop}}
to zero. If
\mathcal{W}
further has a Koszul dualizing subcategory,
then the induced functor
\mathcal{W}/\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{prop}}\to\mathcal{RW}
is a quasi-equivalence.
If this Koszul dualizing subcategory is the compact Fukaya category
\mathcal{F}
, then we have a quasi-equivalence

\mathcal{W}/\mathcal{F}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\mathcal{RW}

Proof.

Non-degeneracy of
X
implies smoothness of
\mathcal{W}
.
The Yoneda functor
\bar{y}:\mathcal{W}\to\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{\infty}
by definition sends
\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{prop}}
to 0, so the first assertion follows from Corollary
5.16
. Under the Koszul duality hypotheses,
Lemma
2.13
implies that the functor induced by
\bar{y}
from
\mathcal{W}/\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{prop}}\to\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{\infty}
is a quasi-equivalence, which by Corollary
5.16
and Theorem
1.1
implies that the functor induced by
j_{\mathcal{RW}}
gives the desired quasi-equivalence
\mathcal{W}/\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{prop}}\cong\mathcal{RW}
.
The final statement follows from the proof of Lemma
2.13
,
which indeed asserts that
\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{\infty}
is quasi-equivalent to
\mathcal{W}/\mathcal{F}
under the stated hypotheses.
∎

6.4.
The open-closed string relationship

For any Liouville manifold
X
, the open-closed map relates the Hochschild homology of
\mathcal{W}(X)
to the symplectic cohomology of
X
:

(6.40)

\mathcal{OC}:\mathrm{HH}_{*-n}(\mathcal{W}(X))\to SH^{*}(X),

which is an isomorphism when
X
is non-degenerate
[
ganatra
]
.
In general, this is a homomorphism of graded vector space,
and a homomorphism of
SH^{*}(X)
-modules,
where we view
\mathrm{HH}_{*-n}(\mathcal{W}(X))
as a module over
SH^{*}(X)
via the closed-open map

(6.41)

\mathcal{CO}:SH^{*}(X)\to\mathrm{HH}^{*}(\mathcal{W}(X))

which is a ring homomorphism.
If
X
is non-degenerate, then we can use the non-compact Calabi-Yau structure to define a product on Hochschild homology
\mathrm{HH}_{*-n}(\mathcal{W}(X))

by identifying it with the Hochschild cohomology
\mathrm{HH}^{*}(\mathcal{W}(X))
,
so that
\mathcal{OC}
becomes an isomorphism of rings.

The purpose of this subsection is to prove Theorem
1.6
,
which will follow from the main result of the paper Theorem
1.1
under two additional folklore results.
The first result is a purely algebraic fact, stated in
[
efimov
]
.
To state it, let us recall some notions in homological algebra.
Let
\mathcal{C}
be an
A_{\infty}
-category, and
M\in\mathrm{ob\ }\mathrm{Perf}\mathcal{C}
.
There is a natural functor

\mathbf{1}_{M}:\mathrm{Perf}{\mathbf{k}}\to\mathrm{Perf}\mathcal{C}

sending
{\mathbf{k}}
to
M
.
The
Chern character
of
M
is defined to be

(6.42)

\mathrm{ch}_{M}=(\mathbf{1}_{M})_{*}(1)\in\mathrm{HH}_{0}(\mathcal{C})\cong\mathrm{HH}_{0}(\mathrm{Perf}\mathcal{C}).

For a perfect
(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})
-bimodule
P
, the Chern character of
P
is defined similarly to be an element

(6.43)

\mathrm{ch}_{P}\in\mathrm{HH}_{*}(\mathcal{C})\otimes\mathrm{HH}_{*}(\mathcal{D}).

of total degree zero defined as follows:
Let
\mathrm{Perf}\mathcal{C}\!-\!\mathcal{D}
denote the category of perfect
(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})
-bimodules.
The Künneth isomorphism for Hochschild homology (along with Morita invariance) says that the Eilenberg-Zilber type map

(6.44)

\mathrm{HH}_{*}(\mathcal{C}^{op})\otimes\mathrm{HH}_{*}(\mathcal{D})\to\mathrm{HH}_{*}(\mathrm{Perf}\mathcal{C}-\mathcal{D}),

induced by the natural functor that takes the
{\mathbf{k}}
-linear tensor product of a left Yoneda
\mathcal{C}
-module with a right Yoneda
\mathcal{D}
-module to get a
(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})
-bimodule, is an isomorphism. The op-invariance property of Hochschild homology further identifies
\mathrm{HH}_{*}(\mathcal{C}^{op})
with
\mathrm{HH}_{*}(\mathcal{C})
. All together these isomorphisms imply that the Chern character of
P
thought of as an object of
\mathrm{Perf}\mathcal{C}-\mathcal{D}
can be viewed as an element of
\mathrm{HH}_{*}(\mathcal{C})\otimes\mathrm{HH}_{*}(\mathcal{D})
as in (
6.43
).
In particular, if
\mathcal{C}
is a smooth
A_{\infty}
-category so that its diagonal bimodule
\mathcal{C}_{\Delta}
is perfect,
we have the Chern character of the diagonal bimodule

\mathrm{ch}_{\mathcal{C}_{\Delta}}\in\mathrm{HH}_{*}(\mathcal{C})\otimes\mathrm{HH}_{*}(\mathcal{C}),

which is also known as the
Shklyarov copairing

[
shklyarov
]
on Hochschild homology

(6.45)

\hat{c}_{Shk}:=\mathrm{ch}_{\mathcal{C}_{\Delta}}:{\mathbf{k}}\to\mathrm{HH}_{*}(\mathcal{C})\otimes\mathrm{HH}_{*}(\mathcal{C}),

This copairing naturally induces a map

(6.46)

c_{Shk}=(\mathrm{ch}_{\mathcal{C}_{\Delta}})_{*}:\mathrm{HH}_{*}(\mathcal{C})^{*}\to\mathrm{HH}_{*}(\mathcal{C}).

By choosing an appropriate chain representative of
\mathrm{ch}_{\mathcal{C}_{\Delta}}
, we see that the above map (
6.46
) is induced by a chain map,
denoted by abuse of notation by

c_{Shk}=(\mathrm{ch}_{\mathcal{C}_{\Delta}})_{*}:\mathrm{CC}_{*}(\mathcal{C})^{*}\to\mathrm{CC}_{*}(\mathcal{C}).

Now Efimov’s result can be stated as the following lemma.

Lemma 6.5

(Folklore, compare
[
efimov
]
p. 37)
.

For any smooth
A_{\infty}
-category
\mathcal{C}
, there is a quasi-isomorphism of chain complexes

(6.47)

\mathrm{CC}_{*}(\mathcal{C},\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{\infty})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\mathrm{Cone}(\mathrm{CC}_{*}(\mathcal{C})^{*}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(c_{Shk})_{*}}}{{\to}}\mathrm{CC}_{*}(\mathcal{C}))

The second result is a statement concerning the relation between the copairing on symplectic cohomology and the
Shklyrarov copairing
on Hochschild homology, which is due to work-in-progress
[
rezchikov
]
.
In the closed-string sector, there is a canonical copairing on symplectic cochain complex similar to the copairing (
4.17
) on wrapped Floer cochain complex,

(6.48)

\hat{c}_{SH}:{\mathbf{k}}\to SC^{*}(X)\otimes SC^{2n-*}(X),

inducing a copairing on symplectic cohomology

(6.49)

\hat{c}_{SH}:{\mathbf{k}}\to SH^{*}(X)\otimes SH^{2n-*}(X).

By analogy with the construction in §
4.2
, this is precisely the copairing inducing the continuation map from symplectic chains to cochains

(6.50)

c_{SH}:SC_{*}(X)\to SC^{*}(X),

where by Poincaré duality
SC_{*}(X)
is quasi-isomorphic to
SC^{2n-*}(X)^{*}
,
which can be seen by a closed-string analogue of the isomorphism
\bar{I}
(
4.7
) for wrapped Floer chain and cochain complexes.
In particular, we have an quasi-isomorphism of chain complexes

(6.51)

\mathrm{Cone}(SC_{*}(X)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle c_{SH}}}{{\to}}SC^{*}(X))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}RFC^{*}(X)

whose homology is the Rabinowitz Floer homology
RFH^{*}(X)
; this quasi-isomorphism is the main result of
[
CFO
]
as mentioned in the introduction.

Rezschikov’s result asserts the following relation between the two copairings
\hat{c}_{SH}
(
6.49
) and
\hat{c}_{Shk}
(
6.45
).

Lemma 6.6

(In progress,
[
rezchikov
]
)
.

Suppose the open-closed map
\mathcal{OC}
(
6.40
) is an isomorphism. Then it transfers
\hat{c}_{SH}
to
\hat{c}_{Shk}
.

Proof of Theorem
1.6
.

By Theorem
1.1
, we have an isomorphism

(6.52)

\mathrm{HH}_{*-n}(\mathcal{W},\mathcal{RW})\cong\mathrm{HH}_{*-n}(\mathcal{W},\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{\infty}),

which by Lemma
6.5
is further isomorphic to

(6.53)

H^{*}(\mathrm{Cone}(\mathrm{CC}_{*}(\mathcal{W})^{*}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(c_{Shk})_{*}}}{{\to}}\mathrm{CC}_{*}(\mathcal{W}))).

Note that
\mathrm{HH}_{*-n}(\mathcal{W},\mathcal{W})^{*}
is isomorphic to
SH^{*}(X)^{*}[-2n]=SH^{2n-*}(X)^{*}
by applying the linear dual of the open-closed isomorphism
\mathcal{OC}
(
6.40
).
Under the open-closed isomorphism
\mathcal{OC}
, and by Lemma
6.6
where we identify
SH^{2n-*}(X)^{*}
with
SH_{*}(X)
by Poincaré duality,
the above group (
6.53
) is isomorphic to

H^{*}(\mathrm{Cone}(SC_{*}(X)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle c_{SH}}}{{\to}}SC^{*}(X)),

which is isomorphic to Rabinowitz Floer homology
RFH^{*}(X)
by the quasi-isomorphism (
6.51
), as desired.
∎

This theorem allows us to give a superficially different proof of the following interesting “non-proper or zero” property of wrapped Fukaya categories recently established in
[
Gnonproper
]
, which is related to the conjectural ‘zero or infinite’ property of symplectic cohomology for Liouville manifolds:

Corollary 6.7

(compare
[
Gnonproper
]
Main Theorem part (1))
.

For any non-degenerate Liouville manifold
(X,\lambda)
, its wrapped Fukaya category
\mathcal{W}:=\mathcal{W}(X)
is either zero or non-proper.
In particular if
\mathcal{W}(X)\neq 0
then given any finite collection
\{L_{i}\}
of Lagrangians split-generating the wrapped Fukaya category,
there exist
i,j
such that the wrapped Floer cohomology
HW^{*}(L_{i},L_{j})
is infinite-dimensional.

Proof.

If
\mathcal{W}
is proper, then
\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{\infty}
is zero so Theorem
1.1
implies
\mathcal{RW}=0
, hence
\mathrm{HH}_{*}(\mathcal{W},\mathcal{RW})=0
.
By Theorem
1.6
, this implies
RFH^{*}(X)=0
.
By
[
ritterTQFT
]
*Thm 13.1,
SH^{*}(X)=0
, which implies (by the usual argument that
HW^{*}(L,L)
is a unital module over
SH^{*}(X)
for each
L
) that
\mathcal{W}
must be zero.
∎
